Compare table cp-fiscal-monetary · 4C.1 4D.1 4B.1 · 6 marks · level 2

Compare fiscal policy and monetary policy.

Fiscal policyMonetary policy
Who decides(given) The Federal Government (the Treasurer and Cabinet), through the budgetThe RBA Monetary Policy Board
Main instrumentsGovernment spending, taxation and transfer paymentsThe cash rate target, forward guidance and additional policy measures
How often it can changeMainly once a year in the budget (plus mid-year updates)Eight meetings a year, so it can change quickly
Implementation lagLong: new spending and tax changes need decisions, laws and planningShort: the cash rate changes the day after a meeting
How it changes ADDirectly through G, and through C and I via tax and transfersIndirectly, through interest rates, credit, asset prices and the exchange rate
Can it target groups or regions?Yes, spending and tax can be aimed at particular groupsNo, one cash rate applies to the whole economy

TariffQuotaSubsidy
Price paid by consumersRises to the world price plus the tariffRises until demand minus supply equals the quota(given) Unchanged at the world price
Domestic productionRisesRisesRises
ImportsFallFall to the quotaFall
Who receives the money from the higher price?The government, as tariff revenueThe importers keep the extra revenue from the higher priceThere is no higher price; producers receive the subsidy
Effect on the budgetRevenue for the governmentNo direct effectA cost to the government
Deadweight lossProduction and consumption lossesProduction and consumption lossesProduction loss only

Headline inflationUnderlying inflation

How it is calculatedPercentage change in the CPI over the yearTrimmed mean or weighted median of the CPI price changes
Includes volatile items such as fuel and fruit?YesNo, extreme changes are trimmed out

Draw a diagram dr-adas-01 · 4D.3 3A.1 · 4 marks · level 1

The Reserve Bank cuts the cash rate by half a percentage point, and households and firms borrow and spend more. Draw an AD–AS diagram to show the effect on the economy.

Lower interest rates raise consumption and investment, which are components of aggregate demand, so AD shifts right from AD1 to AD2. Real GDP rises from Y1 to Y2 and the price level rises from P1 to P2.

Draw a diagram dr-adas-02 · 4C.2 4B.1 · 4 marks · level 1

The Federal Government cuts its spending and raises taxes to return the budget to surplus. Draw an AD–AS diagram to show the effect on the economy.

Government spending is a component of AD and higher taxes reduce disposable income and consumption, so AD shifts left from AD1 to AD2. Real GDP falls from Y1 to Y2 and the price level falls from P1 to P2 (in practice, inflation slows). This is contractionary fiscal policy.

Draw a diagram dr-adas-03 · 3C.3 1A.8 · 4 marks · level 2

Conflict in the Middle East closes a major shipping route and the world price of oil rises sharply. Draw an AD–AS diagram to show the effect on the Australian economy.

Higher fuel and energy costs raise production costs across the economy, so aggregate supply shifts left from AS1 to AS2. The price level rises from P1 to P2 while real GDP falls from Y1 to Y2: cost-push (and imported) inflation together with slower growth, or stagflation.

Draw a diagram dr-adas-04 · 4E.1 3A.6 · 4 marks · level 1

Microeconomic reform raises labour productivity and lowers firms' unit costs. Draw an AD–AS diagram to show the effect.

Lower unit costs mean firms will supply more at each price level, so AS shifts right from AS1 to AS2. Real GDP rises from Y1 to Y2 and the price level falls from P1 to P2: growth without inflationary pressure.

Draw a diagram dr-adas-05 · 1A.8 3A.5 · 4 marks · level 1

A recession in China cuts its demand for Australian iron ore and coal. Draw an AD–AS diagram to show the effect on the Australian economy.

Exports are a component of AD, so lower export demand shifts AD left from AD1 to AD2. Real GDP falls from Y1 to Y2 and the price level falls from P1 to P2. The international business cycle is transmitted to Australia through trade.

Draw a diagram dr-adas-06 · 3A.1 · 4 marks · level 1

Consumer confidence jumps after a run of good economic news, and households spend more of their income. Draw an AD–AS diagram to show the effect.

Consumption is the largest component of AD, so AD shifts right from AD1 to AD2. Real GDP rises from Y1 to Y2 and the price level rises from P1 to P2.

Draw a diagram dr-adas-07 · 3C.3 · 4 marks · level 2

Wages rise much faster than labour productivity across the economy. On the diagram, show the effect on the price level and real GDP.

Unit labour costs rise, so firms supply less at each price level and AS shifts left from AS1 to AS2. The price level rises from P1 to P2 and real GDP falls from Y1 to Y2: cost-push inflation.

Draw a diagram dr-adas-08 · 2C.6 3A.1 · 4 marks · level 2

The Australian dollar depreciates by 15 per cent against the currencies of Australia's trading partners. Draw an AD–AS diagram to show the main effect on the Australian economy.

Australian exports become cheaper overseas and imports dearer, so net exports rise and AD shifts right from AD1 to AD2. Real GDP rises from Y1 to Y2 and the price level rises from P1 to P2. A strong answer also notes that dearer imported inputs push AS left a little (imported inflation), which adds to the rise in prices.

Draw a diagram dr-adas-09 · 4D.3 3C.3 · 4 marks · level 1

The Reserve Bank raises the cash rate to bring inflation back to its 2–3 per cent target. On the diagram, show the effect of higher interest rates.

Higher interest rates reduce consumption and investment, so AD shifts left from AD1 to AD2. The price level falls from P1 to P2 (inflation slows) and real GDP falls from Y1 to Y2, which is the trade-off of tighter monetary policy.

Draw a diagram dr-adas-10 · 3C.3 3A.6 · 4 marks · level 1

The world price of oil falls by a third, cutting transport and energy costs. On the diagram, show the effect on the Australian economy.

Lower input costs let firms supply more at each price level, so AS shifts right from AS1 to AS2. The price level falls from P1 to P2 and real GDP rises from Y1 to Y2.

Draw a diagram dr-lras-01 · 3A.6 4E.1 · 4 marks · level 2

Investment in education, training and new technology increases the economy's productive capacity. Draw an AD–LRAS diagram to show the effect.

Higher productivity and more skilled labour raise potential output, so LRAS shifts right from LRAS1 to LRAS2. Real GDP rises from Y1 to Y2 and the price level falls from P1 to P2, so the economy can grow faster without adding to inflation.

Draw a diagram dr-lras-02 · 3C.3 3A.5 · 4 marks · level 3

A housing boom and strong government spending push aggregate demand beyond the economy's productive capacity. Draw a diagram with LRAS to show why this causes inflation.

AD shifts right from AD1 to AD2 along SRAS. Because the economy is already near capacity (LRAS), most of the extra demand raises the price level from P1 to P2, with only a small rise in real GDP from Y1 to Y2: demand-pull inflation. Name the upward-sloping curve SRAS because LRAS is also on the diagram.

Draw a diagram dr-fx-01 · 2C.2 2C.3 · 4 marks · level 1

The world price of iron ore rises by 30 per cent. Draw a diagram of the market for Australian dollars to show the effect on the exchange rate.

Overseas buyers need more Australian dollars to pay for the same exports, so the demand for A$ shifts right from D1 to D2. The exchange rate rises from e1 to e2 (an appreciation) and the quantity of A$ traded rises from Q1 to Q2.

Draw a diagram dr-fx-02 · 2C.2 4D.3 · 4 marks · level 2

Central banks overseas raise interest rates while the Reserve Bank keeps the cash rate unchanged. Draw a diagram of the market for Australian dollars to show the effect.

The interest rate differential narrows, so Australian assets offer a smaller relative return and foreign investors buy fewer A$. Demand for A$ shifts left from D1 to D2, the exchange rate falls from e1 to e2 (a depreciation) and the quantity traded falls from Q1 to Q2.

Draw a diagram dr-fx-03 · 2C.2 2A.2 · 4 marks · level 1

Australian superannuation funds move more of their money into overseas shares. Draw a diagram of the market for Australian dollars to show the effect.

The funds sell Australian dollars to buy foreign currency (a capital outflow), so the supply of A$ shifts right from S1 to S2. The exchange rate falls from e1 to e2 (a depreciation) and the quantity traded rises from Q1 to Q2.

Draw a diagram dr-fx-04 · 2C.2 2A.1 · 4 marks · level 1

Far fewer international students enrol at Australian universities. Draw a diagram of the market for Australian dollars to show the effect.

Education for overseas students is an export of services, paid for in Australian dollars. Fewer students means less demand for A$, so demand shifts left from D1 to D2 and the exchange rate falls from e1 to e2 (a depreciation).

Draw a diagram dr-fx-05 · 2C.2 · 4 marks · level 1

Australian households cut their spending on imported goods and overseas holidays. Draw a diagram of the market for Australian dollars to show the effect.

Fewer Australian dollars are sold to buy foreign currency, so the supply of A$ shifts left from S1 to S2. The exchange rate rises from e1 to e2 (an appreciation) and the quantity traded falls from Q1 to Q2.

Draw a diagram dr-fx-06 · 4D.3 2C.2 · 4 marks · level 2

The Reserve Bank raises the cash rate while rates overseas stay the same, and foreign investors buy more Australian government bonds. On the diagram, show the effect on the exchange rate.

A wider interest rate differential attracts capital inflow, so demand for A$ shifts right from D1 to D2. The exchange rate rises from e1 to e2 (an appreciation). This is the exchange rate channel of monetary policy: the appreciation also slows inflation by making imports cheaper.

Draw a diagram dr-fx-07 · 2C.2 2C.3 · 4 marks · level 2

Speculators expect the Australian dollar to fall and sell it now. On the diagram, show the effect.

Selling ahead of an expected fall increases the supply of A$, so supply shifts right from S1 to S2 and the exchange rate falls from e1 to e2. The expectation is self-fulfilling: the dollar depreciates.

Draw a diagram dr-fx-08 · 2C.2 1A.8 · 4 marks · level 1

A global downturn reduces world demand for Australia's commodity exports. On the diagram, show the effect on the Australian dollar.

Lower export volumes and prices mean overseas buyers need fewer A$, so demand shifts left from D1 to D2 and the exchange rate falls from e1 to e2. The depreciation cushions the downturn by making Australian exports cheaper.

Draw a diagram dr-lab-01 · 3B.3 · 4 marks · level 1

The economy falls into recession and firms cut production. Draw a diagram of the labour market to show the effect.

The demand for labour is derived from the demand for goods and services, so it shifts left from D1 to D2. Employment falls from Q1 to Q2 and the wage rate falls from W1 to W2 (in practice wages are sticky, so more of the adjustment shows up as cyclical unemployment).

Draw a diagram dr-lab-02 · 3B.1 1A.7 · 4 marks · level 1

Net overseas migration of working-age people rises. Draw a diagram of the labour market to show the effect.

More people are available to work at each wage, so the supply of labour shifts right from S1 to S2. Employment rises from Q1 to Q2 and the wage rate falls from W1 to W2 (or grows more slowly).

Draw a diagram dr-lab-03 · 3B.3 3A.5 · 4 marks · level 1

Strong economic growth lifts the demand for goods and services. On the diagram, show the effect on the labour market.

Firms need more workers to produce more output, so the demand for labour shifts right from D1 to D2. Employment rises from Q1 to Q2 and the wage rate rises from W1 to W2.

Draw a diagram dr-lab-04 · 3B.1 · 4 marks · level 2

A large share of the workforce reaches retirement age and leaves the labour force. Draw a diagram of the labour market to show the effect.

The supply of labour shifts left from S1 to S2 as the participation rate falls. The wage rate rises from W1 to W2 and employment falls from Q1 to Q2.

Draw a diagram dr-mw-01 · 4F.2 3B.3 · 4 marks · level 2

The minimum wage for unskilled workers is set above the wage the market would set. Draw a diagram of the market for unskilled labour to show the effect.

At the minimum wage (Wmin) the quantity of labour supplied (Qs) is greater than the quantity demanded (Qd), so the gap between Qd and Qs is unemployment. Employment falls from Qe to Qd. In practice the effect depends on how far the minimum wage sits above the market wage and on productivity.

Draw a diagram dr-sd-01 · 2D.4 1C.2 · 4 marks · level 2

China places anti-dumping duties of up to 218 per cent on Australian wine. Draw a diagram of the market for Australian wine sold in China to show the effect.

The tariff raises the price Chinese buyers pay, so demand for Australian wine in China shifts left from D1 to D2. The price falls from P1 to P2 and the quantity sold falls from Q1 to Q2. Australian wine exports to China fell from about $1.3 billion to under $1 million by 2023 before the duties were removed in March 2024.

Draw a diagram dr-sd-02 · 4E.2 · 4 marks · level 2

A state government speeds up planning approvals so more new homes can be built. Draw a diagram of the market for new housing to show the effect.

Faster approvals lower costs and delays for builders, so supply shifts right from S1 to S2. The price falls from P1 to P2 and the quantity of new homes rises from Q1 to Q2: a microeconomic policy acting on one product market.

Draw a diagram dr-ceil-01 · 4E.2 · 4 marks · level 2

A state government caps rents below the market rent. Draw a diagram of the rental housing market to show the effect.

At the maximum rent (Pmax) the quantity demanded (Qd) is greater than the quantity supplied (Qs), so there is a shortage between Qs and Qd. Fewer homes are offered for rent than at the market rent (Qe), even though renters who find a home pay less.

Draw a diagram dr-tar-01 · 1C.2 2D.3 · 4 marks · level 2

The government imposes a tariff on imported steel. Draw a diagram of the domestic steel market to show the effects of the tariff.

The domestic price rises from the world price Pw to Pw+t. Domestic production rises from Q1 to Q2 and consumption falls from Q4 to Q3, so imports fall from Q1–Q4 to Q2–Q3. The government collects revenue (the shaded rectangle), producers gain surplus, consumers lose surplus, and the two shaded triangles are deadweight losses.

Draw a diagram dr-quo-01 · 1C.2 · 4 marks · level 3

The government limits imports of cheese with a quota. Draw a diagram of the domestic cheese market to show the effects of the quota.

With imports limited to the quota, the domestic price rises from Pw to P1 until the gap between domestic demand (Q3) and domestic supply (Q2) equals the quota. Domestic production rises from Q1 to Q2 and consumption falls from Q4 to Q3. Unlike a tariff, the government collects no revenue: the importers keep the extra revenue from the higher price (the shaded rectangle). The two triangles are deadweight losses.

Draw a diagram dr-sub-01 · 1C.2 2D.1 · 4 marks · level 3

The government pays local makers of solar panels a subsidy for each panel produced. Draw a diagram of the domestic solar panel market to show the effects.

The subsidy lowers producers' costs, so domestic supply shifts right from S1 to S2. Consumers still pay the world price Pw, so consumption (Q3) does not change. Domestic production rises from Q1 to Q2 and imports fall. Producers receive Pw+s; the shaded areas show the producer surplus gain and the deadweight loss, and the government pays the subsidy on every unit produced (Q2).

Draw a diagram dr-ext-01 · 3F.2 · 4 marks · level 2

Coal-fired power stations emit carbon dioxide and no policy makes them pay for the damage. Draw a diagram to show why the market produces too much electricity.

Producers base supply on their private costs (MPC), but the social cost (MSC) is higher by the external cost. The market produces Q_m where demand meets MPC, more than the socially efficient Qs where demand meets MSC, and the price is too low. The shaded triangle is the deadweight loss: market failure.

Draw a diagram dr-ext-02 · 4G.2 3F.2 · 4 marks · level 3

The government puts a price on carbon equal to the external cost of each unit of electricity. On the diagram, show the effect.

The carbon price makes producers pay the full social cost, so supply shifts up from MPC to MSC. Output falls from Q_m to the socially efficient Qs and the price rises from P_m to Ps: the externality is internalised and the deadweight loss disappears.

Draw a diagram dr-lor-01 · 3E.3 · 4 marks · level 2

The government makes income tax more progressive and raises income support payments. Draw a Lorenz curve diagram to show the effect on the distribution of income.

Higher earners pay a larger share of their income in tax and low-income households receive more in transfers, so disposable income is spread more evenly. The Lorenz curve moves towards the line of equality, from L1 to L2, and the Gini coefficient falls.

Draw a diagram dr-lor-02 · 3E.4 3E.5 · 4 marks · level 2

Share and property prices boom while wages grow slowly. Draw a Lorenz curve diagram to show the effect on the distribution of wealth.

Assets are owned mostly by the highest quintiles, so their wealth grows fastest. The Lorenz curve moves further from the line of equality, from L1 to L2, and the Gini coefficient for wealth rises.

Draw a diagram dr-lor-03 · 3B.6 3E.6 · 4 marks · level 1

Unemployment rises sharply in a recession. On the diagram, show the likely effect on the distribution of income.

Job losses fall mainly on lower-income households, whose market income falls, so income becomes less equal. The Lorenz curve moves away from the line of equality, from L1 to L2, and the Gini coefficient rises (transfer payments such as JobSeeker offset part of the change).

Draw a diagram dr-phil-01 · 4A.7 3B.4 · 4 marks · level 2

Expansionary policy pushes unemployment below the NAIRU. Draw a Phillips curve diagram to show the short-run effect.

The economy moves up along the short-run Phillips curve from A to B: unemployment falls from U1 to U2 and inflation rises from π1 to π2. This shows the conflict between the objectives of full employment and price stability.

Draw a diagram dr-phil-02 · 4A.7 3C.3 · 4 marks · level 3

After several years of high inflation, workers and firms expect prices to keep rising. Draw a Phillips curve diagram to show the effect of higher inflationary expectations.

Higher expectations are built into wage claims and price setting, so the Phillips curve shifts right (up) from PC1 to PC2. Each unemployment rate now comes with higher inflation; the economy may move from A to B with both inflation and unemployment higher. This is why central banks act early to keep expectations anchored.

Draw a diagram dr-ppf-01 · 3A.6 3A.5 · 4 marks · level 1

New technology and investment in capital raise the economy's productive capacity. Draw a production possibility frontier to show the effect.

The economy can now produce more of both capital goods and consumer goods, so the PPF shifts outward from PPF1 to PPF2: long-run economic growth.

Draw a diagram dr-ppf-02 · 3B.6 3A.7 · 4 marks · level 1

A recession leaves many workers and machines idle. Draw a production possibility frontier to show where the economy is producing.

The economy produces at a point inside the frontier, such as A, because resources are unemployed. Output could rise without giving up anything else: the cost of unemployment is the output lost.

Extended response er-1-glob-growth · 1A.3 1A.4 1D.6 1D.5 · 20 marks · level 3

Evaluate the impact of globalisation on economic growth and development in emerging and developing economies.

  • Defines globalisation, growth and development, and treats emerging and developing economies separately
  • Trade and foreign investment: export-led growth, technology transfer and jobs, with evidence (e.g. China, India, Vietnam)
  • Development: poverty reduction and HDI gains, but uneven distribution and rising inequality within countries
  • Costs: volatile capital flows, commodity dependence, environmental damage, exposure to the international business cycle
  • A sustained judgement: large benefits for emerging economies; smaller and less certain gains for many developing economies

Plan

  • Judgement: globalisation has driven growth in emerging economies but its benefits for developing economies are uneven
  • Trade and TNCs as engines of growth (evidence and a diagram such as a PPF shift)
  • Development outcomes: poverty, HDI, inequality within countries
  • Risks: financial flows, commodity prices, environment
  • Conclusion weighing growth against development

Extended response er-1-protection · 1C.1 1C.2 2D.4 · 20 marks · level 3

Analyse the effects of protectionist policies on the global economy.

  • Explains methods of protection with a tariff diagram (price, production, consumption, imports, revenue, deadweight losses)
  • Effects on the protecting economy: higher prices, inefficient resource allocation, retaliation
  • Global effects: lower world trade and growth, trade diversion, retaliation and trade wars (e.g. US tariffs from 2025)
  • Effects on developing economies and on third countries such as Australia
  • Links throughout to efficiency and living standards

Plan

  • Thesis: protection reduces global efficiency and growth, even if some producers gain
  • Tariff diagram and its effects on one economy
  • Retaliation, trade wars and reduced world trade
  • Effects on developing economies and third countries
  • Conclusion

Extended response er-1-china · 1E.1 1D.6 1A.5 · 20 marks · level 3

Evaluate the influence of globalisation on the economy of a country other than Australia.

  • Names the economy and outlines how it is integrated into the global economy (trade, investment, finance, labour, technology)
  • Effects on growth with data, e.g. China's export-led growth since 1978 and WTO entry in 2001
  • Effects on development and distribution: poverty reduction, rising inequality, urban and rural gaps
  • Costs: environmental degradation, exposure to trade tensions and the international business cycle
  • Government policies that shaped the outcome, and a clear overall judgement

Plan

  • Judgement on the net effect of globalisation on the economy
  • Trade and foreign investment as drivers of growth
  • Development and inequality
  • Environmental and external risks
  • Role of government policy; conclusion

Extended response er-2-cad · 2B.4 3D.3 2A.2 · 20 marks · level 3

Spot the error er-4D3-mp · 4D.3 4D.1 · 4 marks · level 1

This paragraph answers: "Explain how a rise in the cash rate affects the economy."

When inflation is above the target band, the RBA raises the cash rate to stimulate slow the economy. Banks pass the increase on, so borrowers face higher interest rates on loans. Higher interest rates encourage discourage borrowing for houses, cars and business investment, so consumption and investment fall. Aggregate demand shifts right left, which eases demand inflation. The higher return on Australian assets also attracts foreign capital, so the Australian dollar depreciates appreciates, making imports cheaper and further reducing inflation.

Spot the error er-1C2-tariff · 1C.2 · 4 marks · level 2

This paragraph answers: "Explain the effects of a tariff on an imported good."

A tariff is a tax on imports, so the domestic price rises from the world price to the world price plus the tariff. Domestic producers respond to the higher price by producing less more, while consumers buy less. Imports therefore rise fall. The government's revenue equals the tariff per unit multiplied by the quantity of imports before after the tariff. Consumers gain lose consumer surplus, and part of that loss is a deadweight loss to society.

Spot the error er-3A3-mult · 3A.3 3A.2 · 3 marks · level 2

This paragraph answers: "Explain the multiplier effect of a rise in government spending."

An increase in government spending is an injection into the circular flow. The people who receive it spend part of it, so it becomes someone else's income, and so on. The simple multiplier is k = 1 ÷ MPC 1 ÷ (1 − MPC). If the marginal propensity to consume is 0.8, the multiplier is 1.25 5, so a $10 billion increase in spending raises national income by $50 billion. If households decide to save more of each extra dollar, the multiplier becomes larger smaller, because more income leaks out of the circular flow in each round.

Spot the error er-3B1-measure · 3B.1 · 3 marks · level 1

This paragraph answers: "Outline how unemployment is measured in Australia."

The ABS Labour Force Survey counts a person as unemployed if they had no paid work in the survey week, were actively looking for work and were available to start. The unemployment rate is the number of unemployed people divided by the working age population labour force, multiplied by 100. The labour force is the employed plus the underemployed unemployed. A discouraged job seeker who has stopped looking for work is counted as unemployed not counted in the labour force, which is why this group is called hidden unemployment.

Spot the error er-2B1-ca · 2B.1 2B.3 · 3 marks · level 2

This paragraph answers: "Explain the relationship between the current account and the capital and financial account."

The current account records trade in goods and services and income flows. Interest paid on Australia's foreign debt is recorded in the financial account primary income section of the current account. When Australia runs a current account deficit, it must be financed by a capital and financial account deficit surplus, as foreigners lend to Australia or invest here. In theory the two accounts sum to one zero, although in practice net errors and omissions are needed to make them balance.

Spot the error er-2C2-factors · 2C.2 2C.3 · 3 marks · level 1

This paragraph answers: "Explain two factors that could cause the Australian dollar to appreciate."

First, if the world price of iron ore and coal rises, overseas buyers need more Australian dollars to pay for our exports. Demand for the Australian dollar rises and the dollar depreciates appreciates. Second, if Australian interest rates rise relative to interest rates overseas, Australian assets offer a higher return. Foreign investors buy Australian dollars to invest here, which reduces increases the demand for the dollar and pushes its value up. A higher Australian dollar means one Australian dollar buys less more foreign currency.

Spot the error er-4C2-fiscal · 4C.2 4C.3 · 3 marks · level 2

This paragraph answers: "Explain how the budget can stabilise the economy."

A budget deficit is contractionary expansionary because the government injects more into the circular flow through spending than it withdraws through tax. In a recession, automatic stabilisers work without any new decisions: tax revenue rises falls and payments such as JobSeeker rise, which supports aggregate demand. To finance a deficit, the government sells Commonwealth Government Securities. If this borrowing competes with private borrowers, it can lower raise interest rates and reduce private investment, which is called crowding out.

Spot the error er-3C1-measure · 3C.1 · 3 marks · level 1

This paragraph answers: "Distinguish between headline and underlying inflation."

Headline inflation is the percentage change in the Consumer Price Index over a year, so it includes every item in the basket. Underlying inflation includes removes the most volatile price changes, such as those for fuel and fruit, so it shows the trend in prices. The RBA's preferred measure of underlying inflation is the GDP deflator trimmed mean. The RBA aims to keep inflation between 1 and 2 2 and 3 per cent on average over time.

Spot the error er-3E1-gini · 3E.1 3E.3 · 3 marks · level 2

This paragraph answers: "Explain how the Lorenz curve and the Gini coefficient measure inequality."

The Lorenz curve plots the cumulative percentage of households against the cumulative percentage of income they receive. The further the curve bows away from the line of equality, the more equal less equal the distribution. The Gini coefficient is the area between the line of equality and the Lorenz curve divided by the total area under the line of equality. A Gini coefficient of 1 means perfect equality inequality. Progressive taxes and transfer payments move the Lorenz curve away from towards the line of equality.

Spot the error er-3F2-ext · 3F.2 4G.2 · 4 marks · level 2

This paragraph answers: "Explain why a negative externality is an example of market failure."

When a power station pollutes the air, it does not pay for the damage it causes to others. Its private costs are therefore higher lower than the social cost of its output, so the marginal social cost curve lies below above the private supply curve. The market produces less more than the socially efficient quantity, at a price that is too low. A subsidy to tax on the polluter, equal to the external cost, would make it pay the full social cost.

Spot the error er-1B1-ca · 1B.1 · 3 marks · level 3

This paragraph answers: "Explain the basis of free trade."

Free trade is based on comparative advantage. A country should specialise in the good it can produce at the highest lowest opportunity cost, and trade for other goods. Even if one country has an absolute advantage in producing both goods, trade cannot can still benefit both countries, as long as their opportunity costs differ. Specialisation raises total world output, so only the more efficient country gains both countries can consume beyond their own production possibility frontiers.

Spot the error er-2C6-jcurve · 2C.6 2B.4 · 3 marks · level 3

This paragraph answers: "Explain the J-curve effect of a depreciation."

After a depreciation, the price of imports in Australian dollars rises straight away. Because import and export volumes take time to respond, the trade balance at first improves worsens: Australia pays more for roughly the same imports. Over the next year or two, buyers switch away from dearer imports and overseas buyers buy more of our cheaper exports, so the trade balance worsens improves. Drawn over time, the path of the trade balance looks like the letter J. The effect only works if exports and imports are completely inelastic responsive enough to price over time.

Spot the error er-4A7-phillips · 4A.7 3B.4 · 3 marks · level 3

This paragraph answers: "Explain the conflict between full employment and price stability."

The short-run Phillips curve shows a positive negative (inverse) relationship between inflation and unemployment. If expansionary policy pushes unemployment below the NAIRU, firms compete for scarce workers and wages rise faster, so inflation tends to fall rise. To bring inflation back to target, the RBA may raise interest rates, which lowers raises unemployment. This shows that policymakers cannot always achieve both objectives at the same time.

Spot the error er-1D3-hdi · 1D.3 1D.1 · 3 marks · level 2

This paragraph answers: "Outline how quality of life is measured."

The Human Development Index combines three dimensions: a long and healthy life (life expectancy at birth), knowledge (years of schooling) and a decent standard of living, measured by the inflation rate gross national income per person at purchasing power parity. The index runs from 0 to 1, and a higher score means lower higher human development. Economic growth always does not always lead to economic development, because the extra income may be spread unevenly or come at the cost of the environment.

Spot the error er-4F2-system · 4F.2 4F.1 · 3 marks · level 3

This paragraph answers: "Outline how minimum pay and conditions are set in Australia."

The National Employment Standards set minimum conditions, such as annual leave and maximum weekly hours, for employees in the national system. Modern awards set minimum pay and conditions for particular industries and occupations and are negotiated between each employer and its workers made and reviewed by the Fair Work Commission. Enterprise agreements are negotiated at the workplace level and must pass the better off overall test, which compares the agreement with the relevant National Employment Standards modern award. The national minimum wage is reviewed every five years each year in the Annual Wage Review.

Spot the error er-2B4-tot · 2B.4 3D.3 · 3 marks · level 2

This paragraph answers: "Explain how a change in the terms of trade affects Australia."

The terms of trade is the export price index divided by the GDP deflator import price index, multiplied by 100. When the world prices of iron ore and coal rise, Australia's terms of trade deteriorate improve, so each unit of exports buys more imports. Export income rises, so the balance on goods and services tends to worsen improve, and the extra demand for Australian dollars tends to make the dollar appreciate.

Spot the error er-4C3-financing · 4C.3 4C.4 · 3 marks · level 3

This paragraph answers: "Explain how the Federal Government finances a budget deficit."

The Federal Government finances most of its deficits by borrowing from the Reserve Bank selling Commonwealth Government Securities, mostly to the private sector. When these bonds are bought by foreign investors, the borrowing adds to Australia's net foreign equity net foreign debt and future interest payments add to the primary income deficit. When the budget returns to surplus, the government can use the money to increase reduce public debt or invest in funds such as the Future Fund.

Spot the error er-2D2-fta · 2D.2 1B.4 · 3 marks · level 1

This paragraph answers: "Outline Australia's approach to free trade agreements."

Australia takes part in multilateral, regional and bilateral trade agreements. A bilateral agreement involves many two countries, such as the Australia–United States Free Trade Agreement. The Regional Comprehensive Economic Partnership (RCEP) is a bilateral regional (plurilateral) agreement that includes China, Japan, Korea and the ten ASEAN members. A risk of preferential agreements is trade creation diversion, where imports switch from the lowest-cost supplier to a higher-cost member of the agreement.
