Multiple choice mc-1A1-01 · 1A.1 · 1 marks · level 1

Which of the following best describes the global economy?

  1. The economies of the G20 countries
  2. The sum of the world's national economies and the links between them through trade, finance and people
  3. The total value of world exports in a year
  4. The economies that belong to the World Trade Organization

Why B: The global economy is all the world's national economies together, linked through trade, financial flows, investment, technology and migration. Exports (C) are only one link; A and D are subsets of countries.

Multiple choice mc-1A2-01 · 1A.2 · 1 marks · level 2

Gross World Product grew by 3.2 per cent in real terms in a year in which world population grew by 1 per cent. Which statement is correct?

  1. Real output per person fell
  2. Real output per person rose by about 2.2 per cent
  3. Real output per person rose by about 4.2 per cent
  4. Nominal output must have grown by less than 3.2 per cent

Why B: Growth in real output per person is roughly real growth minus population growth: 3.2 − 1.0 ≈ 2.2 per cent. Nominal output (D) would usually grow faster than real output because of inflation.

Multiple choice mc-1A3-01 · 1A.3 · 1 marks · level 1

Which of the following would most likely increase world trade in goods and services?

  1. Higher shipping costs
  2. Higher tariffs on manufactured goods
  3. Lower tariffs and the spread of digital trade platforms
  4. More import quotas on farm products

Why C: Lower trade barriers and cheaper ways to buy and sell across borders raise trade. Higher costs and barriers (A, B, D) reduce it.

Multiple choice mc-1A4-01 · 1A.4 · 1 marks · level 2

Which of the following is an example of a global financial flow?

  1. An Australian winery exports wine to the United Kingdom
  2. A Japanese pension fund buys Australian government bonds
  3. A German tourist visits the Great Barrier Reef
  4. A worker migrates from India to Australia

Why B: Buying bonds overseas is a movement of financial capital. Wine exports and tourism are trade in goods and services; migration is a movement of labour.

Multiple choice mc-1A4-02 · 1A.4 1A.8 · 1 marks · level 3

Why can a financial crisis in one country spread quickly to other economies?

  1. Exchange rates are fixed between most major economies
  2. Financial markets are closely integrated, so investors move funds and losses across borders very quickly
  3. Most countries ban foreign investment during a crisis
  4. Governments must keep their budgets balanced

Why B: Integrated financial markets let capital move instantly, so falling asset prices, losses and loss of confidence in one market spread to others (contagion), as in the 2008 global financial crisis.

Multiple choice mc-1A5-01 · 1A.5 · 1 marks · level 1

A transnational corporation is best described as a business that

  1. sells its products only overseas.
  2. is owned by a foreign government.
  3. has production or operations in more than one country.
  4. trades only with members of a free trade agreement.

Why C: A TNC produces or operates in more than one country, usually with its headquarters in one country. It may sell at home and abroad.

Multiple choice mc-1A6-01 · 1A.6 · 1 marks · level 1

Which development has done most to lower the cost of trading services across borders?

  1. Containerised shipping
  2. Fast, cheap internet and digital communication
  3. Higher air freight costs
  4. The use of tariffs

Why B: Services such as finance, education and software can now be delivered online. Containers (A) cut the cost of moving goods rather than services.

Multiple choice mc-1A7-01 · 1A.7 · 1 marks · level 2

A smartphone is designed in the United States, uses parts made in Korea and Japan and is assembled in Vietnam. This is an example of

  1. the international division of labour.
  2. import substitution.
  3. a customs union.
  4. dumping.

Why A: Different stages of production are done in different countries according to their comparative advantage: the international division of labour, organised through global supply chains.

Multiple choice mc-1A8-01 · 1A.8 1D.9 · 1 marks · level 2

A sharp slowdown in China would most likely affect Australia through

  1. higher demand for Australian iron ore.
  2. lower export volumes and prices, and a lower Australian dollar.
  3. a higher Australian terms of trade.
  4. lower unemployment in Australian mining regions.

Why B: China is Australia's largest export market. A slowdown reduces demand for resources, lowering export volumes and prices (the terms of trade fall) and the dollar, which spreads the downturn to Australia.

Multiple choice mc-1B1-01 · 1B.1 · 1 marks · level 2

In one day, Country X can make 10 shirts or 5 bikes, and Country Y can make 6 shirts or 2 bikes. Which statement is correct?

  1. Country X has a comparative advantage in shirts
  2. Country Y has a comparative advantage in bikes
  3. Country X has a comparative advantage in bikes and Country Y in shirts
  4. Neither country can gain from trade, because Country X is better at both

Why C: For X, 1 bike costs 2 shirts; for Y, 1 bike costs 3 shirts. X makes bikes at a lower opportunity cost, so Y has the comparative advantage in shirts (1 shirt costs Y ⅓ bike but costs X ½ bike). Both gain from specialising, even though X has an absolute advantage in both.

Multiple choice mc-1B1-02 · 1B.1 · 1 marks · level 1

Which of the following is a disadvantage of free trade for an economy?

  1. Consumers can choose from a wider range of goods
  2. Resources move to industries in which the economy is most efficient
  3. Jobs may be lost in industries that cannot compete with imports
  4. Firms can sell to larger markets and gain economies of scale

Why C: Free trade forces structural change: import-competing industries may shrink, causing job losses in the short term. A, B and D are advantages.

Multiple choice mc-1B2-01 · 1B.2 · 1 marks · level 1

Which international organisation lends to countries with balance of payments difficulties, usually with conditions attached?

  1. The World Trade Organization
  2. The International Monetary Fund
  3. The OECD
  4. The G20

Why B: The IMF lends to countries facing balance of payments crises and sets conditions on their policies. The WTO deals with trade rules; the OECD and G20 are forums for research and coordination.

Multiple choice mc-1B3-01 · 1B.3 · 1 marks · level 2

Which statement about the G20 is correct?

  1. It can impose binding trade sanctions on its members
  2. It is a forum where major advanced and emerging economies coordinate economic policy
  3. It lends money to developing economies
  4. Its members are the 20 richest economies in Europe

Why B: The G20 brings together leaders and finance ministers of major advanced and emerging economies (including Australia) to coordinate policy. It has no power to impose sanctions and does not lend.

Multiple choice mc-1B4-01 · 1B.4 · 1 marks · level 1

Which of the following is a monetary union?

  1. The Australia–United States Free Trade Agreement
  2. RCEP
  3. The euro area
  4. APEC

Why C: Euro area members share one currency and one central bank (the ECB). The others are trade agreements or forums.

Multiple choice mc-1B4-02 · 1B.4 2D.2 · 1 marks · level 3

Australia signs a free trade agreement with Country Z, and Australians switch from buying cheaper TVs from Country Q to slightly dearer TVs from Country Z. This is an example of

  1. trade creation.
  2. trade diversion.
  3. dumping.
  4. comparative advantage.

Why B: Imports have switched from the lowest-cost producer (Q) to a higher-cost member of the agreement (Z) because Z no longer faces a tariff: trade diversion.

Multiple choice mc-1C1-01 · 1C.1 · 1 marks · level 1

Which argument for protection is most widely accepted by economists?

  1. Protection keeps all domestic jobs safe
  2. Temporary help for an infant industry with real potential to become competitive
  3. Protection improves the trade balance permanently
  4. Protection lowers prices for consumers

Why B: The infant industry argument has some support if the help is temporary and the industry can become competitive. Protection raises prices (D is wrong) and does not save jobs overall (A).

Multiple choice mc-1C2-01 · 1C.2 · 1 marks · level 2

Compared with a tariff that gives domestic producers the same price, an import quota

  1. raises more revenue for the government.
  2. lets importers keep the extra revenue from the higher price.
  3. has no deadweight loss.
  4. lowers the price consumers pay.

Why B: A quota and an equivalent tariff raise the price by the same amount, but with a quota the government collects nothing: the importers who hold the quota keep the extra revenue. Both have deadweight losses.

Multiple choice mc-1C2-02 · 1C.2 · 1 marks · level 2

A production subsidy to an import-competing industry will

  1. raise the price consumers pay.
  2. reduce domestic production.
  3. increase domestic production while consumers still pay the world price.
  4. raise revenue for the government.

Why C: The subsidy shifts domestic supply right. Consumers keep paying the world price, domestic production rises and imports fall, and the government bears the cost.

Multiple choice mc-1D1-01 · 1D.1 · 1 marks · level 1

Which change would show economic development rather than only economic growth?

  1. Real GDP rises by 4 per cent
  2. Exports of coal increase
  3. Life expectancy and school completion rates rise
  4. The population increases

Why C: Development is about wellbeing and living standards, such as health and education. Higher output (A, B) is growth, which may or may not improve living standards.

Multiple choice mc-1D2-01 · 1D.2 · 1 marks · level 2

Which of the following best describes the distribution of global income?

  1. Income is spread evenly between countries
  2. A small share of the world's population, mostly in advanced economies, receives a large share of world income
  3. Emerging economies receive most of the world's income
  4. Developing economies have the fastest growth in income per person

Why B: Global income is very unequal: advanced economies, with a minority of the world's people, receive a large share of world income, although the rise of China and India has narrowed the gap since the 1990s.

Multiple choice mc-1D2-02 · 1D.2 1D.5 · 1 marks · level 3

Strong growth in China and India since the 1990s has most likely

  1. increased inequality between countries but reduced it within every country.
  2. reduced inequality between countries, while inequality within some of those countries rose.
  3. had no effect on the global distribution of income.
  4. reduced the number of people living in extreme poverty in Africa only.

Why B: Rapid growth in large, poorer economies lifted hundreds of millions out of poverty and narrowed the gap between countries. Within China, inequality rose as coastal and urban incomes grew faster.

Multiple choice mc-1D3-01 · 1D.3 · 1 marks · level 1

Why is GNI per person usually converted at purchasing power parity (PPP) to compare living standards?

  1. To remove the effect of inflation over time
  2. To allow for differences in the cost of living between countries
  3. To include unpaid work
  4. To measure the distribution of income

Why B: PPP adjusts for the fact that a dollar buys more in some countries than others, giving a better comparison of what incomes can buy.

Multiple choice mc-1D4-01 · 1D.4 · 1 marks · level 1

Which economy is best described as emerging rather than developing?

  1. Malawi
  2. Niger
  3. India
  4. Mozambique

Why C: India is industrialising rapidly with fast growth and a large middle class: an emerging economy. Malawi, Niger and Mozambique are low-income developing economies.

Multiple choice mc-1D5-01 · 1D.5 · 1 marks · level 2

Which factor best explains why some nations have much higher living standards than others?

  1. Differences in population size
  2. Differences in productive capacity: education, technology, capital, infrastructure and institutions
  3. Differences in the number of trade agreements signed
  4. Differences in inflation rates

Why B: Productive capacity and strong institutions (rule of law, stable government) drive long-run differences in living standards. Population size alone does not.

Multiple choice mc-1D6-01 · 1D.6 · 1 marks · level 2

Which of the following is a likely cost of globalisation for an advanced economy?

  1. Cheaper imported consumer goods
  2. Structural unemployment in manufacturing as production moves offshore
  3. Access to foreign capital for investment
  4. Faster spread of new technology

Why B: As labour-intensive production moves to lower-cost countries, workers in advanced economies' manufacturing industries may lose jobs and need to retrain. A, C and D are benefits.

Multiple choice mc-1D6-02 · 1D.6 1D.8 · 1 marks · level 3

A critic argues that globalisation has led to a "race to the bottom". This means that

  1. countries compete to have the lowest unemployment.
  2. countries lower environmental and labour standards to attract foreign investment.
  3. global interest rates fall to zero.
  4. exporters cut prices below cost to win market share.

Why B: The argument is that to attract TNCs, governments weaken regulation of wages, working conditions and pollution, lowering standards everywhere.

Multiple choice mc-1D7-01 · 1D.7 · 1 marks · level 2

How can foreign direct investment by transnational corporations help a developing economy?

  1. It always increases the government's tax revenue
  2. It brings capital, technology, skills and jobs
  3. It guarantees higher wages for all workers
  4. It reduces the economy's reliance on exports

Why B: FDI can bring new capital, technology and management skills and create jobs. Its benefits depend on conditions; it does not guarantee higher tax revenue or wages for everyone.

Multiple choice mc-1D8-01 · 1D.8 · 1 marks · level 1

Which statement best describes the link between global economic growth and the environment?

  1. Growth always improves the environment
  2. Growth in production and consumption has added to greenhouse gas emissions, pollution and resource depletion
  3. Growth has no effect on the environment
  4. Only advanced economies affect the global environment

Why B: Rising output and energy use have increased emissions, pollution and pressure on resources. Cleaner technology can reduce the damage per unit of output, but total impacts have grown.

Multiple choice mc-1E1-01 · 1E.1 · 1 marks · level 2

Which policy change did most to open China's economy to the world after 1978?

  1. Leaving the World Trade Organization
  2. Economic reforms that allowed markets, private enterprise and foreign investment, including special economic zones
  3. Fixing the price of all goods
  4. Banning exports of manufactured goods

Why B: Under Deng Xiaoping, China introduced market reforms, opened special economic zones to foreign investment and later joined the WTO in 2001, which drove rapid export-led growth.

Multiple choice mc-2A1-01 · 2A.1 · 1 marks · level 1

Which statement best describes the direction of Australia's trade today?

  1. Most exports go to Europe
  2. Most exports go to East and South Asia, with China the largest market
  3. The United States buys most of Australia's resources
  4. New Zealand is Australia's largest export market

Why B: Asia takes about three-quarters of Australia's goods exports, and China alone takes close to 30 per cent of all exports. Trade with Europe has fallen in relative terms since the 1960s.

Multiple choice mc-2A1-02 · 2A.1 · 1 marks · level 2

Which change best describes the composition of Australia's exports over the past 40 years?

  1. Manufactured goods have replaced resources as the main export
  2. Resources and services have grown as a share of exports, and rural goods have fallen
  3. Services have fallen to a very small share of exports
  4. Exports have become less concentrated in a few commodities

Why B: Mining exports (iron ore, coal, gas) and services (education, tourism) have grown in importance, while rural goods have fallen as a share. Exports remain concentrated in a few commodities.

Multiple choice mc-2A2-01 · 2A.2 3D.1 · 1 marks · level 2

Australia's net foreign liabilities are equal to

  1. net foreign debt plus net foreign equity.
  2. net foreign debt minus net foreign equity.
  3. the current account deficit plus the budget deficit.
  4. exports minus imports.

Why A: Net foreign liabilities are what Australia owes the rest of the world in debt plus the net foreign ownership of Australian assets (equity).

Multiple choice mc-2A2-02 · 2A.2 · 1 marks · level 3

Which of the following would increase Australia's net foreign equity?

  1. An Australian bank borrows from a US bank
  2. A Canadian pension fund buys shares in an Australian airport
  3. An Australian company repays an overseas loan
  4. The government sells bonds to Australian super funds

Why B: Foreign purchases of shares and businesses raise foreign ownership of Australian assets: net foreign equity. Borrowing (A) adds to debt; C reduces debt; D is domestic.

Multiple choice mc-2B1-01 · 2B.1 · 1 marks · level 1

Where is the payment of interest on Australia's foreign debt recorded in the balance of payments?

  1. Balance on goods and services
  2. Net primary income
  3. Net secondary income
  4. The capital account

Why B: Interest and dividends paid to and received from overseas are primary income, part of the current account.

Multiple choice mc-2B1-02 · 2B.1 · 1 marks · level 2

Exports of goods $400b, imports of goods $380b, exports of services $90b, imports of services $100b, net primary income −$60b, net secondary income −$2b. What is the current account balance?

  1. +$10b
  2. −$50b
  3. −$52b
  4. −$62b

Why C: BOGS = (400 − 380) + (90 − 100) = +10. Current account = 10 − 60 − 2 = −$52 billion. B leaves out secondary income; D adds the services deficit twice.

Multiple choice mc-2B2-01 · 2B.2 · 1 marks · level 2

An Australian superannuation fund buys $5 billion of shares in US companies. In Australia's balance of payments this is recorded as

  1. a credit in the current account.
  2. portfolio investment abroad, a debit in the financial account.
  3. direct investment in Australia, a credit in the financial account.
  4. a credit in the capital account.

Why B: Buying overseas shares (below 10 per cent of the company) is portfolio investment by Australians abroad, an outflow recorded as a debit in the financial account.

Multiple choice mc-2B3-01 · 2B.3 2A.2 · 1 marks · level 3

Why do persistent financial account surpluses tend to widen Australia's current account deficit over time?

  1. They make the Australian dollar depreciate
  2. They add to foreign liabilities, so more interest and dividends are paid overseas, widening the net primary income deficit
  3. They reduce imports
  4. They are recorded as exports of services

Why B: Each year's inflow adds to foreign debt and equity. Servicing those liabilities raises payments of interest and dividends abroad, which widens the net primary income deficit in the current account.

Multiple choice mc-2B4-01 · 2B.4 · 1 marks · level 2

Export prices rise by 10 per cent and import prices rise by 2 per cent. The terms of trade index, starting at 100, becomes about

  1. 92
  2. 102
  3. 108
  4. 112

Why C: Terms of trade = export price index ÷ import price index × 100 = 110 ÷ 102 × 100 ≈ 107.8. The terms of trade improve.

Multiple choice mc-2B4-02 · 2B.4 3D.2 · 1 marks · level 2

Australia recorded current account surpluses from 2019–20 to 2022–23. Which factor contributed most?

  1. Very high commodity prices that lifted export income
  2. A large rise in imports
  3. A rise in Australia's net primary income deficit
  4. A sharp appreciation of the Australian dollar

Why A: High prices for iron ore, coal and gas lifted the terms of trade and produced large trade surpluses, enough to outweigh the primary income deficit. The current account returned to deficit from 2023–24.

Multiple choice mc-2C1-01 · 2C.1 · 1 marks · level 1

The exchange rate moves from A$1 = US$0.70 to A$1 = US$0.63. What has happened?

  1. The Australian dollar has appreciated by 10 per cent
  2. The Australian dollar has depreciated by 10 per cent
  3. The Australian dollar has depreciated by 7 per cent
  4. The US dollar has depreciated against the Australian dollar

Why B: (0.63 − 0.70) ÷ 0.70 × 100 = −10 per cent: the A$ buys fewer US dollars, a depreciation. The US dollar has appreciated.

Multiple choice mc-2C1-02 · 2C.1 · 1 marks · level 2

The Australian dollar rises against the US dollar but the Trade Weighted Index falls. Which explanation is most likely?

  1. The TWI only measures the dollar against the US dollar
  2. The A$ fell against currencies with large weights in the TWI, such as the Chinese renminbi
  3. The RBA lowered the cash rate
  4. The TWI rises whenever the A$ rises against any currency

Why B: The TWI is weighted by trade; the renminbi has the largest weight. If the A$ falls against major trading partners' currencies, the TWI can fall even while it rises against the US dollar.

Multiple choice mc-2C2-01 · 2C.2 · 1 marks · level 1

Which of the following would increase the supply of Australian dollars on the foreign exchange market?

  1. Overseas investors buy Australian property
  2. The world price of LNG rises
  3. Australians buy more imported cars
  4. More overseas students enrol in Australian universities

Why C: To pay for imports, Australians sell Australian dollars for foreign currency, increasing the supply of A$. The other three increase the demand for A$.

Multiple choice mc-2C3-01 · 2C.3 2C.6 · 1 marks · level 2

Which group is most likely to be helped by an appreciation of the Australian dollar?

  1. Australian exporters of wine
  2. Australian tourism operators
  3. Australian households buying imported goods
  4. Australian manufacturers competing with imports

Why C: An appreciation makes imports cheaper in A$. It hurts exporters, tourism operators and import-competing producers, whose prices rise relative to overseas competitors.

Multiple choice mc-2C4-01 · 2C.4 · 1 marks · level 2

Under a fixed exchange rate, what must the central bank do if there is excess supply of its currency at the fixed rate?

  1. Sell its currency
  2. Buy its currency with foreign reserves
  3. Lower interest rates
  4. Let the currency depreciate

Why B: To hold the peg, the central bank must remove the excess supply by buying its own currency with foreign currency reserves (and may also need to raise interest rates).

Multiple choice mc-2C5-01 · 2C.5 · 1 marks · level 2

Since the Australian dollar was floated, the RBA's direct intervention in the foreign exchange market has mainly been to

  1. set the exchange rate each day.
  2. keep the A$ at parity with the US dollar.
  3. calm a disorderly market during extreme swings.
  4. make exports more competitive every year.

Why C: The dollar floats. The RBA intervenes only rarely, buying or selling A$ to smooth disorderly markets, as it did during the global financial crisis in 2008.

Multiple choice mc-2C6-01 · 2C.6 3C.3 · 1 marks · level 2

A sustained 15 per cent depreciation of the Australian dollar is most likely to

  1. lower inflation and improve the current account straight away.
  2. add to inflation and, after a delay, improve the balance on goods and services.
  3. lower the A$ cost of servicing foreign-currency debt.
  4. reduce Australia's international competitiveness.

Why B: Dearer imports raise inflation, while volumes take time to adjust: the trade balance may worsen first and improve later (the J-curve). Servicing foreign-currency debt costs more (C is wrong) and competitiveness improves (D is wrong).

Multiple choice mc-2D1-01 · 2D.1 · 1 marks · level 1

Which statement best describes Australia's trade policy since the 1980s?

  1. Steady increases in tariffs to protect manufacturing
  2. Large cuts to tariffs and quotas, and a focus on free trade agreements
  3. Quotas on most imports
  4. Leaving the WTO to sign only bilateral deals

Why B: Since the 1980s Australia has cut average tariffs to very low levels (most imports now enter tariff-free) and has pursued multilateral, regional and bilateral agreements.

Multiple choice mc-2D1-02 · 2D.1 1C.2 · 1 marks · level 3

Which of the following is the best example of Australia still assisting an industry?

  1. Removing the tariff on imported cars
  2. Payments and tax incentives for local production of green metals and critical minerals
  3. Abolishing the Export Market Development Grants scheme
  4. Joining RCEP

Why B: Production incentives, such as those under Future Made in Australia, are a form of assistance (subsidies). Removing tariffs (A) is liberalisation; EMDG still operates (C is wrong); RCEP (D) is a trade agreement.

Multiple choice mc-2D2-01 · 2D.2 · 1 marks · level 1

Which of the following is a bilateral free trade agreement?

  1. The Australia–United States Free Trade Agreement
  2. The Regional Comprehensive Economic Partnership
  3. The Comprehensive and Progressive Agreement for Trans-Pacific Partnership
  4. WTO agreements

Why A: A bilateral agreement has two parties: AUSFTA. RCEP and the CPTPP are regional agreements; WTO agreements are multilateral.

Multiple choice mc-2D3-01 · 2D.3 · 1 marks · level 2

How do Australia's low tariffs mainly benefit Australian households?

  1. They raise wages in all import-competing industries
  2. They lower the prices of imported goods and raise real incomes
  3. They increase government tariff revenue
  4. They reduce the number of goods available

Why B: Low tariffs mean lower prices and more choice, raising households' real incomes. They reduce tariff revenue and can cost jobs in import-competing industries.

Multiple choice mc-2D4-01 · 2D.4 · 1 marks · level 2

The United States raises tariffs on imports from China. Which is the most likely indirect effect on Australia?

  1. Higher demand for Australian iron ore
  2. Slower Chinese growth reduces demand for Australian resources, and diverted Chinese goods may compete with Australian producers
  3. Australia's terms of trade improve
  4. Australia's exports to the US must fall

Why B: Even if not targeted, Australia is exposed: weaker Chinese manufacturing reduces demand for resources, and goods blocked from the US may be sold more cheaply in Australia.

Multiple choice mc-3A1-01 · 3A.1 · 1 marks · level 1

Which is the largest component of aggregate demand in Australia?

  1. Government spending
  2. Investment
  3. Household consumption
  4. Net exports

Why C: Household consumption is roughly half of GDP, far larger than any other component.

Multiple choice mc-3A2-01 · 3A.2 · 1 marks · level 2

In an economy, S = $90b, T = $120b, M = $80b, I = $100b, G = $110b and X = $70b. What will happen to national income?

  1. It will rise, because injections exceed withdrawals
  2. It will fall, because withdrawals exceed injections
  3. It will not change, because injections equal withdrawals
  4. It will fall, because the budget is in surplus

Why B: Withdrawals = 90 + 120 + 80 = 290; injections = 100 + 110 + 70 = 280. Withdrawals exceed injections by $10b, so income will fall until they are equal. The budget surplus (D) is part of the story but not the full test.

Multiple choice mc-3A3-01 · 3A.3 · 1 marks · level 2

The marginal propensity to save is 0.25. Investment rises by $20 billion. By how much will national income eventually rise?

  1. $5 billion
  2. $25 billion
  3. $60 billion
  4. $80 billion

Why D: k = 1 ÷ MPS = 1 ÷ 0.25 = 4. ΔY = $20b × 4 = $80 billion.

Multiple choice mc-3A4-01 · 3A.4 · 1 marks · level 2

Nominal GDP rose by 6 per cent and the general price level rose by 4 per cent. Real GDP grew by about

  1. 2 per cent
  2. 4 per cent
  3. 6 per cent
  4. 10 per cent

Why A: Real growth ≈ nominal growth − inflation = 6 − 4 = 2 per cent.

Multiple choice mc-3A5-01 · 3A.5 · 1 marks · level 2

Which is a likely cost of rapid economic growth?

  1. Lower unemployment
  2. Higher tax revenue
  3. Greater pressure on inflation and the environment
  4. Higher living standards

Why C: Fast growth can push demand above capacity, adding to inflation and import spending, and can increase pollution and resource use. A, B and D are benefits.

Multiple choice mc-3A6-01 · 3A.6 · 1 marks · level 1

Which would most likely shift aggregate supply to the right?

  1. An increase in company tax
  2. Higher productivity from new technology
  3. A rise in the world oil price
  4. Wages rising faster than productivity

Why B: Higher productivity lowers unit costs, so firms supply more at each price level. A, C and D raise costs and shift AS left.

Multiple choice mc-3A7-01 · 3A.7 · 1 marks · level 1

A period of falling real GDP and rising unemployment is called a

  1. boom.
  2. recovery.
  3. downturn.
  4. peak.

Why C: A downturn (contraction) is a period of falling or slowing growth with rising unemployment. Two consecutive quarters of negative growth is a technical recession.

Multiple choice mc-3B1-01 · 3B.1 · 1 marks · level 2

Employed 14.5 million, unemployed 0.7 million, working-age population 22.8 million. What is the participation rate?

  1. 63.6 per cent
  2. 66.7 per cent
  3. 4.6 per cent
  4. 4.8 per cent

Why B: Labour force = 14.5 + 0.7 = 15.2 million. Participation rate = 15.2 ÷ 22.8 × 100 = 66.7 per cent. A leaves out the unemployed; C is the unemployment rate.

Multiple choice mc-3B2-01 · 3B.2 3B.3 · 1 marks · level 2

Which statement best describes unemployment in Australia since the COVID-19 recession?

  1. It rose above 10 per cent and stayed there
  2. It fell to its lowest level in about 50 years (around 3.5 per cent in 2022) and has risen gradually since
  3. It has remained above 7 per cent
  4. It has fallen every year since 2020

Why B: Strong demand pushed unemployment down to about 3.5 per cent in 2022–23, the lowest in around 50 years. It rose gradually as growth slowed and interest rates rose (it reached 4.9 per cent in October 2026).

Multiple choice mc-3B4-01 · 3B.4 · 1 marks · level 3

If unemployment falls below the NAIRU, the most likely result is

  1. falling inflation.
  2. rising wage growth and inflation.
  3. a rise in the participation rate to 100 per cent.
  4. an immediate recession.

Why B: Below the NAIRU, employers compete for scarce workers, so wage growth and inflation tend to rise.

Multiple choice mc-3B5-01 · 3B.5 · 1 marks · level 2

Which group usually has an unemployment rate well above the national average?

  1. People aged 35 to 54
  2. People with a university degree
  3. Young people aged 15 to 24
  4. People who own their own business

Why C: Youth unemployment is usually about twice the national rate, because young people have less experience and are often first to lose jobs in a downturn.

Multiple choice mc-3B6-01 · 3B.6 · 1 marks · level 2

Which is an economic cost of unemployment rather than a social cost?

  1. Higher rates of mental illness
  2. Family breakdown
  3. Lost output and lower tax revenue
  4. Social isolation

Why C: Lost production and lower tax revenue (with higher welfare payments) are economic costs. A, B and D are social costs.

Multiple choice mc-3C1-01 · 3C.1 · 1 marks · level 2

The CPI was 132.0 last year and is 137.3 this year. What is the inflation rate?

  1. 3.9 per cent
  2. 4.0 per cent
  3. 5.3 per cent
  4. 37.3 per cent

Why B: (137.3 − 132.0) ÷ 132.0 × 100 = 4.0 per cent. C is the change in index points, which is not a percentage unless the earlier index is 100.

Multiple choice mc-3C2-01 · 3C.2 · 1 marks · level 2

Which statement best describes inflation in Australia since 2021?

  1. It has stayed inside the 2–3 per cent target band throughout
  2. It peaked near 8 per cent in late 2022, eased, then rose again in 2026
  3. Australia has had deflation since 2021
  4. It has been below 1 per cent since 2021

Why B: Headline inflation peaked at 7.8 per cent in the December quarter 2022 after supply shocks and strong demand, fell back towards the target, then rose again in 2026 with higher energy prices, prompting cash rate rises.

Multiple choice mc-3C3-01 · 3C.3 · 1 marks · level 2

Workers ask for bigger pay rises because they expect prices to keep rising, and firms raise prices because they expect costs to rise. This is an example of

  1. imported inflation.
  2. inflationary expectations.
  3. deflation.
  4. demand inflation from government spending.

Why B: Expectations of future inflation feed into wage claims and price setting, which can make inflation self-fulfilling. That is why central banks try to keep expectations anchored.

Multiple choice mc-3C4-01 · 3C.4 · 1 marks · level 2

Which group is most likely to be harmed by unexpectedly high inflation?

  1. Borrowers with fixed-rate loans
  2. Owners of property
  3. Lenders and savers receiving a fixed interest rate
  4. Businesses with large debts

Why C: Unexpected inflation lowers the real value of fixed repayments and savings, so lenders and savers on fixed rates lose and borrowers gain.

Multiple choice mc-3D1-01 · 3D.1 · 1 marks · level 2

Net foreign debt is $1,500 billion, net foreign equity is −$100 billion and GDP is $2,800 billion. Net foreign liabilities as a percentage of GDP are

  1. 46.4 per cent
  2. 50.0 per cent
  3. 53.6 per cent
  4. 57.1 per cent

Why B: NFL = 1,500 + (−100) = $1,400 billion. 1,400 ÷ 2,800 × 100 = 50.0 per cent. C uses debt alone; D adds the equity instead of subtracting.

Multiple choice mc-3D2-01 · 3D.2 · 1 marks · level 2

Which statement best describes the trend in Australia's net foreign equity in recent years?

  1. Foreign ownership of Australian assets has grown much faster than Australian investment abroad
  2. Australian investment abroad, much of it by superannuation funds, has grown so that net foreign equity is negative
  3. Net foreign equity has been unchanged for 20 years
  4. Australia has no foreign equity liabilities

Why B: Large outflows by superannuation funds into overseas shares mean Australians now own more foreign equity than foreigners own here, so net foreign equity is negative and net foreign liabilities are mostly debt.

Multiple choice mc-3D3-01 · 3D.3 · 1 marks · level 3

Which is a structural cause of Australia's current account deficit?

  1. A temporary fall in commodity prices
  2. A short-term boom in domestic spending
  3. National saving that has long been lower than national investment
  4. A temporary recession in our trading partners

Why C: The savings-investment gap is a long-run feature of the economy that has to be funded from overseas, a structural cause. A, B and D are cyclical.

Multiple choice mc-3E1-01 · 3E.1 · 1 marks · level 2

On a Lorenz curve diagram, the area between the line of equality and the curve is 1,200 and the area under the curve is 3,800. The Gini coefficient is

  1. 0.24
  2. 0.32
  3. 0.76
  4. 1.20

Why A: Gini = A ÷ (A + B) = 1,200 ÷ 5,000 = 0.24. B divides by B alone (1,200 ÷ 3,800 = 0.32).

Multiple choice mc-3E2-01 · 3E.2 · 1 marks · level 1

Which is the main source of income for most Australian households?

  1. Social welfare transfers
  2. Wages and salaries
  3. Dividends from shares
  4. Rent from property

Why B: Wages and salaries are by far the largest source of household income. Lower-income households rely more on transfers, and higher-income households receive more capital income.

Multiple choice mc-3E3-01 · 3E.3 · 1 marks · level 2

Which policy would most directly reduce inequality in disposable income?

  1. A rise in the GST rate
  2. Lower top marginal tax rates
  3. Higher income support payments funded by progressive income tax
  4. Lower company tax

Why C: Progressive taxes take a larger share of higher incomes and transfers lift low incomes, narrowing the gap. A raises a regressive tax; B and D mostly help higher-income earners.

Multiple choice mc-3E4-01 · 3E.4 · 1 marks · level 2

Which asset makes up the largest share of Australian household wealth?

  1. Shares
  2. Housing (owner-occupied and investment property)
  3. Bank deposits
  4. Cars and household goods

Why B: Property is the largest part of household wealth, followed by superannuation. Because older and higher-income households own more property, wealth is less equal than income.

Multiple choice mc-3E5-01 · 3E.5 · 1 marks · level 2

Which statement about the distribution of income and wealth in Australia is correct?

  1. Younger households usually hold more wealth than older households
  2. Women's average earnings are below men's (a gender pay gap)
  3. Income is distributed more unequally than wealth
  4. Occupation has no effect on income

Why B: There is a persistent gender pay gap. Wealth peaks for households near retirement age and is less equal than income; occupation strongly affects income.

Multiple choice mc-3E6-01 · 3E.6 · 1 marks · level 3

Which is an economic benefit of some inequality?

  1. Lower crime
  2. Incentives to work, study and take business risks
  3. Greater social cohesion
  4. Higher consumption by low-income households

Why B: Differences in income reward effort, skills and risk-taking. High inequality has costs, such as lost potential, weaker social cohesion and lower consumption.

Multiple choice mc-3F1-01 · 3F.1 · 1 marks · level 1

Ecologically sustainable development means

  1. stopping economic growth to protect the environment.
  2. growth that meets present needs without reducing future generations' ability to meet theirs.
  3. using only renewable energy.
  4. protecting national parks only.

Why B: ESD balances economic growth with the protection of the environment so that future generations are not left worse off (intergenerational equity).

Multiple choice mc-3F2-01 · 3F.2 · 1 marks · level 2

A factory pollutes a river used by farmers downstream. Without government action, the market will

  1. produce the socially efficient output.
  2. produce more than the socially efficient output, at a price that is too low.
  3. produce less than the socially efficient output.
  4. charge farmers for the pollution.

Why B: The factory ignores the external cost, so its private costs are below the social cost. It overproduces, and the price does not reflect the full cost: market failure.

Multiple choice mc-3F3-01 · 3F.3 · 1 marks · level 1

Which is the best example of a public good?

  1. A sandwich
  2. A private swimming pool
  3. National defence
  4. A train ticket

Why C: Defence is non-rival (one person's protection does not reduce another's) and non-excludable (people cannot be stopped from benefiting), so the market would underprovide it.

Multiple choice mc-3F4-01 · 3F.4 4G.2 · 1 marks · level 2

Which environmental issue is an example of the depletion of a natural resource?

  1. Noise from a construction site
  2. Overfishing that reduces fish stocks
  3. Litter in a park
  4. Light pollution in cities

Why B: Overfishing uses up a renewable resource faster than it can regenerate. The others are forms of pollution.

Multiple choice mc-4A1-01 · 4A.1 · 1 marks · level 2

Which statement about economic growth and quality of life is correct?

  1. Higher real GDP always means a higher quality of life
  2. Growth can raise living standards, but quality of life also depends on health, leisure, the environment and how income is shared
  3. Quality of life is measured by the inflation rate
  4. Governments do not aim for economic growth

Why B: Growth gives more goods, services and jobs, but quality of life also depends on non-material factors such as health, leisure, the environment and the distribution of income.

Multiple choice mc-4A2-01 · 4A.2 · 1 marks · level 2

The objective of full employment is best described as

  1. zero unemployment.
  2. an unemployment rate at about the NAIRU, where only frictional, seasonal and some structural unemployment remain.
  3. a participation rate of 100 per cent.
  4. everyone working full-time.

Why B: Full employment allows for people moving between jobs and some mismatch; pushing unemployment below the NAIRU adds to inflation.

Multiple choice mc-4A3-01 · 4A.3 · 1 marks · level 1

Price stability in Australia means

  1. no change in any prices.
  2. inflation of 2 to 3 per cent on average, the RBA's target.
  3. falling prices each year.
  4. inflation of 5 per cent.

Why B: The RBA targets inflation of 2 to 3 per cent, aiming for the midpoint. Low, positive inflation is the goal, not zero or falling prices.

Multiple choice mc-4A4-01 · 4A.4 · 1 marks · level 2

The objective of external stability is best described as

  1. never running a current account deficit.
  2. keeping the current account deficit and foreign liabilities at levels that can be serviced, and avoiding big swings in the exchange rate.
  3. fixing the exchange rate.
  4. having more exports than imports every year.

Why B: External stability means Australia can meet its obligations to the rest of the world over time, so investors stay confident. A deficit is not a problem in itself if it is sustainable.

Multiple choice mc-4A5-01 · 4A.5 · 1 marks · level 2

Which policy best supports the objective of environmental sustainability?

  1. Subsidies for coal-fired power
  2. A market-based price on emissions such as the Safeguard Mechanism's tradeable credits
  3. Lower fuel excise
  4. Removing water use limits

Why B: A price on emissions makes polluters count the external cost. A, C and D encourage more emissions or resource use.

Multiple choice mc-4A6-01 · 4A.6 · 1 marks · level 2

Which change would most likely make the distribution of income more equal?

  1. Lower rates of income support
  2. More progressive personal income tax
  3. A higher GST rate
  4. A rise in share prices

Why B: A more progressive tax system takes a larger share from higher incomes, narrowing the gap in disposable income.

Multiple choice mc-4A7-01 · 4A.7 · 1 marks · level 3

Strong economic growth lifts imports faster than exports. Which two objectives are in conflict?

  1. Price stability and environmental sustainability
  2. Economic growth and external stability
  3. Full employment and economic growth
  4. Distribution of income and price stability

Why B: Faster growth raises spending on imports, widening the current account deficit: a conflict between growth and external stability.

Multiple choice mc-4B1-01 · 4B.1 · 1 marks · level 2

Which policy mix is most suitable when inflation is well above target and unemployment is low?

  1. Expansionary fiscal and expansionary monetary policy
  2. Contractionary monetary policy, supported by a tighter budget
  3. Cuts to the cash rate and tax cuts
  4. Higher tariffs

Why B: With the economy running above capacity, contractionary policy slows demand. Fiscal and monetary policy working in the same direction makes the RBA's job easier.

Multiple choice mc-4C1-01 · 4C.1 · 1 marks · level 1

Government revenue is $700 billion and government spending is $735 billion. The budget outcome is

  1. a surplus of $35 billion.
  2. a deficit of $35 billion.
  3. a deficit of $1,435 billion.
  4. balanced.

Why B: Budget outcome = revenue − spending = 700 − 735 = −$35 billion, a deficit.

Multiple choice mc-4C1-02 · 4C.1 4C.2 · 1 marks · level 3

The budget deficit widens because unemployment rises in a downturn, with no change in policy. This change is

  1. an increase in the structural deficit.
  2. an increase in the cyclical component of the budget, through automatic stabilisers.
  3. discretionary expansionary fiscal policy.
  4. a contractionary budget stance.

Why B: Lower tax receipts and higher welfare payments in a downturn change the cyclical component automatically. The structural balance reflects policy decisions.

Multiple choice mc-4C2-01 · 4C.2 · 1 marks · level 2

Which budget change would most directly improve income distribution?

  1. Cutting the top marginal tax rate
  2. Increasing the rate of JobSeeker and Commonwealth Rent Assistance
  3. Raising excise on fuel
  4. Cutting company tax

Why B: Larger transfers to low-income households raise their disposable incomes, reducing inequality.

Multiple choice mc-4C3-01 · 4C.3 · 1 marks · level 2

How does the Federal Government usually finance a budget deficit?

  1. By printing money through the Reserve Bank
  2. By selling Commonwealth Government Securities to investors in Australia and overseas
  3. By raising the cash rate
  4. By selling Australian dollars on the foreign exchange market

Why B: The Australian Office of Financial Management sells government bonds and notes. Borrowing from overseas investors adds to foreign debt.

Multiple choice mc-4C4-01 · 4C.4 · 1 marks · level 2

Which is a common use of a budget surplus?

  1. Reducing government debt or investing in funds such as the Future Fund
  2. Lowering the cash rate
  3. Buying Australian dollars to raise the exchange rate
  4. Raising tariffs

Why A: A surplus can be used to repay debt, which lowers future interest costs, or to build up assets in funds such as the Future Fund.

Multiple choice mc-4D1-01 · 4D.1 · 1 marks · level 1

The main purpose of monetary policy in Australia is

  1. to balance the budget.
  2. to keep inflation at 2 to 3 per cent and support full employment.
  3. to fix the value of the Australian dollar.
  4. to set the minimum wage.

Why B: The RBA uses the cash rate to keep inflation within its target and support the maximum sustainable level of employment, which promotes sustainable growth.

Multiple choice mc-4D2-01 · 4D.2 · 1 marks · level 2

Who decides the target for the cash rate in Australia?

  1. The Treasurer
  2. The RBA Monetary Policy Board
  3. The commercial banks
  4. The Australian Prudential Regulation Authority

Why B: Since March 2025 the RBA Monetary Policy Board sets monetary policy. The RBA then manages money market conditions so the cash rate stays at the target.

Multiple choice mc-4D3-01 · 4D.3 2C.2 · 1 marks · level 2

The RBA cuts the cash rate while overseas central banks keep theirs unchanged. Which outcome is most likely?

  1. An appreciation of the A$ and lower inflation
  2. A depreciation of the A$, higher spending and more inflationary pressure
  3. Higher mortgage rates and lower spending
  4. Lower investment and a fall in employment

Why B: Lower rates reduce the return on Australian assets, so the dollar tends to depreciate. Cheaper borrowing and a lower dollar lift spending and net exports, adding to activity and inflation.

Multiple choice mc-4D3-02 · 4D.3 4H.1 · 1 marks · level 3

Why might a rise in the cash rate take a long time to lower inflation?

  1. Banks never pass on changes in the cash rate
  2. Households and firms take time to adjust spending, and many loans have fixed rates for a while
  3. The RBA can only change the cash rate once a year
  4. Higher rates immediately raise import prices

Why B: Monetary policy works with long and variable lags: borrowers on fixed rates are affected only when their loans roll over, and spending plans change slowly.

Multiple choice mc-4E1-01 · 4E.1 · 1 marks · level 2

Microeconomic reform mainly aims to

  1. smooth the business cycle in the short run.
  2. increase efficiency and productivity, shifting aggregate supply to the right.
  3. raise the inflation rate.
  4. increase tariffs to protect industry.

Why B: Microeconomic policies work on the supply side: they improve how markets and firms use resources, lifting productivity and potential growth.

Multiple choice mc-4E2-01 · 4E.2 · 1 marks · level 2

The government opens a market for a service to new competitors. In the short run, the most likely effect in that market is

  1. higher prices and fewer firms.
  2. lower prices, more choice and possible job losses at the former monopoly.
  3. no change.
  4. higher prices and more jobs at the former monopoly.

Why B: More competition pushes prices down and lifts efficiency. The incumbent may cut costs and jobs, a short-term cost of reform.

Multiple choice mc-4E3-01 · 4E.3 · 1 marks · level 2

Multiple choice mc-4F1-01 · 4F.1 · 1 marks · level 2

Which statement about Australia's industrial relations system is correct?

  1. Each state sets the pay of all private-sector workers in that state
  2. Most private-sector employees are covered by the national system under the Fair Work Act 2009
  3. There are no minimum wages in Australia
  4. All pay is set in individual contracts

Why B: Most states referred their powers to the Commonwealth, so most private-sector employees are in the national system; Western Australia's unincorporated businesses and state public servants remain in state systems.

Why B: The Fair Work Commission's Annual Wage Review sets the national minimum wage and modern award minimum wages. The Fair Work Ombudsman enforces compliance.

Multiple choice mc-4F3-01 · 4F.3 · 1 marks · level 2

  1. conciliation.
  2. mediation.
  3. arbitration.
  4. protected industrial action.

Why C: Arbitration is a binding decision by an independent third party. In conciliation and mediation the parties are helped to reach their own agreement.

Multiple choice mc-4F4-01 · 4F.4 · 1 marks · level 3

Which is an argument for decentralised (enterprise) bargaining?

  1. It gives every worker the same pay rise
  2. It lets pay and conditions reflect each workplace's productivity
  3. It reduces wage inequality between industries
  4. It removes the need for minimum standards

Why B: Enterprise bargaining can link pay rises to productivity gains at each workplace. Critics say it can widen inequality where workers have little bargaining power.

Multiple choice mc-4F5-01 · 4F.5 3B.3 · 1 marks · level 1

Which policy is best suited to reducing structural unemployment?

  1. Cutting the cash rate
  2. Funding retraining and apprenticeships in skills that are in short supply
  3. A one-off cash payment to households
  4. Raising tariffs

Why B: Structural unemployment comes from a skills or location mismatch, so education and training programs address its cause. A and C mainly help cyclical unemployment.

Multiple choice mc-4G1-01 · 4G.1 · 1 marks · level 1

Which is an example of environmental regulation?

  1. An emissions trading scheme
  2. A ban on clearing native vegetation near rivers
  3. A tax on carbon emissions
  4. A rebate for buying an electric vehicle

Why B: A ban is a regulation: a rule backed by penalties. A, C and D are market-based policies that work through prices.

Multiple choice mc-4G3-01 · 4G.3 4G.4 · 1 marks · level 2

Why B: The Climate Change Act 2022 legislated a 43 per cent cut below 2005 levels by 2030 and net zero emissions by 2050. Australia remains in the Paris Agreement.

Multiple choice mc-4H1-01 · 4H.1 · 1 marks · level 2

The time between an economic problem starting and policymakers being sure it has started is the

  1. implementation lag.
  2. recognition lag.
  3. impact lag.
  4. political constraint.

Why B: Data arrive with a delay and are revised, so it takes time to recognise a downturn or an inflation problem.

Multiple choice mc-4H2-01 · 4H.2 · 1 marks · level 2

Which is the best example of a global influence limiting Australian economic policy?

  2. A global recession that cuts demand for exports even after the RBA lowers interest rates
  3. Delays in collecting GDP data
  4. The time taken to build new infrastructure

Why B: Australia is a small open economy: world events can overwhelm domestic policy. A is a political constraint; C and D are time lags.

Multiple choice mc-4H3-01 · 4H.3 · 1 marks · level 2

  1. a recognition lag.
  2. a global influence.

  4. an automatic stabiliser.

Why C: Political considerations, such as elections or the need to pass laws through the Senate, can stop governments from using the best economic policy at the right time.

Multiple choice mc-4I3-01 · 4I.3 4D.3 · 1 marks · level 2

  3. Fixing the exchange rate

Multiple choice mc-4I4-01 · 4I.4 3D.3 · 1 marks · level 3

Which policy would most directly help external stability in the long run?

  1. Policies that lift national saving and international competitiveness
  2. Larger budget deficits funded by overseas borrowing
  3. Higher tariffs on all imports
  4. A sharp cut in interest rates to encourage borrowing

Why A: Higher saving narrows the savings-investment gap, and greater competitiveness lifts exports, both reducing reliance on foreign borrowing over time.

Read a diagram rd-fig1 · 1C.2 · 8 marks · level 2

The figure shows the domestic market for steel. The world price is $20 a tonne. The government then imposes a tariff of $10 a tonne.

(a) Calculate the quantity of steel imported before the tariff. (1 marks)

80 − 20 = 60 million tonnes.

(b) Calculate the quantity of steel imported after the tariff. (1 marks)

70 − 30 = 40 million tonnes.

(c) Calculate the government's revenue from the tariff. (1 marks)

$10 × 40 million tonnes = $400 million (tariff × imports after the tariff, not before).

(d) Calculate the change in domestic production and the deadweight loss of the tariff. (2 marks)

Domestic production rises from 20 to 30 million tonnes (+10 million). Deadweight loss = ½ × 10 × 10 (production) + ½ × 10 × 10 (consumption) = $50 million + $50 million = $100 million.

(e) Explain who gains and who loses from the tariff. (3 marks)

  • Domestic producers gain: they sell more (20 to 30 million tonnes) at a higher price, a producer surplus gain of $250 million (1)
  • The government gains $400 million of tariff revenue (1)
  • Consumers lose: they pay $30 instead of $20 and buy less, a consumer surplus loss of $750 million, larger than the gains (deadweight loss $100 million) (1)

Read a diagram rd-fig6 · 1C.2 · 6 marks · level 3

The figure shows the domestic market for bicycles (quantities in thousands). The world price is $100. The government imposes a tariff of $20 a bicycle.

(a) Calculate the number of bicycles imported before and after the tariff. (2 marks)

Before: 100,000 − 30,000 = 70,000. After: 80,000 − 40,000 = 40,000.

(b) Calculate the tariff revenue. (1 marks)

$20 × 40,000 = $800,000.

(c) Calculate the loss of consumer surplus, the gain in producer surplus and the total deadweight loss. (3 marks)

Consumer surplus loss = ½ × (100,000 + 80,000) × $20 = $1,800,000. Producer surplus gain = ½ × (30,000 + 40,000) × $20 = $700,000. Deadweight loss = $100,000 (production) + $200,000 (consumption) = $300,000. Check: 1,800,000 = 700,000 + 800,000 + 300,000.

Read a diagram rd-fig2 · 1C.2 · 6 marks · level 3

The figure shows the domestic market for cheese (million kg). The world price is $4 a kg. The government then limits imports with a quota of 30 million kg.

(a) Calculate imports of cheese before the quota. (1 marks)

90 − 20 = 70 million kg.

(b) State the domestic price of cheese once the quota applies. (1 marks)

$6 a kg: the price at which domestic demand (70 million kg) is 30 million kg more than domestic supply (40 million kg).

(c) Calculate the extra revenue importers receive because of the higher price, and explain who receives it. (2 marks)

($6 − $4) × 30 million kg = $60 million. The importers who hold the quota keep it: unlike a tariff, the government receives nothing.

(d) Calculate the deadweight loss of the quota. (2 marks)

½ × (40 − 20) × $2 + ½ × (90 − 70) × $2 = $20 million + $20 million = $40 million. Check: consumer surplus loss $160 million = producer gain $60 million + importers $60 million + deadweight loss $40 million.

Read a diagram rd-fig7 · 1C.2 · 5 marks · level 2

The figure shows the domestic market for sugar (million kg). The world price is $2 a kg. The government sets an import quota of 40 million kg.

(a) Calculate imports before the quota and the domestic price after it. (2 marks)

Before: 100 − 20 = 80 million kg. After the quota the price rises to $3, where demand (80) exceeds domestic supply (40) by the 40 million kg quota.

(b) Calculate the change in domestic production. (1 marks)

It rises from 20 to 40 million kg, an increase of 20 million kg.

(c) Calculate the loss of consumer surplus and explain why it is larger than the gain to producers. (2 marks)

½ × (100 + 80) × $1 = $90 million. Producers gain only $30 million; importers keep $40 million and $20 million is deadweight loss, so the consumers' loss is larger than any one group's gain.

Read a diagram rd-fig3 · 1C.2 2D.1 · 6 marks · level 3

The figure shows the domestic market for canned fruit (million cans). The world price is $50 a can. The government pays local producers a subsidy of $20 a can, which shifts supply from S1 to S2.

(a) Calculate domestic production and imports before and after the subsidy. (2 marks)

Production rises from 40 to 60 million cans. Consumption stays at 120 million cans (consumers still pay $50), so imports fall from 80 to 60 million cans.

(b) Calculate the cost of the subsidy to the government. (1 marks)

$20 × 60 million cans = $1,200 million (the subsidy is paid on all domestic production, not just the increase).

(c) Explain why a subsidy has a smaller deadweight loss than a tariff that gives producers the same protection. (3 marks)

  • Consumers still pay the world price, so consumption does not fall and there is no consumption deadweight loss (1)
  • The only loss is on production: the extra 20 million cans cost more to make here than to import, ½ × 20 × $20 = $200 million (1)
  • The cost moves from consumers to taxpayers through the budget, which has an opportunity cost (1)

Read a diagram rd-fig8 · 1C.2 2D.1 · 4 marks · level 3

The figure shows the domestic market for solar panels (thousands). The world price is $200 a panel. The government pays local makers a subsidy of $80 a panel, which shifts supply from S1 to S2.

(a) Calculate the change in domestic production and the change in imports. (2 marks)

Production rises from 10,000 to 18,000 panels (+8,000). Consumption stays at 40,000, so imports fall from 30,000 to 22,000.

(b) Calculate the cost of the subsidy to the government. (1 marks)

$80 × 18,000 = $1,440,000.

(c) Calculate the deadweight loss. (1 marks)

½ × 8,000 × $80 = $320,000.

Short answer sd-3B1-lf · 3B.1 3B.2 · 7 marks · level 2 · modelled on II2014Q23(a) II2014Q23(b)

The table shows labour market data for a hypothetical economy.

Year 1Year 2
Employed14,00014,200
Unemployed600760
Working-age population22,00022,400

(a) Calculate the unemployment rate in Year 2. (1 marks)

Labour force = 14,200 + 760 = 14,960 thousand. Unemployment rate = 760 ÷ 14,960 × 100 = 5.1%.

(b) Calculate the participation rate in Year 2. (1 marks)

14,960 ÷ 22,400 × 100 = 66.8%.

(c) Explain why the unemployment rate rose even though employment increased. (2 marks)

  • The labour force grew faster than employment: it rose by 360,000 (2.5%) while employment rose by 200,000 (1.4%) (1)
  • More people entered the labour force looking for work (participation rose from 66.4% to 66.8%), so the number unemployed rose (1)

(d) Explain ONE policy the government could use to reduce structural unemployment. (3 marks)

  • Identifies a suitable policy, e.g. education and training or apprenticeship programs (1)
  • Explains how it addresses the skills mismatch between workers and the jobs available (1)
  • Explains the effect, e.g. more employable workers, lower long-term unemployment, possibly a lower NAIRU (1)

Short answer sd-3C1-cpi · 3C.1 3C.3 4D.3 · 6 marks · level 2 · modelled on II2019Q24(a) II2019Q24(b)

The table shows data for a hypothetical economy. The central bank's inflation target is 2 to 3 per cent.

Year 1Year 2Year 3
Consumer Price Index125.0130.0136.5
Unemployment rate (%)4.23.93.6

(a) Calculate the inflation rate in Year 3. (1 marks)

(136.5 − 130.0) ÷ 130.0 × 100 = 5.0%.

(b) Outline ONE likely cause of the inflation shown in the table. (2 marks)

  • Identifies a cause consistent with the data, e.g. demand inflation as unemployment falls to low levels (1)
  • Outlines how it raises prices, e.g. spending grows faster than capacity and tight labour markets push wages up (1)

(c) Justify an appropriate monetary policy stance for this economy. Support your answer with reference to the table. (3 marks)

  • Recommends contractionary (tighter) monetary policy: raise the cash rate (1)
  • Justifies it with the data: inflation (5.0%) is above the 2–3% target and unemployment is low and falling (3.6%), suggesting excess demand (1)
  • Explains how higher interest rates reduce spending and inflation, noting the possible rise in unemployment (1)

Short answer sd-2B1-bop · 2B.1 2B.2 2B.3 · 6 marks · level 2 · modelled on II2023Q22(a) II2011Q22(a)(i)

The table shows balance of payments data for a hypothetical economy. Net errors and omissions are zero.

Item$ billion
Exports of goods480
Imports of goods450
Exports of services110
Imports of services125
Net primary income−75
Net secondary income−2
Balance on capital account−1

(a) Calculate the balance on goods and services. (1 marks)

(480 − 450) + (110 − 125) = 30 − 15 = $15 billion (a surplus).

(b) Calculate the balance on current account. (1 marks)

15 + (−75) + (−2) = −$62 billion (a deficit).

(c) Calculate the balance on the financial account. (1 marks)

Current account + capital account + financial account = 0, so −62 + (−1) + financial account = 0: the financial account is +$63 billion (a surplus).

(d) Explain the relationship between the financial account surplus and the net primary income deficit. (3 marks)

  • The financial account surplus shows foreign borrowing and investment flowing in to fund the current account deficit (1)
  • These inflows add to the economy's foreign debt and equity liabilities (1)
  • Servicing them (interest and dividends) is recorded as debits in net primary income, so the deficit grows over time and must itself be financed (1)

Short answer sd-2C1-xr · 2C.1 2C.3 2C.6 · 6 marks · level 2

The table shows the value of the Australian dollar in two years (hypothetical data).

Year 1Year 2
US dollar0.680.64
Japanese yen98.0104.0
Euro0.600.58
TWI (May 1970 = 100)63.062.4

(a) Calculate the percentage change in the value of the A$ against the US dollar. (1 marks)

(0.64 − 0.68) ÷ 0.68 × 100 = −5.9%, a depreciation.

(b) Identify the currency in the table against which the A$ appreciated. (1 marks)

The Japanese yen (one A$ buys 104 yen instead of 98).

(c) Explain why the TWI fell by much less than the A$ fell against the US dollar. (2 marks)

  • The TWI measures the A$ against a basket of trading partners' currencies, weighted by trade (1)
  • The A$ rose against some currencies (the yen) and fell less against others, so the fall against the US dollar was partly offset (1)

(d) Explain how this change in the exchange rate could affect Australian exporters. (2 marks)

  • Australian exports become cheaper in US dollar terms, so exporters can sell more (1)
  • Contracts priced in US dollars earn more A$, lifting exporters' revenue and profits (1)

Short answer sd-4C1-budget · 4C.1 4C.2 4C.3 · 8 marks · level 3

The table shows budget data for a hypothetical economy.

Year 1Year 2
Revenue680690
Expenditure700735
GDP2,7002,780

(a) Calculate the budget outcome in Year 2, in dollars and as a percentage of GDP. (2 marks)

690 − 735 = −$45 billion, a deficit of 45 ÷ 2,780 × 100 = 1.6% of GDP.

(b) Identify the change in the budget stance from Year 1 to Year 2. (1 marks)

More expansionary: the deficit widened from $20 billion (0.7% of GDP) to $45 billion (1.6% of GDP).

(c) Explain ONE reason the deficit could have widened without any new policy decision. (2 marks)

  • Automatic stabilisers: a slowdown lowers tax revenue and raises welfare payments (1)
  • So the cyclical component of the budget moves towards deficit, even though policy settings are unchanged (1)

(d) Explain how the government could finance this deficit and ONE possible effect. (3 marks)

  • By selling Commonwealth Government Securities (bonds) to investors in Australia or overseas (1)
  • Effect explained, e.g. borrowing competes for funds and may raise interest rates (crowding out) (1)
  • Or: borrowing from overseas adds to foreign debt and future interest payments in the current account (1)

Short answer sd-3A2-flow · 3A.2 3A.3 · 5 marks · level 2 · modelled on II2016Q24(a) II2013Q22(a)

The table shows data for a hypothetical economy. The marginal propensity to consume is 0.75.

Item$ billion
Savings120
Taxation200
Imports140
Investment130
Government spending210
Exports110

(a) State whether national income will rise, fall or stay the same. Give a reason. (1 marks)

It will fall: withdrawals (120 + 200 + 140 = $460b) exceed injections (130 + 210 + 110 = $450b).

(b) Calculate the value of the multiplier. (1 marks)

k = 1 ÷ (1 − 0.75) = 4.

(c) Calculate the change in national income if government spending rises by $10 billion. (1 marks)

$10 billion × 4 = $40 billion increase.

(d) Explain the implications of the multiplier for fluctuations in economic activity. (2 marks)

  • Small changes in spending (such as investment or exports) cause larger changes in national income (1)
  • So shocks are magnified into larger booms and downturns; a larger multiplier means larger fluctuations (1)

Short answer sd-3E1-quint · 3E.1 3E.3 · 6 marks · level 2

The table shows the share of income received by each fifth (quintile) of households in a hypothetical economy.

QuintileMarket incomeDisposable income
Lowest 20%27
Second612
Third1517
Fourth2523
Highest 20%5241

(a) Calculate the ratio of the highest quintile's share of disposable income to the lowest quintile's share. (1 marks)

41 ÷ 7 = 5.9 to 1.

(b) Identify which distribution is more equal, and explain why. (2 marks)

  • Disposable income is more equal: the top quintile's share falls from 52% to 41% and the bottom's rises from 2% to 7% (1)
  • Because progressive income tax takes more from high incomes and transfer payments add to low incomes (1)

(c) Explain how a Lorenz curve for disposable income would differ from one for market income. (3 marks)

  • The Lorenz curve for disposable income lies closer to the line of equality (1)
  • Area A (between the line of equality and the curve) is smaller (1)
  • So the Gini coefficient for disposable income is lower than for market income (1)

Short answer sd-3D1-ext · 3D.1 3D.3 · 6 marks · level 3

The table shows external data for a hypothetical economy.

Item$ billion
Net foreign debt1,450
Net foreign equity−80
Current account deficit55
GDP2,750

(a) Calculate net foreign liabilities as a percentage of GDP. (1 marks)

NFL = 1,450 + (−80) = $1,370 billion; 1,370 ÷ 2,750 × 100 = 49.8% of GDP.

(b) Calculate the current account deficit as a percentage of GDP. (1 marks)

55 ÷ 2,750 × 100 = 2.0% of GDP.

(c) Explain TWO risks of a high level of net foreign debt. (4 marks)

  • Servicing costs: more interest is paid overseas, widening the net primary income deficit and the CAD (2)
  • Vulnerability: lenders may demand a higher risk premium or withdraw funds, causing a depreciation or forcing contractionary policy; a depreciation raises the A$ cost of foreign-currency debt (2)

Short answer sd-2B4-tot · 2B.4 2C.2 · 5 marks · level 2 · modelled on II2010Q21(a)

The table shows price indexes for an economy's exports and imports.

Year 1Year 2Year 3
Export price index100115108
Import price index100104106

(a) Calculate the terms of trade in Year 2. (1 marks)

115 ÷ 104 × 100 = 110.6.

(b) Identify the trend in the terms of trade from Year 2 to Year 3. (1 marks)

It deteriorated: Year 3 = 108 ÷ 106 × 100 = 101.9, down from 110.6.

(c) Explain how the change in the terms of trade from Year 1 to Year 2 could affect the exchange rate and the balance on goods and services. (3 marks)

  • Higher export prices mean more export income for each unit sold (1)
  • The balance on goods and services improves (1)
  • Overseas buyers need more of the domestic currency, so demand for it rises and it appreciates (1)

Short answer sd-3A4-gdp · 3A.4 1D.3 · 4 marks · level 2

The table shows data for a hypothetical economy. Year 1 is the base year.

Year 1Year 2
Nominal GDP ($ billion)2,5002,650
Price index100104

(a) Calculate real GDP in Year 2. (1 marks)

2,650 × 100 ÷ 104 = $2,548 billion.

(b) Calculate the real growth rate from Year 1 to Year 2. (1 marks)

(2,548 − 2,500) ÷ 2,500 × 100 = 1.9%.

(c) Outline ONE limitation of real GDP as a measure of living standards. (2 marks)

  • Identifies a limitation, e.g. it ignores population growth, the distribution of income, unpaid work, leisure or environmental damage (1)
