Short answer sd-1C2-tariff · 1C.2 · 6 marks · level 2 · modelled on II2024Q21(a)(i) II2024Q21(a)(ii)

The table shows the domestic market for a good. The world price is $40, and the government imposes a tariff of $10 a unit.

Price ($)Quantity demandedQuantity supplied by domestic firms
4012030
5011045

(a) Calculate the quantity imported before the tariff. (1 marks)

120 − 30 = 90,000 units.

(b) Calculate the quantity imported after the tariff. (1 marks)

110 − 45 = 65,000 units.

(c) Calculate the government's revenue from the tariff. (1 marks)

$10 × 65,000 = $650,000.

(d) Explain the effect of the tariff on the allocation of resources. (3 marks)

  • Domestic production rises from 30,000 to 45,000 units, drawing resources into a less efficient industry (1)
  • Consumers buy less and pay more, so resources are not allocated to their most valued uses (1)
  • The result is a deadweight loss: production and consumption losses that no one gains (1)

Short answer sd-1D3-hdi · 1D.3 1D.4 1D.1 · 6 marks · level 2

The table shows development indicators for three countries.

Country ACountry BCountry C
GNI per person (PPP, US$)62,00018,0002,200
Life expectancy (years)837762
Mean years of schooling12.78.04.5

(a) Identify which country is most likely a developing economy. (1 marks)

Country C: the lowest income, life expectancy and schooling.

(b) Explain why GNI per person alone is a limited measure of a country's level of development. (3 marks)

  • It measures average income, not how it is distributed (1)
  • It leaves out health, education, freedom and the environment, which are part of wellbeing (1)
  • Two countries with similar incomes can have very different life expectancy and schooling (1)

(c) Outline how the Human Development Index gives a fuller picture. (2 marks)

  • It combines three dimensions: a long and healthy life, knowledge and a decent standard of living (1)
  • Each is measured (life expectancy, years of schooling, GNI per person at PPP) and combined in an index from 0 to 1 (1)

Short answer sa-1A1-01 · 1A.1 · 2 marks · level 1

Outline what is meant by the global economy.

  • All the world's national economies together (1)
  • Linked through trade, financial flows, investment, technology and the movement of people (1)

Short answer sa-1A2-01 · 1A.2 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain why Gross World Product is usually compared using purchasing power parity rather than market exchange rates.

  • GWP adds up the output of all economies, which must be converted to a common currency (1)
  • Market exchange rates do not reflect differences in what money buys in each country (1)
  • PPP adjusts for price differences, so it gives a better comparison of real output, raising the measured share of emerging and developing economies (1)

Short answer sa-1A4-01 · 1A.4 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain why global financial flows have grown faster than world trade.

  • Deregulation of financial markets and removal of capital controls since the 1980s (1)
  • Technology lets funds move instantly and cheaply around the world (1)
  • Investors seek the highest returns and spread risk, and floating exchange rates create speculation and hedging (1)

Short answer sa-1A7-01 · 1A.7 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain how the international division of labour affects advanced economies such as Australia.

  • Labour-intensive production moves to lower-cost economies (1)
  • Advanced economies specialise in skilled services, design and capital-intensive industries (1)
  • Workers in declining industries may face structural unemployment and need to retrain; skilled migration fills shortages (1)

Short answer sa-1B3-01 · 1B.3 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain the influence of the G20 on the global economy.

  • The G20 brings together major advanced and emerging economies, which produce most of world output (1)
  • It coordinates policy, e.g. stimulus during the global financial crisis in 2008–09 and financial regulation reforms (1)
  • Its influence is limited because its decisions are not binding (1)

Short answer sa-2D2-01 · 2D.2 · 4 marks · level 2

Explain TWO benefits for Australia of the Regional Comprehensive Economic Partnership (RCEP).

  • First benefit explained, e.g. lower tariffs and common rules of origin across 15 Asia-Pacific economies make it easier for Australian firms to use regional supply chains (2)
  • Second benefit explained, e.g. better access for Australian services and investment, or a rules-based framework that includes China (2)

Short answer sa-3A1-01 · 3A.1 · 3 marks · level 1

Outline the components of aggregate demand.

  • AD = C + I + G + (X − M) (1)
  • Consumption by households and investment by firms (1)
  • Government spending and net exports (exports minus imports) (1)

Short answer sa-3A7-01 · 3A.7 · 3 marks · level 2

Describe the phases of the business cycle.

  • Upswing (recovery and boom): rising growth, falling unemployment, rising inflation (1)
  • Peak, then downswing: slowing growth, rising unemployment (1)
  • Trough: lowest point; a recession is two consecutive quarters of negative growth (1)

Short answer sa-3B5-01 · 3B.5 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain why young people are more affected by unemployment than other groups.

  • They have less experience and fewer skills than older workers (1)
  • They are often in casual or entry-level jobs that are cut first in a downturn (1)
  • When firms stop hiring, new entrants to the labour force find it hardest to get a first job (1)

Short answer sa-3C2-01 · 3C.2 · 3 marks · level 2

Describe the trend in Australian inflation since 2020.

  • Low inflation in 2020 during the COVID-19 recession (1)
  • A sharp rise to a peak of 7.8 per cent in late 2022, from supply shocks and strong demand (1)
  • A fall back towards the target in 2024–25, then a rise again in 2026 (4.9 per cent in the year to October) (1)

Short answer sa-3D2-01 · 3D.2 · 3 marks · level 2

Describe the trends in Australia's net foreign liabilities over the past decade.

  • Net foreign liabilities have fallen as a share of GDP (1)
  • Net foreign equity has turned negative as superannuation funds invest heavily overseas (1)
  • So the liabilities are now mainly net foreign debt, much of it in Australian dollars (1)

Short answer sa-3E2-01 · 3E.2 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain how the sources of income differ between low-income and high-income households.

  • Low-income households rely more on social welfare transfers, such as pensions and JobSeeker (1)
  • Middle-income households rely mainly on wages and salaries (1)
  • High-income households receive more income from capital (interest, dividends, rent) as well as high wages (1)

Short answer sa-3E4-01 · 3E.4 3E.5 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain why wealth is distributed more unequally than income.

  • Wealth accumulates over a lifetime, so older households hold much more than younger ones (1)
  • Wealth earns income (rent, dividends, capital gains), which is reinvested, so it compounds (1)
  • Property and inheritance concentrate wealth; housing price rises have favoured existing owners (1)

Short answer sa-3E5-01 · 3E.5 · 4 marks · level 2

Describe TWO dimensions of inequality in Australia.

  • First dimension described, e.g. gender: women earn less on average (a gender pay gap) and retire with less superannuation (2)
  • Second dimension described, e.g. age, occupation, ethnic background or Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander peoples, with an example (2)

Short answer sa-3F1-01 · 3F.1 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain the concept of ecologically sustainable development.

  • Development that meets the needs of today without compromising the ability of future generations to meet theirs (1)
  • Balances economic growth with protection of the environment (1)
  • Includes intergenerational equity and the precautionary principle (1)

Short answer sa-3F4-01 · 3F.4 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain why preserving natural environments is an economic issue.

  • Natural environments provide valuable services (clean water, biodiversity, tourism) that are not priced in markets (1)
  • Because they are public goods or common resources, markets overuse them (1)
  • Their loss is often permanent, so the cost falls on future generations (1)

Short answer sa-4A1-01 · 4A.1 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain why economic growth is an objective of government policy.

  • Growth creates jobs and lowers unemployment (1)
  • It raises incomes and living standards (1)
  • It increases tax revenue for services such as health and education (1)

Short answer sa-4A2-01 · 4A.2 · 2 marks · level 1

Outline what is meant by full employment.

  • An unemployment rate at about the NAIRU, not zero (1)
  • Only frictional, seasonal and some structural unemployment remain (1)

Short answer sa-4A3-01 · 4A.3 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain why governments aim for low and stable inflation.

  • High inflation erodes purchasing power, especially for people on fixed incomes (1)
  • Uncertainty about prices discourages investment and distorts decisions (1)
  • Inflation above trading partners' reduces international competitiveness (1)

Short answer sa-4A4-01 · 4A.4 · 3 marks · level 3

Explain what external stability means for Australia.

  • A sustainable current account deficit and level of foreign liabilities (1)
  • Australia can meet its foreign obligations, so investors stay confident (1)
  • An exchange rate without extreme swings (1)

Short answer sa-4A5-01 · 4A.5 · 2 marks · level 1

Outline the objective of environmental sustainability.

  • To use resources and the environment in a way that allows growth to continue in the future (1)
  • E.g. cutting emissions to net zero by 2050 and protecting natural environments (1)

Short answer sa-4A6-01 · 4A.6 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain why governments aim for a more equitable distribution of income.

  • To reduce poverty and give everyone access to a basic standard of living (1)
  • High inequality can weaken social cohesion and lower opportunity (1)
  • Lower-income households spend more of each dollar, supporting demand; equity is also a value judgement (1)

Short answer sa-4B1-01 · 4B.1 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain the rationale for macroeconomic policies.

  • To stabilise the business cycle by managing aggregate demand (1)
  • Expansionary policy in a downturn supports growth and employment (1)
  • Contractionary policy in a boom restrains inflation and external imbalances (1)

Short answer sa-4C4-01 · 4C.4 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain how the government can use a budget surplus.

  • Repay government debt, lowering future interest costs (1)
  • Invest in funds such as the Future Fund for future liabilities (1)
  • A surplus is contractionary, which can help restrain inflation in a boom (1)

Short answer sa-4D1-01 · 4D.1 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain the purpose of monetary policy in Australia.

  • To keep inflation within the 2–3 per cent target on average (1)
  • To support full employment (the maximum sustainable level of employment) (1)
  • Low, stable inflation and employment promote sustainable growth and welfare (1)

Short answer sa-4F1-01 · 4F.1 · 3 marks · level 3

Outline the roles of the national and state industrial relations systems.

  • The national system under the Fair Work Act 2009 covers most private-sector employees (1)
  • The Fair Work Commission sets minimum wages and awards and approves agreements; the Fair Work Ombudsman enforces them (1)
  • State systems cover state public servants and, in WA, unincorporated businesses (1)

Short answer sa-4F2-01 · 4F.2 · 3 marks · level 2

Outline how minimum wages are set in Australia.

  • The Fair Work Commission conducts the Annual Wage Review (1)
  • It sets the national minimum wage and modern award minimum wages (1)
  • It weighs factors such as living costs, inflation, productivity, employment and the needs of the low-paid (1)

Short answer sa-4F3-01 · 4F.3 · 3 marks · level 2

Outline how industrial disputes are resolved in Australia.

  • Through negotiation between the parties (1)
  • The Fair Work Commission can help by conciliation or mediation (1)
  • In some cases it can arbitrate (make a binding decision); protected industrial action is allowed only during bargaining (1)

Short answer sa-4F5-01 · 4F.5 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain how education and training programs can reduce unemployment.

  • They give workers the skills firms need, reducing the skills mismatch (1)
  • They help the long-term unemployed and young people become job-ready (1)
  • A more skilled workforce raises productivity and can lower the NAIRU (1)

Short answer sa-4G3-01 · 4G.3 4G.4 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain how emissions targets are used in environmental management.

  • Targets set a goal, e.g. a 43 per cent cut on 2005 levels by 2030 and net zero by 2050 (1)
  • They guide policies such as the Safeguard Mechanism and support for renewable energy (1)
  • They give investors certainty and meet commitments under the Paris Agreement (1)

Short answer sa-4H2-01 · 4H.2 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain how global influences can limit the effectiveness of Australian economic policy.

  • Australia is a small open economy, affected by world growth, commodity prices and interest rates overseas (1)
  • A global downturn can reduce growth despite expansionary policy at home (1)
  • Global supply shocks (e.g. oil prices) can raise inflation that the RBA can only slow at the cost of jobs (1)

Short answer sa-4H3-01 · 4H.3 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain how political constraints can limit economic policy.

  • Governments may avoid unpopular measures, such as tax rises or spending cuts, before an election (1)
  • Legislation must pass the Senate, where minor parties may block it (1)
  • Promises and pressure from interest groups can lead to policies that are not the best economically (1)

Short answer sa-4I2-01 · 4I.2 4F.5 · 4 marks · level 3

Assess the effectiveness of government policies in reducing unemployment in Australia.

  • Macroeconomic policy supported demand during COVID-19, helping unemployment fall to about 3.5 per cent by 2022 (1)
  • Monetary tightening from 2022 and in 2026 saw unemployment rise gradually (to 4.9 per cent in October 2026) (1)
  • Labour market programs target structural and long-term unemployment (1)
  • Judgement: effective in reducing cyclical unemployment, less so for structural unemployment (1)

Short answer sa-4I4-01 · 4I.4 3D.3 · 4 marks · level 3

Explain how government policies can improve Australia's external stability.

  • Fiscal consolidation raises national saving and reduces reliance on foreign borrowing (1)
  • Microeconomic reform improves international competitiveness and export performance (1)
  • Monetary policy keeps inflation low, supporting competitiveness and investor confidence (1)
  • A floating exchange rate helps absorb external shocks (1)

Explain how government policies affect the distribution of income in Australia.

  • Progressive income tax narrows differences in after-tax income (1)
  • Transfer payments lift low incomes (1)
  • The social wage provides services such as Medicare and education (1)
  • Minimum wages and labour market policies affect market incomes (1)

Short answer sa-4I6-01 · 4I.6 4G.2 · 4 marks · level 3

Assess the effectiveness of policies to achieve environmental sustainability in Australia.

  • Emissions have fallen (about a quarter below 2005 levels), largely from electricity and land use (1)
  • Limits: emissions from transport and industry, political constraints, and dependence on fossil fuel exports (1)

Short answer sa-1A3-01 · 1A.3 1A.6 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain how changes in technology have increased world trade in goods and services.

  • Containerisation and larger ships have cut the cost of moving goods (1)
  • The internet and digital platforms let services such as education, finance and design be traded across borders (1)
  • Lower costs and better information mean more goods and services can be traded profitably, so trade grows faster than world output (1)

Short answer sa-1A5-01 · 1A.5 1D.7 · 4 marks · level 2

Explain TWO effects of transnational corporations on a host economy.

  • One positive effect, explained: e.g. foreign direct investment brings capital, technology and management skills, raising productivity and creating jobs (2)
  • One negative effect, explained: e.g. profits flow back to the home country, local firms may be crowded out, or TNCs may use transfer pricing to reduce tax paid (2)

Short answer sa-1A8-01 · 1A.8 1D.9 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain how a downturn in the international business cycle can affect the Australian economy.

  • Weaker growth overseas lowers demand for Australian exports, especially resources (1)
  • Lower export volumes and prices reduce the terms of trade, national income and investment in the resources sector (1)
  • Growth slows and unemployment rises in Australia; the dollar may depreciate, which partly cushions the effect (1)

Short answer sa-1B1-01 · 1B.1 · 4 marks · level 2

Explain how free trade can increase living standards in both countries that trade.

  • Each country specialises in goods in which it has a comparative advantage (lower opportunity cost) (1)
  • Specialisation raises total output, so world production rises (1)
  • Through trade, each country consumes beyond its own production possibilities (1)
  • Consumers benefit from lower prices and more choice; firms gain economies of scale from larger markets (1)

Short answer sa-1B2-01 · 1B.2 · 4 marks · level 2

Distinguish between the roles of the World Trade Organization and the International Monetary Fund.

  • WTO: sets and enforces the rules of world trade (1)
  • WTO: runs negotiations to reduce trade barriers and settles trade disputes between members (1)
  • IMF: promotes international financial stability and monitors members' economic policies (1)
  • IMF: lends to countries facing balance of payments crises, usually with conditions (1)

Short answer sa-1B4-01 · 1B.4 · 4 marks · level 3

Assess the advantages and disadvantages of bilateral free trade agreements compared with multilateral agreements.

  • Advantage: bilateral agreements are quicker and easier to negotiate than multilateral deals that need every WTO member to agree (1)
  • Advantage: they can go further, covering services, investment and other areas (1)
  • Disadvantage: they can cause trade diversion away from lower-cost non-members (1)
  • Disadvantage: many overlapping agreements with different rules of origin (a "noodle bowl") raise compliance costs; judgement on balance (1)

Short answer sa-1C1-01 · 1C.1 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain the infant industry argument for protection.

  • A new industry has higher average costs than established overseas firms because it has not yet reached a large scale (1)
  • Temporary protection lets it grow and gain economies of scale and experience, lowering costs (1)
  • Once it can compete, protection should be removed; the risk is that the industry never becomes competitive and protection becomes permanent (1)

Short answer sa-1C2-01 · 1C.2 · 4 marks · level 2

With the aid of a diagram, explain the effects of a tariff on domestic producers and consumers.

  • Diagram: domestic D and S, world price Pw and Pw+t, Q1 to Q4 (1)
  • The domestic price rises from Pw to Pw+t (1)
  • Domestic producers increase output from Q1 to Q2 and gain producer surplus (1)
  • Consumers pay more and buy less (Q4 to Q3), losing consumer surplus; imports fall (1)

Short answer sa-1D1-01 · 1D.1 1D.3 · 3 marks · level 1

Distinguish between economic growth and economic development.

  • Economic growth is an increase in real GDP, the volume of goods and services produced (1)
  • Economic development is a broader improvement in wellbeing: health, education, living standards and freedom (1)
  • Growth can occur without development if the gains are not shared or come with environmental damage; development is measured by indicators such as the HDI (1)

Short answer sa-1D2-01 · 1D.2 · 4 marks · level 3

Explain why the global distribution of income has changed since 1990.

  • Rapid growth in large emerging economies, especially China and India, has raised incomes for billions of people (1)
  • This has narrowed the gap between countries and greatly reduced extreme poverty (1)
  • Inequality within many countries has risen, as gains from globalisation and technology went mostly to owners of capital and skilled workers (1)
  • Many developing economies, especially in sub-Saharan Africa, have grown more slowly, so some gaps remain wide (1)

Short answer sa-1D4-01 · 1D.4 · 3 marks · level 1

Outline ONE feature that distinguishes emerging economies from developing economies.

  • A clear feature, e.g. emerging economies have fast growth driven by industrialisation and rising exports of manufactures (2)
  • A contrast with developing economies, e.g. which have low incomes and rely mostly on agriculture or commodity exports (1)

Short answer sa-1D5-01 · 1D.5 · 4 marks · level 2

Explain TWO reasons for differences in living standards between nations.

  • First reason explained, e.g. differences in human capital: education and health raise productivity and incomes (2)
  • Second reason explained, e.g. institutions and governance (rule of law, property rights, stable government) encourage investment; or access to capital and technology; or geography and resources (2)

Short answer sa-1D6-01 · 1D.6 1D.8 · 5 marks · level 3

Assess the effects of globalisation on the environment.

  • Negative: higher world output and trade raise energy use, greenhouse gas emissions and resource depletion (1)
  • Negative: production may shift to countries with weaker environmental rules ("pollution havens") (1)
  • Positive: globalisation spreads cleaner technology, such as cheaper solar panels and batteries (1)
  • Positive: global cooperation through agreements such as the Paris Agreement (1)
  • Judgement supported by reasoning, e.g. net effect depends on policy and technology (1)

Short answer sa-1E1-01 · 1E.1 · 5 marks · level 3

Analyse the influence of globalisation on the economy of a country other than Australia.

  • Names the economy and describes how it is integrated into the global economy, e.g. China's trade, foreign investment and WTO entry in 2001 (1)
  • Effect on growth, with evidence, e.g. export-led growth averaging close to 10 per cent a year for three decades (1)
  • Effect on development or poverty, e.g. hundreds of millions lifted out of extreme poverty (1)
  • A cost, e.g. rising inequality between regions, pollution, or dependence on exports and exposure to tariffs (1)
  • Links the effects back to globalisation rather than describing them separately (1)

Short answer sa-2A1-01 · 2A.1 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain why the direction of Australia's trade has shifted towards Asia.

  • Rapid industrialisation in Asia, especially China, has raised demand for Australian resources and energy (1)
  • Rising incomes in Asia increased demand for Australian services such as education and tourism, and for food (1)
  • Proximity, trade agreements (e.g. with China, Japan and Korea) and Britain's entry to the European Economic Community in 1973 shifted trade away from Europe (1)

Short answer sa-2B1-01 · 2B.1 2B.2 · 4 marks · level 2

Explain how the purchase of an Australian business by a foreign company affects Australia's balance of payments, now and in the future.

  • Now: the purchase is a capital inflow, recorded as a credit (foreign direct investment in Australia) in the financial account (1)
  • It adds to Australia's foreign liabilities (net foreign equity) (1)
  • In future: profits and dividends paid to the foreign owners are debits in net primary income (1)
  • This widens the current account deficit over time (1)

Short answer sa-2B3-01 · 2B.3 2A.2 · 4 marks · level 3

Analyse the relationship between the balance on the capital and financial account and the net primary income balance.

  • A surplus on the capital and financial account means foreign borrowing and investment flow into Australia (1)
  • These inflows add to Australia's foreign debt and foreign equity liabilities (1)
  • Servicing the liabilities requires interest and dividend payments overseas, debits in net primary income (1)
  • So persistent financial account surpluses lead to larger net primary income deficits, which widen the current account deficit and must themselves be financed: a cycle (1)

Short answer sa-2B4-01 · 2B.4 · 4 marks · level 2

Explain how a rise in the terms of trade affects Australia's balance of payments.

  • The terms of trade rise when export prices rise relative to import prices (1)
  • Export income rises, so the balance on goods and services improves (1)
  • The current account deficit narrows (or a surplus appears, as in 2019–20 to 2022–23) (1)
  • Profits of foreign-owned mining firms rise, so more dividends flow overseas, widening the net primary income deficit and partly offsetting the gain (1)

Short answer sa-2C2-01 · 2C.2 · 4 marks · level 2

With the aid of a diagram, explain how a rise in Australian interest rates relative to overseas rates affects the value of the Australian dollar.

  • Diagram of the market for A$ with demand shifting right (D1 to D2), and e1 to e2 (1)
  • Higher relative interest rates raise the return on Australian assets (1)
  • Foreign investors buy A$ to invest in Australia, so demand for A$ rises (1)
  • The exchange rate appreciates from e1 to e2 (1)

Short answer sa-2C4-01 · 2C.4 2C.5 · 4 marks · level 3

Compare a floating exchange rate with a fixed exchange rate.

  • Floating: the rate is set by supply and demand; the central bank intervenes rarely (1)
  • Fixed: the government or central bank sets the rate and defends it by buying or selling currency (1)
  • Floating: monetary policy can target inflation, and the rate absorbs shocks such as a fall in commodity prices (1)
  • Fixed: gives certainty to traders, but needs large reserves and can be attacked by speculators (1)

Short answer sa-2C6-01 · 2C.6 3C.3 · 5 marks · level 3

Analyse the effects of a sustained depreciation of the Australian dollar on the Australian economy.

  • Exports become cheaper overseas and imports dearer, raising international competitiveness (1)
  • Net exports and aggregate demand rise, lifting growth and employment (1)
  • Import prices rise, adding to inflation (imported inflation) (1)
  • The trade balance may worsen first and then improve (J-curve); the A$ cost of foreign-currency debt rises (1)
  • Valuation effects: foreign-currency assets are worth more in A$, which can lower net foreign liabilities (1)

Short answer sa-2D1-01 · 2D.1 2D.3 · 4 marks · level 2

Explain the effects of tariff reductions on Australian firms and consumers.

  • Consumers pay lower prices for imported goods and have more choice, raising real incomes (1)
  • Firms that use imported inputs gain lower costs, improving competitiveness (1)
  • Import-competing firms face more competition; some may close, causing structural unemployment (1)
  • Resources move to more efficient, export-oriented industries in the long run (1)

Short answer sa-2D4-01 · 2D.4 · 4 marks · level 3

Explain how protectionist policies of other countries affect Australia.

  • Tariffs or quotas on Australian goods reduce export volumes and prices, e.g. China's duties on Australian wine and barley from 2020 (1)
  • Export income and employment in the affected industries fall (1)
  • Subsidies overseas (e.g. EU and US farm support) lower world prices, hurting Australian exporters in third markets (1)
  • Trade wars between major economies slow world growth and divert goods to Australia, affecting Australian firms indirectly (1)

Short answer sa-3A3-01 · 3A.3 4C.2 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain how the multiplier affects the impact of a change in government spending on national income.

  • A rise in government spending is an injection that becomes income for others (1)
  • Recipients spend part of each extra dollar (the MPC), creating further rounds of income (1)
  • National income rises by a multiple of the original change: ΔY = ΔG × k, where k = 1 ÷ (1 − MPC) (1)

Short answer sa-3A5-01 · 3A.5 · 4 marks · level 3

Assess the arguments for and against increasing Australia's rate of economic growth.

  • For: higher growth creates jobs and lowers unemployment (1)
  • For: higher incomes and tax revenue fund services and raise living standards (1)
  • Against: growth above capacity raises inflation and widens the current account deficit (1)
  • Against: environmental costs and resource depletion; judgement about sustainable growth (1)

Short answer sa-3A6-01 · 3A.6 4E.1 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain how improvements in efficiency and technology increase aggregate supply.

  • New technology and better ways of working raise productivity: more output from the same inputs (1)
  • Unit costs fall, so firms can supply more at each price level (1)
  • Aggregate supply (and potential output) shifts right, allowing growth without extra inflation (1)

Short answer sa-3B3-01 · 3B.3 · 4 marks · level 2

Distinguish between cyclical and structural unemployment, using an example of each.

  • Cyclical: caused by a lack of aggregate demand in a downturn (1)
  • Example, e.g. retail or construction jobs lost in a recession (1)
  • Structural: caused by a mismatch between workers' skills or location and the jobs available, often from technology or changes in industry (1)
  • Example, e.g. car manufacturing workers after the closure of car plants in 2017 (1)

Short answer sa-3B4-01 · 3B.4 4A.7 · 3 marks · level 3

Explain the relationship between the NAIRU and inflation.

  • The NAIRU is the lowest unemployment rate that can be sustained without inflation rising (1)
  • Below the NAIRU, tight labour markets push wage growth up, raising costs and inflation (1)
  • Above it, spare capacity keeps wage growth and inflation subdued; structural reform can lower the NAIRU (1)

Short answer sa-3B6-01 · 3B.6 · 4 marks · level 1

Explain TWO costs of unemployment.

  • An economic cost explained, e.g. lost output and lower living standards, or higher welfare spending and lower tax revenue (2)
  • A social cost explained, e.g. poorer mental health, family stress, or loss of skills that makes it harder to find work later (2)

Short answer sa-3C1-01 · 3C.1 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain why the RBA pays close attention to underlying inflation as well as headline inflation.

  • Headline inflation can be swung by volatile or one-off price changes such as fuel, fruit or government rebates (1)
  • Underlying measures such as the trimmed mean remove extreme price changes, showing the trend in inflation (1)
  • Monetary policy works with lags, so the RBA responds to the persistent trend rather than temporary changes (1)

Short answer sa-3C3-01 · 3C.3 2C.6 · 4 marks · level 2

Explain how a depreciation of the Australian dollar can cause inflation.

  • A depreciation raises the A$ price of imported goods (1)
  • Consumer goods such as cars and electronics cost more, raising the CPI directly (1)
  • Imported inputs such as fuel and machinery cost more, raising firms' costs (cost inflation) (1)
  • Higher import prices can lift inflationary expectations and wage claims (1)

Short answer sa-3C4-01 · 3C.4 · 4 marks · level 2

Explain ONE positive and ONE negative effect of inflation.

  • A positive effect explained, e.g. moderate inflation lets real wages adjust and avoids the risks of deflation (2)
  • A negative effect explained, e.g. lower purchasing power for people on fixed incomes, uncertainty for investment, or loss of international competitiveness (2)

Short answer sa-3D3-01 · 3D.3 · 5 marks · level 3

Discuss the effects of a continued current account deficit on the Australian economy.

  • A CAD allows investment higher than national saving, funding growth (1)
  • It adds to foreign liabilities, so more income is paid overseas (net primary income), widening the CAD further (1)
  • A high level of foreign debt can raise the risk premium on borrowing and make the economy vulnerable to a loss of investor confidence (1)
  • It can lead to downward pressure on the exchange rate and constrain policy (1)
  • Balanced judgement, e.g. sustainable if borrowing funds productive investment (the Pitchford view) (1)

Short answer sa-3E3-01 · 3E.3 · 4 marks · level 2

Explain how the tax and transfer system reduces inequality in Australia.

  • Progressive income tax: higher earners pay a higher share of income (1)
  • Transfer payments, such as JobSeeker and the Age Pension, lift the incomes of low-income households (1)
  • The social wage (Medicare, public education, housing assistance) gives services regardless of income (1)
  • Together these make disposable income more equal than market income: the Gini coefficient falls (1)

Short answer sa-3E6-01 · 3E.6 · 4 marks · level 3

Assess the economic costs and benefits of inequality.

  • Benefit: differences in income create incentives to work, train and invest (1)
  • Cost: low-income households consume less, which can weaken aggregate demand (1)
  • Cost: lost potential, as people cannot afford education, and lower social cohesion (1)
  • Judgement: moderate inequality may encourage effort, but high inequality reduces growth and wellbeing (1)

Short answer sa-3F2-01 · 3F.2 3F.3 · 4 marks · level 2

Explain why the market fails to protect the natural environment.

  • Negative externalities: producers ignore external costs such as pollution, so output is too high (1)
  • Public goods and free riders: clean air and biodiversity are non-excludable, so they are underprovided (1)
  • Prices do not reflect the full social cost, so resources are overused (1)
  • Future generations are not represented in today's market decisions (1)

Short answer sa-4A7-01 · 4A.7 · 4 marks · level 3

Explain how a conflict can arise between the objectives of economic growth and environmental sustainability.

  • Faster growth means more production and energy use (1)
  • With current technology this raises emissions, pollution and resource use (1)
  • Policies to cut emissions, such as a price on carbon, raise costs and can slow growth in the short run (1)
  • Clean technology and structural change can reduce the conflict over time (1)

Short answer sa-4C2-01 · 4C.2 4C.1 · 4 marks · level 2

Explain how automatic stabilisers in the budget help reduce fluctuations in economic activity.

  • Automatic stabilisers are parts of the budget that change with the cycle without new decisions (1)
  • In a downturn, tax revenue falls and welfare payments rise, supporting incomes and AD (1)
  • In a boom, tax revenue rises and welfare payments fall, restraining AD (1)
  • So the budget moves towards deficit in downturns and surplus in booms, smoothing the cycle (1)

Short answer sa-4D2-01 · 4D.2 · 3 marks · level 3

Outline how the RBA implements a change in monetary policy.

  • The Monetary Policy Board decides the target for the cash rate and announces it (1)
  • The RBA manages the supply of money in the overnight market (exchange settlement balances), through open market operations and the interest rates on its standing facilities, so the cash rate settles at the target (1)
  • Banks pass the change through to lending and deposit rates (1)

Short answer sa-4D3-01 · 4D.3 · 5 marks · level 3

Analyse how an increase in the cash rate affects economic activity and the exchange rate.

  • Higher interest rates raise borrowing costs and repayments on variable loans (1)
  • Consumption and investment fall; asset prices and wealth may fall too (1)
  • Aggregate demand slows, reducing growth and inflation; unemployment may rise (1)
  • The higher return on Australian assets increases demand for A$, causing an appreciation (1)
  • The appreciation lowers import prices and net exports, adding to the effect on inflation; note the time lags (1)

Short answer sa-4E2-01 · 4E.2 4E.3 · 4 marks · level 2

Explain how competition policy can improve the efficiency of a product market.

  • Competition policy stops anti-competitive conduct such as cartels and the misuse of market power (1)
  • More competition forces firms to cut costs and prices (allocative and technical efficiency) (1)
  • Firms innovate to keep customers (dynamic efficiency) (1)
  • Consumers gain lower prices and more choice; resources go to their most valued uses (1)

Short answer sa-4F4-01 · 4F.4 · 4 marks · level 3

Discuss the arguments for and against decentralised wage determination.

  • For: pay can reflect each workplace's productivity, which can lift productivity growth (1)
  • For: flexibility for firms to adjust to their own conditions (1)
  • Against: workers with little bargaining power may fall behind, widening inequality (1)
  • Against: can lead to more industrial disputes; safety nets such as the NES and awards remain important (1)

Short answer sa-4G2-01 · 4G.2 4G.1 · 4 marks · level 2

Compare the use of regulation and market-based policies to reduce pollution.

  • Regulation sets rules, such as emission standards or bans, enforced by penalties (1)
  • Market-based policies, such as taxes or emissions trading, put a price on pollution (1)
  • Regulation gives a more certain result but can be costly because every firm must meet the same rule (1)
  • Market-based policies cut pollution where it is cheapest, but the outcome of a tax is less certain (1)

Short answer sa-4H1-01 · 4H.1 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain how time lags limit the effectiveness of fiscal policy.

  • Recognition lag: data are delayed, so problems are identified late (1)
  • Implementation lag: budget changes need planning and laws passed by Parliament (1)
  • Impact lag: spending and tax changes take time to affect demand, so policy may take effect when it is no longer needed (1)

Short answer sa-4I1-01 · 4I.1 4E.1 · 4 marks · level 3

Assess the effectiveness of microeconomic policies in promoting economic growth.

  • Microeconomic reform raises productivity and shifts aggregate supply right, lifting potential growth (1)
  • Evidence, e.g. tariff cuts, competition policy and financial deregulation contributed to strong productivity growth in the 1990s (1)
  • Limitations: benefits take years, there are short-term job losses, and reform can be politically difficult (1)
  • Judgement: effective for long-run growth, but slower productivity growth since the mid-2000s suggests the pace of reform matters (1)

Cause and effect chain y11-ch-P2c-frost · P2c.4 P2d.1 P2b.4 · 5 marks · level 1

Frost hits the coffee crop: Frost destroys a large share of the coffee crop in a major growing country.

  1. The supply of coffee ↓ Less coffee is available at every price
  2. The world price of coffee ↑ At the old price there is a shortage, so the price rises
  3. The quantity of coffee bought ↓ Buyers contract their purchases at the higher price
  4. Café prices for a cup of coffee ↑ Cafés pass on the higher cost of beans
  5. The demand for tea ↑ Tea is a substitute, so some coffee drinkers switch

Cause and effect chain y11-ch-P2i-excise · P2i.2 P2e.4 P5b.5 · 6 marks · level 2

A higher tobacco excise: The government raises the excise tax on each packet of cigarettes.

  1. Producers' costs per packet ↑ The tax is paid on each packet sold
  2. The supply of cigarettes ↓ Higher costs shift the supply curve up by the tax
  3. The price consumers pay ↑ Producers pass most of the tax on because demand is inelastic
  4. The quantity of cigarettes bought ↓ Some smokers cut back or quit, though only a little in the short run
  5. Tax revenue ↑ Demand is inelastic, so sales fall less than the tax per packet rises
  6. Smoking-related health costs over time ↓ Fewer people smoke, a smaller negative externality

Cause and effect chain y11-ch-P4c-rates · P4c.3 P4c.7 P4c.4 · 6 marks · level 2

Interest rates rise: Banks raise interest rates on loans and deposits.

  1. The cost of borrowing ↑ Interest is the price of credit
  2. Borrowing for housing and business investment ↓ Fewer projects and purchases are worth the higher cost
  3. The reward for saving ↑ Deposits earn more interest
  4. Mortgage repayments for existing borrowers ↑ Variable-rate loans cost more each month
  5. Housing affordability for new buyers ↓ Buyers can borrow less for the same repayment
  6. Household spending ↓ Borrowers have less left over and savers have a reason to save

Cause and effect chain y11-ch-P3a-thrift · P3a.6 P1d.5 P1e.6 · 6 marks · level 3

Everyone saves more: the paradox of thrift: Worried about the future, all households decide to save a larger share of their income.

  1. Household consumption ↓ Saving is income that is not spent
  2. Leakages from the circular flow ↑ Saving is a leakage
  3. Business sales ↓ Firms sell less to households
  4. Production and employment ↓ Firms cut output and jobs
  5. National income ↓ Lower production means lower incomes, so households may end up saving no more than before
  6. Business investment ↓ With weaker sales, firms need less new equipment, so spending falls further

Cause and effect chain y11-ch-P3c-tech · P3c.4 P3c.5 P2c.4 P1c.2 · 6 marks · level 2

A productivity breakthrough: A new technology lets factories produce much more with the same workers and machines.

  1. Productivity ↑ More output from the same inputs
  2. Costs of producing each unit ↓ Fixed costs are spread over more output
  3. Supply ↑ Firms will supply more at every price
  4. Prices of the goods ↓ The supply curve shifts right
  5. Real wages and living standards ↑ Higher output per worker allows higher real incomes
  6. The economy's production possibilities ↑ The frontier shifts outwards

Cause and effect chain y11-ch-P1e-exports · P1e.6 P1e.7 P1d.5 P6a.4 · 6 marks · level 2

A recession overseas: Australia's major trading partners fall into recession.

  1. Demand for Australian exports ↓ Overseas buyers spend less
  2. Injections into the circular flow ↓ Exports are an injection
  3. Real GDP growth in Australia ↓ Lower spending means lower production
  4. Unemployment ↑ Firms need fewer workers
  5. Government tax revenue ↓ Incomes and profits fall
  6. Government spending on unemployment benefits ↑ More people need income support

Compare perfect competition and monopoly.

Perfect competitionMonopoly
Number of sellers(given) Very manyOne
Type of productIdentical (homogeneous)Unique, with no close substitutes
Barriers to entryNoneVery high
Pricing powerPrice taker: sells at the market pricePrice maker: sets its own price
ExampleA market for one variety of wheatThe only water supplier in a city

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P2b-01 · P2b.4 P2d.1 P2d.2 · 4 marks · level 1

A heatwave hits Sydney over summer. Draw a demand and supply diagram to show the effect on the market for ice cream.

Hot weather changes tastes in favour of ice cream, so demand increases: the demand curve shifts right from D1 to D2. At the old price there is a shortage, so the price rises from P1 to P2 and the quantity bought and sold rises from Q1 to Q2.

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P2b-02 · P2b.4 P2d.1 · 4 marks · level 2

Household incomes fall during a recession. Draw a demand and supply diagram to show the effect on the market for overseas holidays (a normal good).

Lower incomes reduce demand for a normal good, so the demand curve shifts left from D1 to D2. The price falls from P1 to P2 and the quantity falls from Q1 to Q2.

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P2b-03 · P2b.4 P2d.2 · 4 marks · level 2

Strong population growth adds many new households to a city. Draw a demand and supply diagram to show the effect on the market for rental housing.

More households increase demand for rental housing: the demand curve shifts right from D1 to D2. Rents rise from P1 to P2 and the quantity of rental housing rises from Q1 to Q2 (only a little in the short run, because supply is inelastic).

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P2c-01 · P2c.4 P2d.1 P2d.2 · 4 marks · level 1

Frost destroys a large part of the coffee crop in Brazil, a major producer. Draw a demand and supply diagram to show the effect on the world market for coffee.

Less coffee is available at every price, so supply decreases: the supply curve shifts left from S1 to S2. The price of coffee rises from P1 to P2 and the quantity falls from Q1 to Q2.

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P2c-02 · P2c.4 P2d.2 · 4 marks · level 1

A new manufacturing technology lowers the cost of making solar panels. Draw a demand and supply diagram to show the effect on the market for solar panels.

Lower production costs mean firms will supply more at every price: supply increases and the curve shifts right from S1 to S2. The price falls from P1 to P2 and the quantity rises from Q1 to Q2.

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P2c-03 · P2c.4 P2d.1 · 4 marks · level 2

Cattle farmers expect beef prices to rise strongly next year, so they hold back cattle from sale. Draw a demand and supply diagram to show the effect on the market for beef this year.

Expected future prices are a non-price factor: holding stock back reduces supply now, so the supply curve shifts left from S1 to S2. The price rises from P1 to P2 and the quantity sold falls from Q1 to Q2.

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P2b-04 · P2b.3 P2b.5 · 4 marks · level 1

A pizza shop cuts the price of its pizzas. Nothing else changes. Draw a diagram to show the effect on the quantity of pizzas demanded.

A change in the good's own price is a movement along the demand curve, not a shift. The price falls from P1 to P2, so the quantity demanded expands from Q1 to Q2 (point A to point B): an expansion of demand.

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P2c-04 · P2c.3 P2c.5 · 4 marks · level 1

The price of strawberries rises. Nothing else changes. Draw a diagram to show the effect on the quantity of strawberries supplied.

The higher price is a movement along the supply curve: the quantity supplied expands from Q1 to Q2 as the price rises from P1 to P2 (point A to point B), because growing strawberries is now more profitable.

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P2i-01 · P2i.3 P2i.2 · 4 marks · level 2

To make renting more affordable, the government sets a maximum rent below the equilibrium rent. Draw a diagram to show the effect on the rental market.

The binding ceiling Pmax is below the equilibrium price Pe. At Pmax the quantity demanded (Qd) is greater than the quantity supplied (Qs), so there is a shortage of rental housing. Fewer homes are rented than at equilibrium (Qe), and landlords may let homes run down or sell them.

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P2i-02 · P2i.3 P2i.2 · 4 marks · level 2

The government guarantees wheat farmers a minimum price above the equilibrium price. Draw a diagram to show the effect on the market for wheat.

The binding floor Pmin is above the equilibrium price Pe. At Pmin the quantity supplied (Qs) exceeds the quantity demanded (Qd): a surplus of wheat, which the government may have to buy and store.

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P2i-03 · P2i.3 P5b.5 P2i.2 · 4 marks · level 2

The government increases the excise tax on each packet of cigarettes. Draw a diagram to show the effect on the market for cigarettes.

The tax raises producers' costs, so supply decreases: the supply curve shifts up by the amount of the tax, from S1 to S2. The price consumers pay rises from P1 to P2 and the quantity falls from Q1 to Q2. Producers keep P3 (P2 less the tax). The shaded rectangle is the tax revenue (tax × Q2). Because demand is inelastic, consumers bear most of the tax.

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P2i-04 · P2i.3 P2i.2 P2h.3 · 4 marks · level 2

The government places a tax of 50 cents on each bottle of sugary drink. Draw a diagram to show the effect, and shade the tax revenue.

Supply shifts up by the tax from S1 to S2. The price rises from P1 to P2 and the quantity falls from Q1 to Q2. Consumers pay P2, producers keep P3 (P2 − 50 cents), and the government's revenue is the tax per bottle × Q2 (the shaded rectangle). The red triangle is the deadweight loss, the consumption and production that no longer take place.

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P2h-01 · P2h.4 P2h.3 · 4 marks · level 2

Coal-fired power stations release emissions that harm people living nearby and the climate. Draw a diagram to show this negative externality in production.

The market supply curve reflects only private costs (MPC). The marginal social cost (MSC) is higher, because of the cost of emissions to others. The market produces Q_m, where D (MPB) = MPC, more than the efficient quantity Qs, where D = MSC. The shaded triangle is the deadweight loss from over-production.

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P2h-02 · P2h.4 P2h.3 · 4 marks · level 2

When a person is vaccinated, others also benefit because the disease spreads less. Draw a diagram to show this positive externality in consumption.

The demand curve shows private benefits only (D, MPB). The marginal social benefit (MSB) is higher, because of the benefit to others. The market settles at Q_m, where MPB = S, less than the efficient quantity Qs, where MSB = MSC (S). The shaded triangle is the welfare lost through under-consumption; a subsidy or free provision could raise output to Qs.

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P2h-03 · P2h.4 P2h.3 P2i.2 · 4 marks · level 3

Education is a merit good. Draw a diagram to show why, left to the market, too little education would be consumed, and explain ONE policy to correct this.

Society gains extra benefits from education, so MSB lies above private demand (D, MPB). The market produces Q_m, less than the efficient Qs where MSB = S (MSC). A subsidy that lowers the price, or free provision of schooling, raises consumption towards Qs and removes the welfare loss (shaded).

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P2e-01 · P2e.1 P2e.2 · 4 marks · level 1

Draw a relatively elastic demand curve and show the effect of a small rise in price on the quantity demanded.

A relatively elastic demand curve is flat. A small rise in price from P1 to P2 causes a large fall in quantity demanded from Q1 to Q2, so the percentage change in quantity is larger than the percentage change in price (PED > 1).

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P2e-02 · P2e.1 P2e.2 P2e.4 · 4 marks · level 1

Draw a demand curve for petrol, a good with price inelastic demand in the short run, and show the effect of a rise in its price.

The demand curve is steep. A large rise in price from P1 to P2 causes only a small fall in quantity demanded from Q1 to Q2 (PED < 1), because petrol has few close substitutes in the short run.

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P2e-03 · P2e.1 · 4 marks · level 1

Draw a perfectly inelastic demand curve.

A vertical demand curve at Q1: the quantity demanded does not change whatever the price (PED = 0).

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P2e-04 · P2e.1 P2e.2 · 4 marks · level 2

Draw a unit elastic demand curve and show a rise in price from P1 to P2.

A unit elastic demand curve is a curve (a rectangular hyperbola) along which the percentage change in quantity demanded always equals the percentage change in price (PED = 1), so total revenue does not change when the price changes.

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P2f-01 · P2f.1 P2f.2 P2f.3 · 4 marks · level 2

Draw a supply curve for new housing in the short run, and show the effect of a rise in house prices on the quantity supplied.

Supply is inelastic in the short run (a steep curve) because building takes time and land and approvals are limited. A large rise in price from P1 to P2 brings only a small rise in quantity supplied from Q1 to Q2 (PES < 1).

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P2f-02 · P2f.1 P2f.2 P2f.3 · 4 marks · level 2

A clothing maker has plenty of spare capacity. Draw its supply curve for T-shirts and show the effect of a small rise in price.

Supply is elastic (a flat curve), because spare capacity lets the firm raise output quickly. A small rise in price from P1 to P2 brings a large rise in quantity supplied from Q1 to Q2 (PES > 1).

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P4c-01 · P4c.6 P4c.4 · 4 marks · level 2

Business confidence rises and many firms plan to borrow to expand. Draw a loanable funds diagram to show the effect on interest rates.

More investment plans increase the demand for loanable funds: the demand curve shifts right from D1 to D2. The interest rate rises from r1 to r2 and the quantity of funds borrowed and lent rises from Q1 to Q2.

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P4c-02 · P4c.6 P4c.5 · 4 marks · level 2

Households decide to save a larger share of their income. Draw a loanable funds diagram to show the effect on interest rates.

More saving increases the supply of loanable funds: the supply curve shifts right from S1 to S2. The interest rate falls from r1 to r2 and the quantity of funds lent rises from Q1 to Q2.

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P4c-03 · P4c.6 P4c.5 · 4 marks · level 3

Foreign investors become worried about risk and withdraw funds from Australia. Draw a loanable funds diagram to show the effect on interest rates in Australia.

Less foreign investment reduces the supply of loanable funds: the supply curve shifts left from S1 to S2. The interest rate rises from r1 to r2 and the quantity of funds lent falls from Q1 to Q2.

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P3c-01 · P3c.6 P3c.5 · 4 marks · level 2

Draw a long-run average cost (LRAC) curve. Label economies of scale, diseconomies of scale and the technical optimum.

The LRAC curve is U-shaped. While it falls, the firm enjoys economies of scale (e.g. bulk buying, specialisation, better technology). Its lowest point is the technical optimum (output Q*). Beyond it, diseconomies of scale (e.g. coordination and communication problems) push average costs up.

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P1e-01 · P1e.4 · 4 marks · level 1

Draw a diagram of the business cycle. Label the trend, a peak, a trough, a contraction and an expansion.

Real GDP rises over time along its trend, but fluctuates around it. A peak is a high point, followed by a contraction (downturn) to a trough, the low point, then an expansion (upturn) to the next peak.

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P1c-01 · P1c.2 P1c.5 · 4 marks · level 1

An economy's workers become more skilled and its technology improves. Draw a production possibilities frontier to show the effect.

Better skills and technology increase the economy's productive capacity, so the frontier shifts outwards from PPF1 to PPF2: the economy can now produce more of both goods.

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P1c-02 · P1c.5 · 4 marks · level 1

An economy is in recession and many workers are unemployed. Draw a production possibilities frontier and mark the economy's position.

The economy is at a point such as A inside its frontier: it could produce more of both goods with the same resources, because labour and capital are unemployed or underemployed.

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P1c-03 · P1c.1 P1c.5 · 4 marks · level 2

Draw a production possibilities frontier. Mark two efficient combinations (A and B), an inefficient combination (C) and an unattainable combination (D).

A and B lie on the frontier: all resources are used efficiently. C lies inside it: some resources are unemployed. D lies outside it: it cannot be produced with current resources and technology. Moving from A to B has an opportunity cost: less of the capital good for more of the consumer good.

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P3d-01 · P3d.3 · 4 marks · level 2

Self-service checkouts and AI reduce the number of checkout workers supermarkets need. Draw a labour market diagram to show the effect on the market for checkout workers.

Automation reduces the demand for checkout workers: the demand curve shifts left from D1 to D2. The wage falls from W1 to W2 and employment falls from Q1 to Q2.

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P3b-01 · P3b.1 P3b.2 · 4 marks · level 2

The government makes childcare cheaper and more widely available. Draw a labour market diagram to show the effect on the supply of labour.

Lower childcare costs encourage more parents to work, so the supply of labour increases: the supply curve shifts right from S1 to S2. The wage falls from W1 to W2 (or rises more slowly) and employment rises from Q1 to Q2.

Draw a diagram y11-dr-P3d-02 · P3d.1 P2i.3 · 4 marks · level 2

A minimum wage is set above the equilibrium wage in the market for low-skilled labour. Draw a diagram to show the effect.

At Wmin, above the equilibrium wage We, the quantity of labour supplied (Qs) is greater than the quantity demanded (Qd). The gap is unemployment. Workers who keep their jobs earn more, but fewer are employed than at equilibrium (Qe).

Spot the error y11-er-P2b-demand · P2b.3 P2b.4 · 4 marks · level 1

This paragraph answers: "Explain the difference between a change in quantity demanded and a change in demand."

A fall in the price of a good causes an increase in demand an expansion in the quantity demanded, which is shown as a movement along the demand curve. A change in a non-price factor, such as income, shifts the whole demand curve. For example, if incomes rise, the demand for a normal good decreases increases and the demand curve shifts to the left right. If the price of a substitute falls, consumers switch to the substitute, so the demand for the original good increases decreases.

Spot the error y11-er-P2e-ped · P2e.3 P2e.5 · 3 marks · level 2

This paragraph answers: "A bakery raises the price of a loaf from $5 to $6 and sales fall from 1,000 to 900 loaves a week. Calculate and explain the price elasticity of demand."

The price rises by $1, or 10 per cent $1, or 20 per cent and the quantity demanded falls by 10 per cent. Using the percentage method, PED = % change in quantity demanded ÷ % change in price = 10 ÷ 20 = 0.5. Because PED is greater less than 1, demand is relatively inelastic. This means the bakery's total revenue falls rises when it raises its price: it rises from $5,000 to $5,400 a week.

Spot the error y11-er-P2i-ceiling · P2i.3 · 3 marks · level 2

This paragraph answers: "Explain the effects of a binding price ceiling."

A binding price ceiling is set above below the equilibrium price. At this lower price the quantity demanded expands and the quantity supplied expands contracts. As a result there is a surplus shortage of the good. Some buyers who are willing to pay more cannot buy the good, so queues, waiting lists or black markets may develop.

Spot the error y11-er-P2h-tax · P2h.3 P2i.2 · 3 marks · level 2

This paragraph answers: "Explain how a tax can correct a negative externality in production."

When a firm pollutes, the marginal social cost of production is less greater than its marginal private cost, because others bear part of the cost. The market therefore produces less more than the socially efficient quantity. A tax equal to the external cost raises the firm's costs, so supply increases decreases. The price rises and the quantity falls towards the efficient level, where marginal social cost equals marginal social benefit.

Spot the error y11-er-P1e-cycle · P1e.4 P1e.5 · 3 marks · level 1

This paragraph answers: "Describe the stages of the business cycle."

During an expansion, real GDP grows and unemployment rises falls. The highest point of the cycle is the trough peak, when the economy is close to full capacity and inflation is likely to be rising. A contraction follows, during which growth slows or real GDP falls and unemployment rises. The lowest point of the cycle is the trough. Two consecutive quarters of rising falling real GDP are often called a recession.

Spot the error y11-er-P1c-ppf · P1c.2 P1c.4 P1c.5 · 4 marks · level 2

This paragraph answers: "Explain what a production possibilities frontier shows."

A production possibilities frontier shows the maximum combinations of two goods an economy can produce with its unlimited given resources and technology. A point on the frontier shows that resources are used efficiently. A point outside inside the frontier shows unemployed resources. Moving along the frontier to produce more of one good means producing less of the other: this is the profit opportunity cost of the choice. An improvement in technology shifts the frontier inwards outwards.

Spot the error y11-er-P4c-funds · P4c.5 P4c.6 P4c.7 · 3 marks · level 2

This paragraph answers: "Explain how a rise in household saving affects interest rates."

When households save more, the demand for supply of loanable funds increases. The supply curve shifts to the right, so the interest rate rises falls and the quantity of funds borrowed and lent rises. Lower interest rates reduce the cost of credit, so businesses are more likely to borrow to invest. Lower mortgage rates also make housing less more affordable for new borrowers, although cheaper credit can push up house prices over time.

Spot the error y11-er-P1d-flow · P1d.2 P1d.4 · 3 marks · level 2

This paragraph answers: "Explain when the circular flow of income is in equilibrium."

Leakages are income that is not passed on as spending on domestic goods and services: savings, taxation and exports imports. Injections are spending that comes from outside the household–business flow: investment, government spending and exports. The economy is in equilibrium when S + T + X S + T + M equals I + G + X. If injections are greater than leakages, national income will fall rise until the two are equal again.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P1a-01 · P1a.2 · 1 marks · level 1

The basic economic problem arises because

  1. resources are unlimited but people's wants are limited
  2. people's wants are unlimited but the resources to satisfy them are scarce
  3. governments control most of the economy's resources
  4. prices are too high for many consumers

Why B: Scarcity, unlimited wants and limited resources, forces every economy to make choices. A reverses the problem; C and D describe particular situations, not the basic problem.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P1a-02 · P1a.4 · 1 marks · level 1

Kate could spend Saturday at the beach or work a shift that pays $160. She chooses to work. What is the opportunity cost of working?

  1. The $160 she earns
  2. The cost of travelling to work
  3. Nothing, because she is paid
  4. The day at the beach she gives up

Why D: Opportunity cost is the next best alternative given up when a choice is made, here the day at the beach. The $160 is the benefit of working, not its cost.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P1a-03 · P1a.3 · 1 marks · level 1

Which of the following is an economic decision made by a producer?

  1. A family deciding whether to buy a new car
  2. A bakery deciding how many loaves to bake each morning
  3. The Federal Government deciding how much to spend on hospitals
  4. A student deciding whether to save her part-time earnings

Why B: Producers (businesses) decide what and how much to produce. A and D are consumer decisions; C is a government decision.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P1a-04 · P1a.5 · 1 marks · level 2

A café owner is deciding whether to stay open one extra hour each evening. The extra hour would bring in $300 of sales and add $220 to wages and other costs. Using the cost–benefit principle at the margin, she should

  1. stay open, because the marginal benefit ($300) is greater than the marginal cost ($220)
  2. close, because the café already makes a profit during the day
  3. stay open only if the café's total revenue for the day is above its total cost
  4. close, because wages are a cost of production

Why A: A decision at the margin compares the extra benefit with the extra cost of that one choice. The extra hour adds $80 to profit, so it is worth doing; the rest of the day's results (B, C) do not change that.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P1a-05 · P1a.6 · 1 marks · level 2

A council gives a $50 rebate to households that buy a water-saving shower head. Some of these households then take longer showers. This is an example of

  1. an intended consequence of a disincentive
  2. a market reaching equilibrium
  3. the opportunity cost of saving water
  4. an unintended consequence of an incentive

Why D: The rebate is an incentive to save water; longer showers are a response the council did not intend, which reduces the water saved.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P1a-06 · P1a.9 · 1 marks · level 2

Many people keep the superannuation fund their employer chose for them, even when another fund would charge lower fees and leave them better off. Behavioural economists explain this mainly as

  1. rational, self-interested behaviour
  2. the law of diminishing marginal utility
  3. status quo (default) bias
  4. a response to perfect information

Why C: People tend to stick with the default option even when switching would benefit them, which challenges the assumption that people always act rationally in their own interest.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P1a-07 · P1a.10 · 1 marks · level 1

Which of the following is a macroeconomic issue?

  1. The price of avocados at a local market
  2. The wage paid to a nurse
  3. Australia's unemployment rate
  4. A firm's decision to hire one more worker

Why C: Macroeconomics studies the economy as a whole, such as total output, unemployment and inflation. A, B and D concern individual markets, workers or firms (microeconomics).

Multiple choice y11-mc-P1a-08 · P1a.8 · 1 marks · level 2

Economic models such as demand and supply often assume ceteris paribus. This means that

  1. the government does not intervene in markets
  2. consumers have perfect information
  3. all other factors are held constant while one factor changes
  4. prices never change

Why C: Ceteris paribus ("other things equal") lets a model show the effect of one change at a time. It is a simplifying assumption, not a claim that nothing else ever changes.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P1b-01 · P1b.2 · 1 marks · level 1

Which factor income is the reward for entrepreneurship?

  1. Rent
  2. Wages
  3. Interest
  4. Profit

Why D: Entrepreneurs organise the other factors and take the risk, and their reward is profit. Rent is paid for land, wages for labour and interest for capital.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P1b-02 · P1b.1 · 1 marks · level 1

A tractor used on a farm is an example of which factor of production?

  1. Land (natural resources)
  2. Labour
  3. Capital
  4. Entrepreneurship

Why C: Capital is a man-made good used to produce other goods and services. The soil the tractor works is land.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P1b-03 · P1b.4 · 1 marks · level 2

In a planned (command) economy, the question "What to produce?" is mainly answered by

  1. consumers, through the price mechanism
  2. government planners
  3. tradition and custom
  4. competition between private firms

Why B: In a planned economy the state owns most resources and a central authority decides what is produced. A and D describe a free market economy; C describes a pre-industrial (traditional) economy.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P1b-04 · P1b.4 · 1 marks · level 2

Australia is best described as a mixed market economy because

  1. the government owns all the major industries
  2. prices are set by a central planning authority
  3. production is decided mainly by tradition
  4. most resources are allocated by markets, while the government provides some goods and services and regulates markets

Why D: A mixed economy combines market allocation with a significant role for government, for example health, education, welfare and regulation in Australia.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P1b-05 · P1b.5 · 1 marks · level 1

When money acts as a measure of value (unit of account), it

  1. is accepted in exchange for goods and services
  2. allows the values of different goods and services to be compared in the same units
  3. can be saved and spent later
  4. is backed by gold

Why B: Prices in dollars let us compare the value of very different things. A is money as a medium of exchange; C is money as a store of value.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P1b-06 · P1b.3 · 1 marks · level 1

The key economic question "How to distribute production?" is about

  1. which goods and services to produce
  2. which methods and combinations of resources to use
  3. who receives the goods and services that are produced
  4. what quantity of each good to produce

Why C: Distribution is about who gets the output. A is "What to produce?", B is "How to produce?" and D is "How much to produce?".

Multiple choice y11-mc-P1c-01 · P1c.4 P1c.1 · 1 marks · level 2
