The production possibilities schedule shows an economy that produces only food and machines. What is the opportunity cost of increasing machine production from 20 to 30 units?

  1. 10 tonnes of food
  2. 20 tonnes of food
  3. 55 tonnes of food
  4. 10 machines

Why B: Moving from C to D, food falls from 75 to 55 tonnes, so the 10 extra machines cost 20 tonnes of food (2 tonnes per machine).

Multiple choice y11-mc-P1c-02 · P1c.5 · 1 marks · level 1

A point inside an economy's production possibilities frontier shows

  1. a combination of goods that cannot be produced with current resources
  2. full and efficient use of all resources
  3. unemployed or underemployed resources
  4. economic growth

Why C: Inside the frontier the economy could produce more of one good without giving up any of the other, so some resources are idle or not used efficiently. A describes a point outside the frontier.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P1c-03 · P1c.2 P1c.5 · 1 marks · level 2

Which of the following would shift an economy's production possibilities frontier outwards?

  1. A fall in unemployment
  2. A new technology that raises productivity in all industries
  3. Moving resources from making consumer goods to making capital goods
  4. A recession

Why B: Better technology lets the same resources produce more of both goods. A moves the economy from inside the frontier towards it; C is a movement along the frontier; D moves the economy inside it.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P1c-04 · P1c.3 · 1 marks · level 2

Which of the following is an assumption of the production possibilities model?

  1. Prices are set by the government
  2. Resources can grow without limit during the period
  3. The economy produces two goods with a fixed quantity of resources and given technology
  4. Consumers decide what is produced

Why C: The model holds resources and technology fixed and assumes they are fully used, so it can show the trade-off between two goods.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P1c-05 · P1c.6 · 1 marks · level 2

An economy decides to produce more capital goods and fewer consumer goods this year. The most likely result is

  1. no opportunity cost, because capital goods are useful
  2. a point inside the production possibilities frontier
  3. a production possibilities frontier that shifts inwards
  4. lower consumption now but a frontier that can shift outwards in the future

Why D: The opportunity cost is consumer goods today. More machinery and infrastructure raise future productive capacity, so the frontier can shift out later.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P1d-01 · P1d.2 · 1 marks · level 1

Which of the following is an injection into the circular flow of income?

  1. Savings
  2. Taxation
  3. Exports
  4. Imports

Why C: Exports bring spending into the economy from overseas. Savings, taxation and imports are leakages from the circular flow.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P1d-02 · P1d.4 · 1 marks · level 2

In an economy, S = $60b, T = $90b, M = $50b, I = $70b, G = $85b and X = $55b. This economy is

  1. in equilibrium
  2. in disequilibrium, and national income will fall
  3. in disequilibrium, and national income will rise
  4. in equilibrium, because government spending is less than taxation

Why C: Leakages S + T + M = $200b and injections I + G + X = $210b. Injections exceed leakages, so spending and income rise until the two are equal.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P1d-03 · P1d.1 · 1 marks · level 1

In the circular flow of income, households provide businesses with

  1. goods and services
  2. taxes
  3. profits
  4. the factors of production: land, labour, capital and enterprise

Why D: Households own the factors of production and sell their services to businesses in return for factor incomes. Businesses provide goods and services to households.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P1d-04 · P1d.5 · 1 marks · level 2

Households decide to save a larger share of their income. Other things equal, the circular flow model predicts that

  1. leakages fall and national income rises
  2. injections rise and national income rises
  3. leakages rise and national income falls
  4. there is no effect, because savings stay in the economy

Why C: Saving is a leakage: money saved is not spent on goods and services, so spending and income fall unless the savings are matched by more investment.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P1d-05 · P1d.1 · 1 marks · level 2

Which sector's main role in the circular flow of income is to channel savings to businesses for investment?

  1. The international sector
  2. The government sector
  3. The financial sector
  4. The household sector

Why C: Banks, super funds and other financial institutions take in savings and lend or invest them, linking a leakage (S) with an injection (I).

Multiple choice y11-mc-P1e-01 · P1e.4 · 1 marks · level 1

The stage of the business cycle at which real GDP stops rising and begins to fall is the

  1. trough
  2. upturn
  3. expansion
  4. peak

Why D: The peak is the high point of the cycle, after which a contraction begins. The trough is the low point, after which an expansion (upturn) begins.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P1e-02 · P1e.5 · 1 marks · level 2

During the contraction (downturn) phase of the business cycle, we would usually expect

  1. unemployment to fall and inflation to rise
  2. both unemployment and inflation to fall
  3. unemployment to rise and inflationary pressure to ease
  4. business profits to rise

Why C: Spending and production slow, so firms need fewer workers and have less ability to raise prices.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P1e-03 · P1e.1 · 1 marks · level 2

An economy's real GDP rose from $2,000 billion to $2,050 billion over a year. Its rate of economic growth was

  1. 2.5 per cent
  2. 5.0 per cent
  3. 50 per cent
  4. 2.4 per cent

Why A: Growth = (2,050 − 2,000) ÷ 2,000 × 100 = 2.5 per cent. D divides by the new value instead of the original one.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P1e-04 · P1e.6 · 1 marks · level 2

Which of the following is most likely to start a downturn in Australia's business cycle?

  1. A rise in business confidence
  2. A large fall in exports because major trading partners are in recession
  3. Lower interest rates
  4. Rising household wealth

Why B: Exports are an injection; a large fall reduces spending, production and income. A, C and D would usually lift spending.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P1e-05 · P1e.2 · 1 marks · level 1

The main reason economies pursue higher GDP is to

  1. reduce exports
  2. increase inflation
  3. raise material living standards and create jobs
  4. reduce the size of government

Why C: Higher output means more goods and services and more income per person, and growing production needs more workers.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P1e-06 · P1e.3 P1e.1 · 1 marks · level 1

Which statement about Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander businesses is correct?

  1. They operate only in the arts
  2. They are not counted in Australia's GDP
  3. They add to GDP through the goods and services they produce, the people they employ and the incomes they pay, across many industries
  4. They are all owned by government

Why C: Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander businesses operate in industries such as construction, professional services, tourism, retail, the arts and native foods, and their output is part of GDP.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P1e-07 · P1e.7 · 1 marks · level 2

During a contraction in the business cycle, the Federal Government's budget usually moves towards deficit because

  1. tax revenue rises and spending on unemployment benefits falls
  2. neither tax revenue nor spending changes
  3. both tax revenue and spending rise
  4. tax revenue falls and spending on unemployment benefits rises

Why D: Lower incomes and profits mean less tax collected, while more people qualify for income support.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2a-01 · P2a.1 · 1 marks · level 2

When a good becomes scarce and its price rises, the higher price gives firms a reason to produce more of it. This is the price mechanism's

  1. signalling function
  2. redistribution function
  3. rationing function
  4. incentive function

Why D: The higher price makes production more profitable, an incentive to supply more. The signalling function is the price change telling people the good has become scarcer; rationing is limiting who buys it.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2a-02 · P2a.1 · 1 marks · level 2

Concert tickets sell out at a high price, so only people willing and able to pay that price can attend. This shows the price mechanism's

  1. signalling function
  2. incentive function
  3. rationing function
  4. stabilisation function

Why C: A higher price rations a scarce good among buyers: those who will not or cannot pay that price go without.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2a-03 · P2a.2 · 1 marks · level 2

Markets work better when property rights are clearly defined and enforced because

  1. firms no longer need to compete
  2. the government can then set all prices
  3. owners can be confident they will keep the rewards of their effort and investment
  4. consumers no longer need information

Why C: Secure property rights and enforceable contracts give people the confidence to invest, produce and trade.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2a-04 · P2a.3 · 1 marks · level 1

The market in which businesses hire workers is an example of

  1. a product market
  2. a foreign exchange market
  3. a factor market
  4. an equity market

Why C: Labour is a factor of production, so it is traded in a factor market. Goods and services are traded in product markets.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2b-01 · P2b.2 · 1 marks · level 1

The law of demand states that, other things equal,

  1. as income rises, demand rises
  2. as the price of a good rises, the quantity demanded rises
  3. as the price of a good rises, the quantity demanded falls
  4. as supply rises, demand rises

Why C: The law of demand is the inverse relationship between a good's own price and the quantity demanded, which gives the demand curve its downward slope.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2b-02 · P2b.3 P2b.4 · 1 marks · level 2

A fall in the price of coffee leads to

  1. an increase in demand for coffee: the demand curve shifts right
  2. a contraction in the quantity of coffee demanded
  3. a decrease in demand for coffee
  4. an expansion in the quantity of coffee demanded: a movement along the demand curve

Why D: A change in the good's own price causes a movement along its demand curve. Only a change in a non-price factor shifts the curve.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2b-03 · P2b.4 · 1 marks · level 2

Which of the following would increase the demand for electric cars?

  1. A fall in the price of electric cars
  2. A rise in the price of petrol
  3. A rise in the price of electricity
  4. A fall in consumers' incomes

Why B: Dearer petrol makes petrol cars (substitutes for electric cars) more costly to run, so demand for electric cars increases. A is a movement along the curve; C raises the cost of a complement; D reduces demand for a normal good.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2b-04 · P2b.4 · 1 marks · level 2

Tea and coffee are substitutes. If the price of tea rises, the demand for coffee will

  1. expand along the demand curve for coffee
  2. decrease: the demand curve for coffee shifts to the left
  3. increase: the demand curve for coffee shifts to the right
  4. stay the same

Why C: Some tea drinkers switch to coffee, so more coffee is demanded at every price of coffee.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2b-05 · P2b.1 · 1 marks · level 1

Effective demand is

  1. the desire for a good or service
  2. demand from businesses only
  3. the total quantity supplied in a market
  4. the desire for a good or service backed by the ability and willingness to pay

Why D: Wanting something is not enough; demand only counts in a market when the buyer can and will pay.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2c-01 · P2c.2 · 1 marks · level 1

The supply curve slopes upwards because

  1. at higher prices production is more profitable, so firms supply more
  2. consumers buy more at higher prices
  3. costs of production fall as prices rise
  4. the government requires firms to produce more

Why A: A higher price covers the higher costs of extra output and gives firms an incentive to produce more (the law of supply).

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2c-05 · P2c.1 · 1 marks · level 1

The market supply of a good is

  1. the quantity one firm will supply at each price
  2. the quantity consumers want to buy at each price
  3. the sum of the quantities all firms in the market will supply at each price
  4. the total stock of the good held in warehouses

Why C: Market supply adds together every firm's individual supply at each price. Supply is effective only when firms are willing and able to produce at that price.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2c-02 · P2c.4 · 1 marks · level 2

Which of the following would decrease the supply of wheat?

  1. A new, higher-yielding seed variety
  2. A drought that destroys part of the crop
  3. A fall in the price of fertiliser
  4. More farmers deciding to grow wheat

Why B: The drought reduces the quantity of wheat available at every price, so supply shifts left. A, C and D all increase supply.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2c-03 · P2c.3 · 1 marks · level 2

A rise in the price of strawberries causes

  1. an increase in the supply of strawberries
  2. the supply curve of strawberries to shift right
  3. a decrease in the supply of strawberries
  4. an expansion in the quantity of strawberries supplied

Why D: A change in the good's own price is a movement along the supply curve (an expansion when the price rises), not a shift.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2c-04 · P2c.4 · 1 marks · level 2

Cattle farmers expect the price of beef to rise sharply next year, so they hold back cattle from sale now. The supply of beef this year will

  1. increase
  2. not change
  3. expand along the supply curve
  4. decrease

Why D: Expected future prices are a non-price factor: holding stock back for a better price later reduces supply now, shifting the supply curve left.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2d-01 · P2d.1 · 1 marks · level 1

Demand for a good increases while supply does not change. The equilibrium price and quantity will

  1. price falls and quantity rises
  2. price rises and quantity falls
  3. both rise
  4. both fall

Why C: At the old price there is now a shortage, so the price rises; the higher price causes an expansion of supply, so quantity rises too.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2d-02 · P2d.1 P2d.2 · 1 marks · level 3

Demand for avocados increases and, at the same time, a bumper harvest increases supply. What happens to the market equilibrium?

  1. Price and quantity both rise
  2. Price falls and quantity does not change
  3. Quantity rises, but the change in price depends on the relative size of the two shifts
  4. Price rises, but the change in quantity cannot be known

Why C: Both shifts raise quantity. The demand increase pushes price up and the supply increase pushes it down, so the net effect on price depends on which shift is larger.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2d-04 · P2d.2 P2d.1 · 1 marks · level 2

Using the schedule for mangoes, at a price of $2 there is

  1. a surplus of 300
  2. an equilibrium
  3. a shortage of 750
  4. a shortage of 300

Why D: At $2, quantity demanded (750) exceeds quantity supplied (450) by 300: a shortage, which pushes the price up towards $3.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2d-05 · P2d.3 · 1 marks · level 2

A competitive market equilibrium is said to be efficient because

  1. firms earn the highest possible profit
  2. everyone can afford the good
  3. resources are used to produce output up to the point where the value buyers place on the last unit equals the cost of producing it
  4. the government sets the price

Why C: At equilibrium, every unit that buyers value at more than its cost is produced, and no unit that costs more than it is worth to buyers is produced (allocative efficiency). It does not mean everyone can afford it.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2e-01 · P2e.3 · 1 marks · level 2

The price of a movie ticket rises by 10 per cent and the quantity demanded falls by 25 per cent. Using the percentage method, the price elasticity of demand is

  1. 0.4
  2. 0.25
  3. 15
  4. 2.5

Why D: PED = % change in quantity demanded ÷ % change in price = −25 ÷ 10 = −2.5, usually written as 2.5. It is greater than 1, so demand is relatively elastic. A divides the wrong way round.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2e-02 · P2e.1 · 1 marks · level 1

Demand for a good is perfectly inelastic. This means that

  1. the quantity demanded does not change when the price changes
  2. any price rise causes the quantity demanded to fall to zero
  3. the percentage change in quantity equals the percentage change in price
  4. the demand curve is horizontal

Why A: Perfectly inelastic demand (PED = 0) is a vertical demand curve. B and D describe perfectly elastic demand; C describes unit elasticity.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2e-03 · P2e.4 · 1 marks · level 2

Which of the following is most likely to have price inelastic demand?

  1. One particular brand of soft drink
  2. Overseas holidays
  3. Insulin for people with diabetes
  4. Restaurant meals

Why C: Insulin is a necessity with no close substitute, so buyers keep buying when the price rises. A has many close substitutes; B and D are not necessities.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2e-04 · P2e.5 · 1 marks · level 2

A business sells a product with price elastic demand. If it raises its price, its total revenue will

  1. rise
  2. double
  3. stay the same
  4. fall

Why D: With elastic demand, the percentage fall in quantity sold is larger than the percentage rise in price, so price × quantity falls.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2e-05 · P2e.4 · 1 marks · level 2

Demand for petrol is usually more price elastic in the long run than in the short run because

  1. over time, people can find substitutes, such as more fuel-efficient or electric cars and public transport
  2. petrol becomes cheaper over time
  3. incomes fall over time
  4. petrol becomes more addictive over time

Why A: The time horizon matters: with more time, buyers can change their habits and find alternatives, so they respond more to a price change.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2e-06 · P2e.2 · 1 marks · level 2

Compared with a relatively inelastic demand curve drawn through the same point, a relatively elastic demand curve is

  1. flatter, because a small change in price causes a large change in quantity demanded
  2. steeper, because a large change in price causes a small change in quantity demanded
  3. vertical
  4. upward sloping

Why A: The flatter the demand curve through a point, the more the quantity demanded responds to a given price change.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2f-01 · P2f.1 P2f.3 · 1 marks · level 2

On the morning of a fish market, the supply of fresh fish is likely to be

  1. perfectly elastic
  2. relatively elastic
  3. very inelastic, because the day's catch is already fixed
  4. unit elastic

Why C: In the very short run sellers cannot catch more fish, and fresh fish cannot be stored for long, so the quantity supplied barely responds to price.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2f-02 · P2f.3 · 1 marks · level 2

Which of the following makes the supply of a good more price elastic?

  1. Firms have spare (excess) capacity
  2. A very short time period
  3. The good perishes quickly and cannot be stored
  4. Production needs specialised workers who are hard to find

Why A: Spare capacity lets firms raise output quickly when the price rises. B, C and D all make supply less elastic.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2f-03 · P2f.4 · 1 marks · level 2

Price elasticity of supply matters to a business because

  1. a firm with elastic supply can raise its output quickly to take advantage of a price rise
  2. it shows how consumers respond to price changes
  3. it sets the rate of tax on the firm's product
  4. it matters only to governments

Why A: Firms that can respond quickly to higher prices earn more from a rise in demand. B describes price elasticity of demand.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2f-04 · P2f.1 P2f.2 · 1 marks · level 1

The amount of land in a city centre is fixed. Its supply curve is

  1. horizontal (perfectly elastic)
  2. upward sloping and flat
  3. vertical (perfectly inelastic)
  4. downward sloping

Why C: However high the price, no more city-centre land can be supplied, so PES = 0 and the supply curve is vertical.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2g-01 · P2g.2 · 1 marks · level 1

A market with a single seller, a product with no close substitutes and very high barriers to entry is

  1. perfect competition
  2. monopolistic competition
  3. an oligopoly
  4. a monopoly

Why D: These are the characteristics of a monopoly, which gives the seller strong pricing power.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2g-02 · P2g.2 · 1 marks · level 2

Which market structure best describes Australia's supermarket industry?

  1. Perfect competition
  2. Monopolistic competition
  3. Oligopoly
  4. Monopoly

Why C: A few large firms, led by Coles and Woolworths, hold most of the market, with high barriers to entry and businesses that watch and respond to each other's prices.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2g-03 · P2g.2 · 1 marks · level 2

Cafés in a large city are an example of monopolistic competition because

  1. there are many sellers offering similar but differentiated products, with low barriers to entry
  2. one café controls the whole market
  3. every café sells identical coffee at the same price
  4. a few large chains dominate and new cafés cannot enter

Why A: Each café differentiates itself (location, quality, atmosphere), which gives it a little pricing power, but it is easy for new cafés to open.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2g-04 · P2g.3 · 1 marks · level 2

An increase in market concentration (fewer, larger firms) is most likely to lead to

  1. lower prices and higher output
  2. perfect competition
  3. more firms entering the market
  4. higher prices, lower output and higher profits for the remaining firms

Why D: With less competition, firms have more pricing power: they can charge more and supply less. Quality and innovation may also suffer.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2g-05 · P2g.4 · 1 marks · level 2

A social media platform becomes more valuable to each user as more people join it. This is

  1. first-mover advantage
  2. consumer sovereignty
  3. an economy of scale in production
  4. a network effect

Why D: Network effects make large platforms more attractive and harder for new rivals to challenge, which adds to their market power.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2h-01 · P2h.3 · 1 marks · level 1

A negative externality is

  1. a cost imposed on third parties who are not part of the transaction
  2. a benefit received by the buyer
  3. a cost paid by the producer
  4. a tax on a good

Why A: Pollution affecting nearby residents is an example: the cost is not paid by the buyer or seller, so the market does not take it into account.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2h-02 · P2h.5 · 1 marks · level 2

A lighthouse is an example of a public good because it is

  1. rival and excludable
  2. provided only by private firms
  3. non-rival and non-excludable
  4. a demerit good

Why C: One ship using the light does not reduce its use by others (non-rival), and ships that do not pay cannot be stopped from using it (non-excludable).

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2h-03 · P2h.5 · 1 marks · level 2

The free rider problem means that

  1. people can benefit from a good without paying for it, so private firms will not supply enough of it
  2. the government provides goods free of charge
  3. monopolies charge high prices
  4. consumers lack information about products

Why A: If people can use a good without paying, few will pay, so a profit-seeking firm will under-provide it. This is why public goods are usually provided by government.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2h-04 · P2h.7 · 1 marks · level 2

A used-car seller knows a car has a faulty gearbox, but the buyer does not. This is an example of

  1. a positive externality
  2. perfect competition
  3. a public good
  4. asymmetric information

Why D: One side of the transaction knows more than the other, so the buyer may pay more than the car is worth. This can lead to market failure.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2h-05 · P2h.6 · 1 marks · level 2

Two petrol stations in a town secretly agree to keep their prices at the same high level. This is

  1. predatory pricing
  2. consumer sovereignty
  3. a network effect
  4. collusion

Why D: Collusion is an abuse of market power: firms agree not to compete, so consumers pay more. Predatory pricing is setting prices very low to drive rivals out.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2h-06 · P2h.3 · 1 marks · level 2

Education is often called a merit good because

  1. left to the market, it would be under-consumed, as people do not take into account its benefits to others
  2. left to the market, it would be over-consumed
  3. it is a public good
  4. it is a demerit good

Why A: An educated workforce benefits the whole community (a positive externality), so private demand is less than the socially efficient amount.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2h-07 · P2h.3 · 1 marks · level 3

Overfishing in international waters is an example of the problem of

  1. public goods
  2. merit goods
  3. common-pool resources
  4. asymmetric information

Why C: Fish stocks are rival (one boat's catch leaves fewer fish for others) but hard to exclude people from, so each user has an incentive to overuse them.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2h-08 · P2h.2 · 1 marks · level 2

Which of the following is a challenge some Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander businesses face in accessing capital?

  1. Interest rates set specifically for them by the Reserve Bank
  2. Too many lenders competing for their business
  3. A law that bans them from borrowing
  4. Fewer assets that lenders accept as security, such as land that can be mortgaged, which can make loans harder to obtain

Why D: Lenders usually want security for a loan. Land held communally or under native title generally cannot be mortgaged, and lower inherited wealth can mean fewer other assets.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2h-09 · P2h.8 · 1 marks · level 2

Income and wealth inequality can be seen as a market failure because

  1. markets reward people according to what they own and can sell, which can leave some people socially excluded and unable to meet basic needs
  2. markets always produce equal incomes
  3. inequality raises GDP
  4. the price mechanism sets the same wage for everyone

Why A: Markets allocate according to purchasing power, not need, so they may not achieve the community's social objectives.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2h-10 · P2h.1 · 1 marks · level 2

Which of the following is a limitation of the price mechanism?

  1. It allocates goods to people with purchasing power, regardless of need
  2. It responds to changes in consumer preferences
  3. It signals changes in scarcity
  4. It gives producers an incentive to supply more

Why A: B, C and D are strengths of the price mechanism. Allocating by ability to pay is one reason markets may not meet social objectives.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2i-01 · P2i.2 · 1 marks · level 1

The government wants fewer people to smoke. Which policy directly raises the price of cigarettes?

  1. A subsidy
  2. Direct provision
  3. A price ceiling
  4. An excise tax

Why D: An excise is a per-unit tax that raises producers' costs and so the price consumers pay, reducing the quantity of cigarettes bought.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2i-02 · P2i.3 · 1 marks · level 2

A binding price ceiling, set below the equilibrium price, will cause

  1. a surplus
  2. no change in the market
  3. a shortage
  4. an increase in supply

Why C: At the lower price, quantity demanded expands and quantity supplied contracts, so demand exceeds supply.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2i-03 · P2i.3 · 1 marks · level 2

A price floor is binding only when it is set

  1. below the equilibrium price
  2. by the market
  3. at the equilibrium price
  4. above the equilibrium price

Why D: A floor stops the price falling below it. If it is below equilibrium the market price is already higher, so the floor has no effect.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2i-04 · P2i.5 · 1 marks · level 2

An industry lobbies the government for a subsidy that benefits its firms but not the wider community. This is an example of

  1. rent-seeking
  2. consumer sovereignty
  3. a positive externality
  4. a nudge

Why A: Rent-seeking is using resources to gain a benefit from government rather than by producing something of value, and it can lead to government failure.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2i-05 · P2i.2 · 1 marks · level 2

Placing fruit at eye level and lollies on the top shelf of a school canteen is an example of

  1. a tax
  2. a regulation
  3. a nudge
  4. a quota

Why C: A nudge changes how choices are presented to encourage better decisions, without banning options or changing prices.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2i-06 · P2i.5 P2i.4 · 1 marks · level 3

A government subsidises fertiliser to raise farm output, but farmers overuse it and runoff damages a river. This is best described as

  1. government failure through an unintended consequence
  2. a public good
  3. allocative efficiency
  4. a binding price floor

Why A: The intervention aimed to help but created a new negative externality, so resources may end up less efficiently used.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P2i-07 · P2i.3 P5b.5 · 1 marks · level 3

A per-unit tax is placed on a good with very price inelastic demand. Most of the burden of the tax will fall on

  1. producers
  2. no one, because the price will not change
  3. the government
  4. consumers

Why D: When demand is inelastic, buyers keep buying as the price rises, so producers can pass most of the tax on to them in a higher price.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P3a-01 · P3a.5 P3a.4 · 1 marks · level 2

A household's weekly income rises from $1,000 to $1,200 and its weekly consumption rises from $900 to $1,050. Its marginal propensity to consume (MPC) is

  1. 0.75
  2. 0.875
  3. 0.25
  4. 1.33

Why A: MPC = change in consumption ÷ change in income = 150 ÷ 200 = 0.75. B is consumption ÷ income at the new level, the average, not the marginal, propensity.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P3a-02 · P3a.4 · 1 marks · level 1

If the marginal propensity to consume is 0.8, the marginal propensity to save is

  1. 0.8
  2. 1.8
  3. 0.2
  4. 1.25

Why C: Every extra dollar of income is either spent or saved, so MPC + MPS = 1 and MPS = 1 − 0.8 = 0.2.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P3a-03 · P3a.2 · 1 marks · level 2

The law of diminishing marginal utility states that

  1. total satisfaction falls as more of a good is consumed
  2. utility rises with the price of a good
  3. consumers always buy the cheapest good available
  4. each extra unit consumed adds less satisfaction than the one before

Why D: The third slice of pizza adds less satisfaction than the first. Total utility can still rise; it is the extra (marginal) utility that falls.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P3a-04 · P3a.8 · 1 marks · level 2

As their incomes rise, people buy fewer packets of generic store-brand noodles. Store-brand noodles are

  1. a normal good
  2. a merit good
  3. a Veblen good
  4. an inferior good

Why D: Demand for an inferior good falls as income rises, because buyers switch to preferred alternatives. Demand for a normal good rises with income.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P3a-05 · P3a.8 · 1 marks · level 2

Demand for a luxury handbag rises when its price rises, because a high price signals status. The handbag is

  1. a Veblen good
  2. an inferior good
  3. a public good
  4. a complement

Why A: For a Veblen good, a higher price makes the good more desirable as a status symbol, the opposite of the usual law of demand.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P3a-06 · P3a.6 · 1 marks · level 3

The paradox of thrift suggests that if all households try to save more during a downturn,

  1. total saving will rise sharply
  2. interest rates will rise
  3. spending and incomes will fall, so total saving may not rise at all
  4. the economy will grow faster

Why C: One household saving more is sensible, but if everyone spends less, firms sell less and incomes fall, which limits how much people can actually save.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P3a-07 · P3a.7 · 1 marks · level 2

A subscription service is easy to join online but can only be cancelled by phoning during business hours. This is an example of

  1. a nudge
  2. a public good
  3. consumer sovereignty
  4. a sludge

Why D: A sludge is friction deliberately put in the way of a choice that is good for the consumer, here cancelling, so that people keep paying.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P3a-08 · P3a.7 · 1 marks · level 2

Consumer sovereignty means that

  1. consumers' spending decisions determine what is produced
  2. the government decides what consumers buy
  3. firms control what consumers want
  4. consumers set the prices of goods

Why A: In a market economy, firms produce what consumers are willing to buy. Marketing and behavioural sludges can weaken this.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P3a-09 · P3a.3 · 1 marks · level 2

A shopper pays more for free-range eggs because she is concerned about animal welfare. This shows the consumer as

  1. an agent who cares only about the lowest price
  2. a Veblen consumer
  3. an inferior-good buyer
  4. a socially and ethically motivated agent

Why D: Consumers' choices are also shaped by values, culture and social norms, not only by price and their own satisfaction.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P3b-01 · P3b.1 P3b.2 · 1 marks · level 2

Which of the following is most likely to increase the supply of labour?

  1. Raising the school leaving age
  2. More affordable and available childcare
  3. An ageing population
  4. Higher income tax rates for people returning to work

Why B: Childcare costs and availability are a major barrier to parents working, so making care cheaper and easier to find raises participation. A, C and D reduce labour supply.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P3b-02 · P3b.2 · 1 marks · level 1

Which of the following is a disincentive to participating in the workforce?

  1. Flexible working hours
  2. Paid parental leave
  3. A lack of affordable care for children or older relatives
  4. Subsidised training

Why C: People with caring responsibilities may not be able to work if care is unaffordable or unavailable. A, B and D make working easier.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P3b-03 · P3b.1 · 1 marks · level 2

Occupational mobility of labour refers to

  1. workers' ability to move between different jobs or occupations
  2. workers moving between cities and regions
  3. workers travelling further to work each day
  4. businesses moving production overseas

Why A: Occupational mobility depends on skills and training; B is geographic mobility.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P3c-01 · P3c.2 · 1 marks · level 2

A business earned revenue of $500,000 and paid $380,000 in explicit costs. Its owner could have earned $90,000 working for another firm. What are the business's accounting profit and economic profit?

  1. Accounting profit $120,000; economic profit $30,000
  2. Accounting profit $120,000; economic profit $210,000
  3. Accounting profit $30,000; economic profit $120,000
  4. Accounting profit $500,000; economic profit $120,000

Why A: Accounting profit = 500,000 − 380,000 = $120,000. Economic profit also subtracts the opportunity cost of the owner's time: 120,000 − 90,000 = $30,000.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P3c-02 · P3c.3 · 1 marks · level 1

Australia Post, a business owned by the Australian Government, is an example of

  1. a private enterprise
  2. a sole trader
  3. a public enterprise
  4. a not-for-profit charity

Why C: A public enterprise (government business enterprise) is owned by government but sells goods and services in the market.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P3c-03 · P3c.4 · 1 marks · level 1

Productivity measures

  1. the total output of an economy
  2. the profits earned by businesses
  3. the number of people employed
  4. output per unit of input, such as output per hour worked

Why D: Higher productivity means more output from the same inputs, which lowers costs and supports higher wages and living standards.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P3c-04 · P3c.5 · 1 marks · level 2

A large supermarket chain buys stock in huge quantities at a lower price per unit than small grocers pay. This is an example of

  1. an external economy of scale
  2. capital deepening
  3. a diseconomy of scale
  4. an internal economy of scale

Why D: Purchasing economies come from the firm's own size, so they are internal. External economies come from the growth of the whole industry or region.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P3c-05 · P3c.5 · 1 marks · level 2

Capital deepening means

  1. increasing the amount of capital per worker
  2. increasing the number of workers
  3. borrowing more money
  4. selling off machinery

Why A: Giving each worker more or better equipment raises output per worker, a key source of productivity growth.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P3c-06 · P3c.6 · 1 marks · level 2

On a long-run average cost (LRAC) curve, the technical optimum is the level of output at which

  1. long-run average cost is lowest
  2. total output is largest
  3. economies of scale begin
  4. total cost is lowest

Why A: Up to the technical optimum the firm enjoys economies of scale; beyond it, diseconomies of scale push average cost up.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P3d-01 · P3d.1 · 1 marks · level 1

In Australia, the national minimum wage is set each year by

  1. the Reserve Bank of Australia
  2. each employer
  3. the Fair Work Commission
  4. the Australian Competition and Consumer Commission

Why C: The Fair Work Commission's annual wage review sets the national minimum wage and the minimum rates in modern awards.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P3d-02 · P3d.3 · 1 marks · level 2

Which of the following is most likely to reduce businesses' demand for labour?

  1. A rise in total output
  2. Cheaper automation and AI that can do tasks workers now do
  3. A fall in the cost of labour compared with the cost of capital
  4. Higher business profits

Why B: When machines and software become cheaper relative to labour, firms may replace some workers. A, C and D would usually increase the demand for labour.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P3d-03 · P3d.1 · 1 marks · level 1

Which of the following is a non-wage benefit of a job?

  1. Overtime pay
  2. Flexible working hours
  3. The hourly rate of pay
  4. Penalty rates for weekend work

Why B: Non-wage benefits are rewards other than pay, such as flexibility, leave, training or a work car. A, C and D are all forms of wage.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P4a-01 · P4a.1 · 1 marks · level 1

The main role of the financial sector in the economy is to

  1. produce goods and services for households
  2. channel funds from savers to borrowers who want to invest
  3. set tax rates
  4. regulate international trade

Why B: By linking savers with borrowers, the financial sector turns savings into investment, which helps the economy grow.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P4a-02 · P4a.2 · 1 marks · level 1

Which of the following is a financial intermediary?

  1. A superannuation fund
  2. A supermarket
  3. The Australian Competition and Consumer Commission
  4. The Fair Work Commission

Why A: A super fund pools members' savings and invests them in shares, bonds, property and infrastructure, standing between savers and the users of funds.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P4a-03 · P4a.3 · 1 marks · level 2

Superannuation supports the Australian economy mainly by

  1. increasing government spending
  2. building a large pool of savings that is invested in shares, bonds, property and infrastructure
  3. lowering the cash rate
  4. increasing imports

Why B: Compulsory contributions create long-term savings that fund investment, and they also provide retirement incomes.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P4a-04 · P4a.3 · 1 marks · level 1

From 1 July 2025, the superannuation guarantee requires employers to contribute what percentage of an employee's ordinary time earnings to super?

  1. 9.5 per cent
  2. 10 per cent
  3. 11.5 per cent
  4. 12 per cent

Why D: The superannuation guarantee rose in half-point steps from 9.5 per cent in 2020–21 to 12 per cent from 1 July 2025 (Australian Taxation Office).

Multiple choice y11-mc-P4b-01 · P4b.2 · 1 marks · level 2

A company sells new shares to investors for the first time in an initial public offering (IPO). This takes place in the

  1. secondary share market
  2. primary share market
  3. debt market
  4. foreign exchange market

Why B: New shares are issued in the primary market, which raises funds for the company. Later trading of those shares between investors is the secondary market.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P4b-02 · P4b.1 · 1 marks · level 1

A shareholder in a company owns

  1. a loan to the company that must be repaid with interest
  2. part of the company and a claim on its profits
  3. a government bond
  4. nothing until the company is sold

Why B: Shares are equity: part-ownership, with a share of profits paid as dividends. A loan is debt.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P4b-03 · P4b.2 · 1 marks · level 2

The secondary share market, such as the Australian Securities Exchange, helps the economy mainly because

  1. companies receive new funds every time their shares are traded
  2. investors can easily sell shares, which makes them more willing to buy new shares when companies issue them
  3. the government sets share prices on it
  4. it removes the risk of owning shares

Why B: Liquidity in the secondary market supports the primary market. Companies do not receive money when existing shares change hands.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P4c-01 · P4c.3 · 1 marks · level 1

An interest rate can best be described as

  1. the price of money: the cost of borrowing and the reward for saving
  2. the rate of inflation
  3. the exchange rate
  4. the rate of tax on savings

Why A: Borrowers pay interest for the use of funds and savers receive it for lending them.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P4c-02 · P4c.6 P4c.4 · 1 marks · level 2

In the loanable funds market, an increase in the demand for funds, with supply unchanged, will

  1. raise the interest rate and the quantity of funds lent
  2. lower the interest rate
  3. reduce the quantity of funds lent
  4. have no effect on the interest rate

Why A: The demand curve for funds shifts right, so the interest rate rises until the extra borrowing is matched by an expansion in the quantity of funds supplied.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P4c-03 · P4c.5 · 1 marks · level 2

Which of the following would increase the supply of loanable funds?

  1. Households saving a larger share of their income
  2. A fall in business confidence
  3. A larger government budget deficit
  4. Higher household spending

Why A: More saving means more funds available to lend. B and C affect the demand for funds; D reduces saving.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P4c-04 · P4c.7 · 1 marks · level 2

A rise in interest rates is most likely to

  1. make housing more affordable for new buyers
  2. raise mortgage repayments and reduce how much buyers can borrow
  3. raise house prices straight away
  4. have no effect on housing

Why B: Higher rates increase the cost of servicing a loan, so existing borrowers pay more and new buyers can borrow less.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P4c-05 · P4c.2 · 1 marks · level 2

When the Australian Government sells newly issued bonds to raise funds, it is borrowing in the

  1. primary debt (bond) market
  2. secondary share market
  3. foreign exchange market
  4. equity market

Why A: New bonds are sold in the primary market. Investors may later trade them in the secondary bond market.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P4c-06 · P4c.3 · 1 marks · level 3

Riskier borrowers usually pay higher interest rates because

  1. lenders want a higher return to compensate for the greater chance that they will not be repaid
  2. the Reserve Bank requires it
  3. risky borrowers always borrow less
  4. interest rates are set by the government

Why A: The interest rate includes a risk premium, so it also acts as an indicator of risk.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P5a-01 · P5a.1 · 1 marks · level 2

Unemployment benefits and a progressive income tax help the government to

  1. reallocate resources
  2. redistribute income
  3. correct a public good problem
  4. privatise government businesses

Why B: Taxing higher incomes at higher rates and paying benefits to those on low incomes makes the distribution of income more equal.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P5a-02 · P5a.1 · 1 marks · level 2

When the government increases its spending during a recession to support jobs, it is carrying out its role of

  1. stabilisation
  2. redistribution
  3. reallocation
  4. privatisation

Why A: Stabilisation means smoothing the business cycle: supporting spending in a downturn and restraining it when the economy overheats.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P5a-03 · P5a.4 · 1 marks · level 1

A budget deficit occurs when

  1. government revenue is greater than government expenditure
  2. government expenditure is greater than government revenue
  3. imports are greater than exports
  4. saving is greater than investment

Why B: The government must borrow to cover a deficit. A describes a budget surplus.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P5a-04 · P5a.6 · 1 marks · level 2

An energy company sends each household a report comparing its electricity use with that of similar households nearby, to encourage it to use less. This is an example of

  1. a tax
  2. a regulation
  3. a nudge
  4. privatisation

Why C: The comparison uses social norms to change behaviour without changing prices or banning anything.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P5a-05 · P5a.7 · 1 marks · level 2

Which of the following is an argument for privatising a government business?

  1. Private ownership and competition may make the business more efficient
  2. Privatisation guarantees lower prices for consumers
  3. Privatisation ensures services reach remote areas
  4. Privatisation increases government ownership

Why A: Supporters argue that the profit motive and competition improve efficiency and service. B and C are not guaranteed; a private monopoly may raise prices or cut unprofitable services.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P5a-06 · P5a.2 · 1 marks · level 2

Under the National Agreement on Closing the Gap (2020), Australian governments agreed to share decision-making on policy with

  1. the Reserve Bank of Australia
  2. the Coalition of Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander Community-Controlled Peak Organisations (the Coalition of Peaks)
  3. the Australian Competition and Consumer Commission
  4. the G20

Why B: The Coalition of Peaks is a party to the National Agreement, which commits governments to formal partnerships and shared decision-making with Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander organisations.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P5a-07 · P5a.5 · 1 marks · level 1

A law requiring builders to meet safety standards is an example of

  1. regulation
  2. a subsidy
  3. a tax
  4. direct provision of a good

Why A: Regulation sets rules that businesses and households must follow, to prevent or reduce harm.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P5b-01 · P5b.2 · 1 marks · level 1

The largest single source of Australian Government revenue is

  1. company tax
  2. the goods and services tax (GST)
  3. individual (personal) income tax
  4. fuel excise

Why C: Taxes on individuals' incomes raise more than any other source, around half of the Australian Government's tax revenue.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P5b-02 · P5b.5 · 1 marks · level 2

A direct tax is a tax that

  1. is paid when goods and services are bought
  2. is levied on income or profits and paid by the person or business that earns it
  3. is always regressive
  4. is collected by retailers

Why B: Income tax and company tax are direct taxes. A and D describe indirect taxes such as the GST.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P5b-03 · P5b.5 · 1 marks · level 2

The GST is an indirect tax because

  1. it is levied on incomes
  2. businesses pay it to the government, but its cost is passed on to consumers in higher prices
  3. it is a tax on wealth
  4. it applies only to imported goods

Why B: The legal incidence falls on businesses, which collect and remit the tax, but most of the economic incidence falls on consumers.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P5b-04 · P5b.5 · 1 marks · level 3

The legal incidence of a tax refers to

  1. who actually bears the cost of the tax
  2. who is legally required to pay the tax to the government
  3. the rate at which the tax is charged
  4. the revenue the tax raises

Why B: Legal incidence is who must pay the tax office. Economic incidence (A) is who finally bears the cost, which depends on price elasticities.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P5b-05 · P5b.6 · 1 marks · level 2

A tax that taxpayers find easy to understand and comply with meets which feature of a good tax?

  1. Fairness
  2. Simplicity
  3. Efficiency
  4. Progressivity

Why B: Simplicity means a tax is easy to understand, comply with and collect. Fairness concerns who pays; efficiency concerns how little the tax distorts decisions.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P5b-06 · P5b.3 · 1 marks · level 2

Which of the following is a tax on wealth?

  1. The GST
  2. Personal income tax
  3. Land tax
  4. Payroll tax

Why C: Land tax is charged on the value of land owned, a stock of wealth. The GST taxes consumption; income and payroll taxes are taxes on income.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P5b-07 · P5b.6 · 1 marks · level 2

A tax is progressive when

  1. everyone pays the same amount of tax
  2. the average tax rate rises as income rises
  3. lower-income earners pay a larger share of their income in tax
  4. everyone pays the same percentage of their income

Why B: Under a progressive tax, people with higher incomes pay a larger proportion of their income. C describes a regressive tax and D a proportional tax.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P5b-08 · P5b.7 · 1 marks · level 3

Which of the following is a common argument for reform of the Australian tax system?

  1. Heavy reliance on income tax may discourage work and investment, and bracket creep raises average tax rates over time
  2. Australia has no income tax
  3. The GST base is too broad
  4. Companies in Australia pay no tax

Why A: Reviews have pointed to Australia's reliance on income taxes and to bracket creep. A common view is that the GST base is too narrow, because of exemptions such as fresh food, the opposite of C.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P5c-01 · P5c.2 · 1 marks · level 1

The largest category of Australian Government spending is

  1. defence
  2. education
  3. social security and welfare
  4. transport and infrastructure

Why C: Payments such as the age pension, family payments, disability support and unemployment benefits make social security and welfare the largest category, around a third of spending.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P5c-02 · P5c.1 · 1 marks · level 2

Which of the following is non-recurrent (capital) government spending?

  1. Paying teachers' salaries
  2. The age pension
  3. Building a new hospital
  4. Interest on government debt

Why C: Capital spending creates long-lasting assets such as hospitals, roads and schools. A, B and D occur every year (recurrent spending).

Multiple choice y11-mc-P5c-03 · P5c.3 · 1 marks · level 3

Which of the following is a reason given for reform of Australian Government spending?

  1. Spending on programs such as the NDIS, aged care, health and interest on debt is growing faster than revenue
  2. The government spends nothing on welfare
  3. Spending never affects the budget balance
  4. Most spending goes on defence

Why A: Fast-growing spending pressures, many linked to an ageing population, raise questions about the budget's long-term sustainability and how well spending is targeted.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P6a-01 · P6a.1 · 1 marks · level 2

International trade raises living standards mainly because

  1. countries specialise according to comparative advantage and trade, so more is produced from the same resources
  2. imports always cost more than exports
  3. countries become self-sufficient
  4. trade removes the need for money

Why A: Voluntary exchange benefits both sides: each country specialises in what it produces at the lowest opportunity cost and trades for the rest.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P6a-02 · P6a.2 · 1 marks · level 1

Australia's largest two-way trading partner is

  1. the United States
  2. Japan
  3. China
  4. the United Kingdom

Why C: China has been Australia's largest two-way trading partner since 2007 (Department of Foreign Affairs and Trade), mainly through Australian exports of iron ore and other resources.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P6a-03 · P6a.2 · 1 marks · level 2

Which statement best describes the composition of Australia's goods exports?

  1. They are mostly manufactured goods such as cars and machinery
  2. They are dominated by mineral and energy resources such as iron ore, coal, natural gas and gold
  3. They are mostly services
  4. They are mostly agricultural products

Why B: Resources make up the largest share of Australia's exports. Rural goods such as beef and wheat and services such as education and tourism are also important, but smaller.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P6a-04 · P6a.4 · 1 marks · level 2

A country's exports of goods and services are worth $480 billion and its imports are worth $510 billion. Its trade balance is

  1. a surplus of $30 billion
  2. a deficit of $30 billion
  3. balanced
  4. a surplus of $990 billion

Why B: Trade balance = exports − imports = 480 − 510 = −$30 billion, a trade deficit.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P6a-05 · P6a.3 · 1 marks · level 2

From at least the 1700s until 1907, fishermen from Makassar (in present-day Indonesia) sailed each year to the Arnhem Land coast and traded and worked with Yolŋu people. The main product they took back, to be sold on to China, was

  1. wool
  2. trepang (sea cucumber)
  3. gold
  4. iron ore

Why B: The fishermen caught, boiled and dried trepang on the Arnhem Land coast and sold it to Chinese traders. Yolŋu people traded with them for goods such as cloth, tobacco, knives and rice (National Museum of Australia).

Multiple choice y11-mc-P6b-01 · P6b.1 · 1 marks · level 2

Foreign equity investment in Australia occurs when

  1. an Australian bank borrows from overseas lenders
  2. overseas investors buy shares in, or take ownership of, Australian businesses or property
  3. Australians buy shares in overseas companies
  4. the Reserve Bank buys foreign currency

Why B: Equity is ownership. A creates foreign debt; C is Australian investment abroad.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P6b-02 · P6b.1 · 1 marks · level 2

Which of the following adds to Australia's foreign debt?

  1. A Japanese company buying an Australian mine
  2. An Australian company borrowing from a US bank
  3. An Australian buying shares in a US company
  4. A tourist spending money in Sydney

Why B: Borrowing from overseas creates a debt that must be repaid with interest. A is foreign equity in Australia.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P6b-03 · P6b.2 · 1 marks · level 2

Which country is the largest source of foreign investment in Australia?

  1. China
  2. The United States
  3. India
  4. New Zealand

Why B: The United States holds the largest stock of foreign investment in Australia (Australian Bureau of Statistics, International Investment Position). China's investment is much smaller than its share of Australia's trade.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P6c-01 · P6c.2 · 1 marks · level 2

If A$1 = US$0.65, how much does a laptop priced at US$1,300 cost in Australian dollars?

  1. A$845
  2. A$2,000
  3. A$1,300
  4. A$1,950

Why B: Australian dollars = US$ price ÷ US$ per A$ = 1,300 ÷ 0.65 = A$2,000. A multiplies instead of dividing.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P6c-02 · P6c.1 · 1 marks · level 1

Exchange rates are needed for international trade and investment because

  1. countries use different currencies, so buyers must convert their currency to pay sellers
  2. every country uses the US dollar
  3. exchange rates set the prices of goods within each country
  4. exchange rates prevent trade between countries

Why A: An Australian importer paying a Japanese supplier must buy yen with Australian dollars, at the exchange rate.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P6c-03 · P6c.2 · 1 marks · level 2

The Australian dollar rises from US$0.62 to US$0.68. The Australian dollar has

  1. depreciated
  2. appreciated
  3. been devalued by the Reserve Bank
  4. stayed the same

Why B: Each Australian dollar now buys more US dollars, so its value has risen (appreciated). C applies only to fixed exchange rates.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P6d-01 · P6d.1 · 1 marks · level 1

The balance of payments is

  1. the Federal Government's budget
  2. a record of all economic transactions between a country's residents and the rest of the world over a period of time
  3. a record of a country's foreign debt only
  4. the difference between national saving and investment

Why B: It records trade, income and financial flows with the rest of the world, usually each quarter and year.

Multiple choice y11-mc-P6d-02 · P6d.1 · 1 marks · level 2

Which of the following is recorded in the current account of Australia's balance of payments?

  1. A foreign company buying an Australian mine
  2. Exports of Australian beef
  3. The Reserve Bank's holdings of foreign currency
  4. Australian banks borrowing from overseas

Why B: The current account records trade in goods and services and income flows. A, C and D are financial account transactions.

Short answer y11-sd-P1c-ppf · P1c.4 P1c.1 P1c.5 · 5 marks · level 2

The production possibilities schedule shows the combinations of cars and wheat an economy can produce when it uses all its resources efficiently.

PointCars ('000)Wheat ('000 tonnes)
A0200
B10190
C20170
D30140
E40100
F5040

(a) Calculate the opportunity cost of increasing car production from 20,000 to 30,000. (1 marks)

Wheat falls from 170,000 to 140,000 tonnes, so the 10,000 extra cars cost 30,000 tonnes of wheat (3 tonnes per car).

(b) Explain why the opportunity cost of producing cars rises as more cars are produced. (2 marks)

  • Each extra 10,000 cars costs more wheat: 10, 20, 30, 40 and then 60 thousand tonnes (1)
  • Resources are not equally suited to both goods; as car output rises, resources better suited to growing wheat are moved into cars, so more wheat is given up for each car (1)

(c) The economy is producing 20,000 cars and 120,000 tonnes of wheat. Explain what this shows. (2 marks)

  • This point is inside the frontier: with 20,000 cars the economy could produce 170,000 tonnes of wheat (1)
  • Resources are unemployed or underemployed (or used inefficiently), so 50,000 tonnes of wheat could be gained at no opportunity cost (1)

Short answer y11-sd-P1d-flow · P1d.4 P1d.5 P1d.2 · 5 marks · level 2

The table shows the flows in the circular flow of income for an economy over one year.

Flow$ billion
Savings (S)120
Taxation (T)160
Imports (M)110
Investment (I)140
Government spending (G)150
Exports (X)90

(a) Calculate total leakages and total injections. (1 marks)

Leakages S + T + M = 120 + 160 + 110 = $390 billion. Injections I + G + X = 140 + 150 + 90 = $380 billion.

(b) Explain what will happen to national income in this economy. (2 marks)

  • Leakages exceed injections by $10 billion, so less is spent than is received as income (1)
  • Spending, production and income fall until leakages again equal injections (equilibrium) (1)

(c) Explain ONE way the government could keep national income at its current level. (2 marks)

  • Identifies a suitable change, e.g. increase government spending (G) or reduce taxation (T) by $10 billion (1)
  • Explains it: injections rise or leakages fall until S + T + M = I + G + X at the current level of income (1)

Short answer y11-sd-P1e-gdp · P1e.4 P1e.5 P1e.7 · 6 marks · level 2

The table shows quarterly growth in real GDP and the unemployment rate for an economy.

QuarterReal GDP growth (%)Unemployment rate (%)
10.94.0
20.74.0
30.24.2
4−0.34.6
5−0.55.1
60.15.4
70.65.3

(a) Identify the two consecutive quarters in which real GDP fell. (1 marks)

Quarters 4 and 5 (growth of −0.3 and −0.5 per cent).

(b) Identify the stage of the business cycle in Quarters 4 and 5, and the trough. (2 marks)

Quarters 4 and 5 are a contraction (downturn), the two quarters of falling GDP often called a recession. The trough is around Quarter 5 to 6, after which growth recovers.

(c) Explain why unemployment kept rising in Quarter 6, even though GDP had begun to grow again. (3 marks)

  • Unemployment lags the business cycle: firms wait to be confident the recovery will last before hiring (1)
  • Growth of 0.1 per cent is too weak to create enough jobs for the growing labour force (1)
  • Firms first use their existing workers more fully (more hours) before taking on new staff (1)

Short answer y11-sd-P2b-movies · P2b.3 P2b.4 P2b.5 · 5 marks · level 2

The table shows the weekly demand schedule for tickets at a cinema.

Price ($)Tickets demanded per week
10800
12700
14600
16500
18400

(a) Calculate the change in the quantity of tickets demanded when the price rises from $12 to $16. (1 marks)

The quantity demanded falls from 700 to 500 tickets a week, a contraction of 200 tickets.

(b) Distinguish between a contraction of demand and a decrease in demand, using this cinema as an example. (2 marks)

  • A contraction of demand is a movement along the demand curve caused by a rise in the good's own price, e.g. the rise from $12 to $16 (1)
  • A decrease in demand is a shift of the whole curve to the left caused by a non-price factor, e.g. a fall in incomes, so fewer tickets are demanded at every price (1)

(c) Streaming services raise their subscription prices sharply. Explain the likely effect on the demand for cinema tickets. (2 marks)

  • Streaming and cinema are substitutes (1)
  • Some viewers switch to the cinema, so demand for tickets increases: more tickets are demanded at each price and the demand curve shifts right (1)

Short answer y11-sd-P2d-market · P2d.1 P2d.2 P2b.5 P2c.5 · 5 marks · level 2

The table shows the market for a good.

Price ($)Quantity demandedQuantity supplied
21,000400
3900600
4800800
57001,000
66001,200

(a) Identify the equilibrium price and quantity. (1 marks)

$4 and 800 units, where quantity demanded equals quantity supplied.

(b) Calculate the surplus or shortage at a price of $5, and explain what will happen to the price. (2 marks)

At $5 quantity supplied (1,000) exceeds quantity demanded (700): a surplus of 300 units. Sellers cut prices to clear unsold stock, so the price falls back towards $4.

(c) Demand increases by 300 units at every price. Identify the new equilibrium price and quantity. (2 marks)

The new quantities demanded are 1,300, 1,200, 1,100, 1,000 and 900. At $5, quantity demanded (1,000) equals quantity supplied (1,000), so the new equilibrium is $5 and 1,000 units: both price and quantity rise.

Short answer y11-sd-P2e-cinema · P2e.3 P2e.5 P2e.4 · 6 marks · level 2

A cinema raises the price of a ticket from $20 to $22. Its weekly ticket sales fall from 2,000 to 1,700.

(a) Calculate the price elasticity of demand using the percentage method. (2 marks)

% change in quantity = (1,700 − 2,000) ÷ 2,000 × 100 = −15%. % change in price = (22 − 20) ÷ 20 × 100 = 10%. PED = −15 ÷ 10 = −1.5 (1.5): demand is relatively elastic.

(b) Calculate the change in the cinema's weekly revenue, and explain it using the price elasticity of demand. (2 marks)

Revenue falls from $40,000 (20 × 2,000) to $37,400 (22 × 1,700), down $2,600. Because demand is elastic, the percentage fall in tickets sold (15%) is larger than the percentage rise in price (10%).

(c) Outline ONE reason why the demand for cinema tickets may be price elastic. (2 marks)

  • Identifies a reason, e.g. close substitutes such as streaming and other entertainment; not a necessity (1)
  • Outlines it: when the price rises, many customers switch to the substitute or go less often (1)

Short answer y11-sd-P2e-tr · P2e.3 P2e.1 P2e.5 · 6 marks · level 3

The table shows what happens to the quantity demanded of three goods when each one's price rises by 20 per cent.

GoodChange in quantity demanded
Petrol−4%
Restaurant meals−30%
Bus fares (one city)−20%

(a) Calculate the price elasticity of demand for each good. (2 marks)

Petrol: 4 ÷ 20 = 0.2 (inelastic). Restaurant meals: 30 ÷ 20 = 1.5 (elastic). Bus fares: 20 ÷ 20 = 1 (unit elastic).

(b) Explain what happens to the total revenue of petrol sellers and of restaurants after the price rise. (2 marks)

  • Petrol: demand is inelastic, so the percentage fall in quantity is smaller than the price rise and total revenue rises (1)
  • Restaurants: demand is elastic, so the fall in quantity outweighs the price rise and total revenue falls (1)

(c) Explain why the government might prefer to tax petrol rather than restaurant meals if its aim is to raise revenue. (2 marks)

  • Because demand for petrol is inelastic, a tax barely reduces the quantity bought (1)
  • So the tax base stays large and the tax raises a lot of revenue with little change in behaviour (1)

Short answer y11-sd-P2i-tax · P2i.3 P5b.5 P2i.2 · 5 marks · level 3

Before a tax, 10,000 bottles of a sugary drink are sold each week at $3.00 a bottle. The government places a tax of $0.50 on each bottle. Afterwards, 9,500 bottles a week are sold at $3.40.

(a) Calculate the government's weekly revenue from the tax. (1 marks)

$0.50 × 9,500 = $4,750 a week (the tax per unit times the quantity sold after the tax).

(b) Calculate how much of the $0.50 tax is paid by consumers and how much by producers. (2 marks)

Consumers pay $0.40 more ($3.40 − $3.00). Producers now keep $2.90 a bottle ($3.40 − $0.50), $0.10 less than before. So consumers bear $0.40 and producers $0.10 of each $0.50.

(c) Explain why consumers bear most of this tax. (2 marks)

  • Demand is price inelastic: the price rose by about 13% but the quantity bought fell by only 5% (PED about 0.4), so buyers kept buying at the higher price (1)
  • So producers could pass most of the tax on in a higher price: the economic incidence falls mainly on consumers, even though producers pay the tax to the government (legal incidence) (1)

Short answer y11-sd-P3a-mpc · P3a.4 P3a.5 · 4 marks · level 2

The table shows a household's weekly disposable income and consumption.

Disposable incomeConsumption
800760
1,000920
1,2001,080

(a) Calculate the marginal propensity to consume when income rises from $1,000 to $1,200. (1 marks)

MPC = change in consumption ÷ change in income = (1,080 − 920) ÷ (1,200 − 1,000) = 160 ÷ 200 = 0.8.

(b) Calculate the marginal propensity to save. (1 marks)

MPS = 1 − MPC = 1 − 0.8 = 0.2 (the household saves 20 cents of each extra dollar).

(c) Explain what happens to the household's saving as its income rises. (2 marks)

  • Saving (income − consumption) rises from $40 to $80 to $120 a week (1)
  • The share of income saved also rises, from 5% to about 8% to 10%: as income rises, households can save a larger proportion (1)

Short answer y11-sd-P3c-profit · P3c.2 · 5 marks · level 2

Mia runs a bakery. Last year it earned revenue of $420,000 and paid $250,000 for ingredients, rent and wages. Mia put $60,000 of her own savings into the business, which could have earned 5 per cent interest in a bank, and she gave up a job paying $85,000 a year.

(a) Calculate the bakery's accounting profit. (1 marks)

Accounting profit = revenue − explicit costs = 420,000 − 250,000 = $170,000.

(b) Calculate the bakery's economic profit. (2 marks)

Implicit (opportunity) costs = 85,000 + (5% × 60,000) = 85,000 + 3,000 = $88,000. Economic profit = 170,000 − 88,000 = $82,000.

(c) Explain why economic profit is a better guide than accounting profit to whether Mia should keep running the bakery. (2 marks)

  • Economic profit also counts opportunity costs: the income from her next best job and the interest on her savings (1)
  • A positive economic profit ($82,000) shows the bakery earns more than her resources would earn elsewhere, so staying in business is worthwhile (1)

Short answer y11-sd-P3c-prod · P3c.4 P3c.5 · 6 marks · level 2

The table shows output and hours worked at a factory before and after it installed new machinery.

BeforeAfter
Output (units a week)12,00015,000
Hours worked a week2,4002,500

(a) Calculate labour productivity (output per hour worked) before and after, and the percentage change. (2 marks)

Before: 12,000 ÷ 2,400 = 5 units an hour. After: 15,000 ÷ 2,500 = 6 units an hour. Change = (6 − 5) ÷ 5 × 100 = 20 per cent.

(b) Explain how the new machinery raised productivity. (2 marks)

  • It is capital deepening: each worker has more and better capital to work with (1)
  • So more output is produced in each hour of work, lowering the cost of each unit (1)

(c) Explain why higher productivity is important for living standards. (2 marks)

  • Higher output per hour allows higher real wages without raising unit costs or prices (1)
  • It raises the economy's productive capacity, so incomes and living standards can rise over time (1)

Short answer y11-sd-P4c-mortgage · P4c.7 P4c.3 · 4 marks · level 2

A household has a home loan of $600,000. Ignoring repayments of the amount borrowed, its yearly interest cost is the loan multiplied by the interest rate.

(a) Calculate the yearly interest at interest rates of 5 per cent and 6 per cent. (1 marks)

At 5%: $600,000 × 0.05 = $30,000. At 6%: $600,000 × 0.06 = $36,000, which is $6,000 a year (about $500 a month) more.

(b) Explain how a rise in interest rates from 5 to 6 per cent affects housing affordability. (3 marks)

  • Existing borrowers pay more interest, leaving less income for other spending (1)
  • New buyers can borrow less for the same repayment, because banks test whether they can afford repayments (1)
  • Fewer people can afford to buy, so demand for housing falls, which may slow or reduce house prices over time (1)

Short answer y11-sd-P5b-tax · P5b.6 P5b.4 · 5 marks · level 2

In a hypothetical income tax system, no tax is paid on the first $20,000 of income, 20 per cent is paid on income from $20,001 to $60,000, and 40 per cent on income above $60,000.

(a) Calculate the tax paid, and the average tax rate, for a person earning $80,000. (2 marks)

20% × 40,000 = $8,000 plus 40% × 20,000 = $8,000, so the tax is $16,000. The average tax rate is 16,000 ÷ 80,000 = 20 per cent.

(b) Identify this person's marginal tax rate. (1 marks)

40 per cent, the rate on each extra dollar of income above $60,000.

(c) Using a person earning $40,000 for comparison, explain why this tax is progressive. (2 marks)

  • At $40,000 the tax is 20% × 20,000 = $4,000, an average rate of 10 per cent (1)
  • The average tax rate rises with income (10 per cent at $40,000, 20 per cent at $80,000), so higher earners pay a larger share of their income (1)

Deficit: services imports (110) exceed services exports (90) by $20 billion.
