Short answer y11-sa-P1a-01 · P1a.2 P1a.4 · 3 marks · level 1

Explain the relationship between scarcity and opportunity cost.

  • Scarcity: people's wants are unlimited but the resources to satisfy them are limited (1)
  • So every individual, business and government must choose how to use resources (1)
  • Each choice has an opportunity cost, the next best alternative forgone, e.g. a council that builds a pool cannot use the same funds for a library (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P1a-02 · P1a.6 P1a.9 · 4 marks · level 2

Explain how incentives and behavioural biases can each affect economic decisions. Use an example of each.

  • Incentives are rewards (or penalties) that encourage (or discourage) a choice (1)
  • Example, e.g. a rebate on solar panels encourages households to install them; a tax on sugary drinks discourages buying them (1)
  • Behavioural biases mean people do not always make the rational, self-interested choice that economic models assume (1)
  • Example, e.g. present bias leads people to under-save for retirement; default bias keeps people in a high-fee super fund (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P1a-03 · P1a.7 · 3 marks · level 1

Outline TWO ways data is used in economic decision-making.

  • Identifies two uses, e.g. informing public policy, monitoring economic performance, forecasting (1)
  • Outlines the first with an example, e.g. unemployment data shows governments where to target job programs (1)
  • Outlines the second with an example, e.g. the RBA uses inflation and wage data to forecast inflation before it sets the cash rate (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P1a-04 · P1a.10 P1a.11 · 3 marks · level 1

Distinguish between microeconomics and macroeconomics, using an example of an issue studied in each.

  • Microeconomics studies individual consumers, firms and markets (1)
  • Macroeconomics studies the economy as a whole: total output, employment, inflation, trade (1)
  • Examples, e.g. micro: why the price of rental housing in Sydney is rising; macro: why Australia's unemployment rate has risen (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P1a-05 · P1a.8 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain why economists use models, and ONE limitation of economic models.

  • Models simplify a complex economy so economists can see the effect of one change at a time (1)
  • They rely on assumptions, such as ceteris paribus and rational behaviour, to make predictions (1)
  • Limitation, e.g. real people and markets do not always behave as assumed (behavioural economics), so predictions can be wrong (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P1b-01 · P1b.3 P1b.4 · 4 marks · level 2

Compare how a planned economy and a free market economy answer the question "What to produce?"

  • Planned economy: a central authority (government) decides what is produced, according to its plan and priorities (1)
  • Free market economy: consumers' spending decides, through the price mechanism and the profit motive (1)
  • Similarity: both must choose because resources are scarce (1)
  • Difference in outcomes, e.g. markets respond quickly to consumer wants but may under-provide public goods; planning can direct resources to priorities but often creates shortages and surpluses (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P1b-02 · P1b.5 · 3 marks · level 1

Outline THREE functions of money.

  • Medium of exchange: accepted in payment for goods and services, avoiding the need for barter (1)
  • Measure of value (unit of account): lets the values of different goods be compared in dollars (1)
  • Store of value: can be saved and spent later (also a standard of deferred payment for debts) (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P1b-03 · P1b.1 P1b.2 · 4 marks · level 1

Identify the FOUR factors of production and the income each one earns.

  • Land (natural resources): rent (1)
  • Labour: wages (1)
  • Capital: interest (1)
  • Entrepreneurship: profit (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P1c-01 · P1c.2 P1c.6 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain how producing more capital goods today can affect an economy's production possibilities in the future.

  • More capital goods today means fewer consumer goods: an opportunity cost of lower consumption now (1)
  • Capital goods (machinery, infrastructure, technology) raise the economy's productive capacity (1)
  • So the production possibilities frontier shifts outwards in the future, allowing more of both goods (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P1d-01 · P1d.1 P1d.3 · 4 marks · level 2

Describe the roles of the household sector and the financial sector in the circular flow of income.

  • Households own the factors of production and supply them to businesses in return for income (1)
  • Households spend most of their income on goods and services (consumption) and save the rest (1)
  • The financial sector collects households' savings (a leakage) (1)
  • It lends or invests these funds, mainly to businesses for investment (an injection), linking saving to investment (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P1e-01 · P1e.4 P1e.5 · 4 marks · level 2

Describe the stages of the business cycle and how unemployment and inflation typically change in each.

  • Expansion (upturn): real GDP growth speeds up, unemployment falls, inflationary pressure builds (1)
  • Peak: the economy is near full capacity; unemployment is low and inflation is likely to be rising (1)
  • Contraction (downturn): growth slows or GDP falls; unemployment rises and inflation eases (1)
  • Trough: the lowest point; unemployment is high and inflation low, before recovery begins (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P1e-02 · P1e.3 P1e.1 · 3 marks · level 2

Outline how Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander businesses contribute to GDP.

  • They produce goods and services counted in GDP, in industries such as construction, professional services, tourism, the arts and native foods (1)
  • They employ people, including many Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander workers, and pay wages, so they add to incomes and spending (1)
  • They buy from and sell to other businesses and government, e.g. through the Commonwealth's Indigenous Procurement Policy, supporting activity in cities and regional and remote areas (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P1e-03 · P1e.6 P1e.7 · 4 marks · level 3

Explain ONE cause of a contraction in the business cycle and ONE effect of the contraction on households.

  • Identifies a cause, e.g. higher interest rates, a fall in business or consumer confidence, a world recession that cuts exports (1)
  • Explains how it reduces spending (aggregate demand) and production (1)
  • Identifies an effect on households, e.g. job losses, fewer hours, slower wage growth (1)
  • Explains it, e.g. lower incomes reduce living standards and confidence, so households cut spending further (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P2a-01 · P2a.1 · 3 marks · level 2

During an outbreak of illness, demand for hand sanitiser surges and its price rises. Explain the signalling, incentive and rationing functions of the price mechanism using this example.

  • Signalling: the higher price tells producers and consumers that hand sanitiser has become scarcer (1)
  • Incentive: higher prices and profits encourage firms to produce more and new firms to enter (1)
  • Rationing: the higher price limits purchases to those most willing and able to pay, so the available stock is shared out (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P2a-02 · P2a.2 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain why the rule of law and the enforcement of property rights and contracts are important for markets to work.

  • Secure property rights let owners keep the rewards of their effort and investment, giving them an incentive to produce and invest (1)
  • Enforceable contracts let buyers and sellers trust that agreements will be honoured, so they are willing to trade (1)
  • Without them, people face theft, fraud and uncertainty, so less trade and investment take place (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P2b-01 · P2b.4 · 4 marks · level 2

Explain how a rise in incomes and a rise in the price of a complement would each affect the demand for a good. Use examples.

  • A rise in incomes increases demand for normal goods: more is demanded at each price and the curve shifts right (1)
  • Example, e.g. restaurant meals; for inferior goods demand would decrease (1)
  • A rise in the price of a complement decreases demand for the good, because the two are used together (1)
  • Example, e.g. dearer printers reduce the demand for printer ink; the demand curve shifts left (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P2c-01 · P2c.4 · 4 marks · level 2

Explain how TWO non-price factors could increase the supply of solar panels.

  • Identifies a first factor, e.g. improved technology in making panels (1)
  • Explains it: lower costs mean firms will supply more at each price, so supply shifts right (1)
  • Identifies a second factor, e.g. more suppliers entering the market, or cheaper factors of production such as silicon (1)
  • Explains it (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P2c-02 · P2c.3 P2c.4 · 2 marks · level 1

Distinguish between an expansion of supply and an increase in supply.

  • An expansion of supply is a movement along the supply curve caused by a rise in the good's own price (1)
  • An increase in supply is a shift of the whole supply curve to the right, caused by a non-price factor such as better technology (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P2d-01 · P2d.3 · 3 marks · level 3

Explain how market equilibrium contributes to an efficient allocation of resources.

  • At equilibrium the quantity demanded equals the quantity supplied: no shortage or surplus (1)
  • Every unit that buyers value at more than its cost of production is produced, and none that cost more than they are worth to buyers (1)
  • So resources go to their most valued uses (allocative efficiency), and competition pushes firms to produce at least cost (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P2e-01 · P2e.4 · 4 marks · level 2

Explain TWO factors that make the demand for cigarettes price inelastic.

  • Addictiveness: smokers find it hard to cut back when the price rises (1)
  • So the quantity demanded falls by a smaller percentage than the price rises (1)
  • Few close substitutes for smokers (1)
  • So there is little to switch to; demand stays relatively unresponsive (time horizon: it becomes more elastic over the long run as some people quit) (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P2e-02 · P2e.5 · 3 marks · level 3

Explain why a business needs to know the price elasticity of demand for its product when setting its price.

  • If demand is elastic, a price rise reduces total revenue and a price cut raises it (1)
  • If demand is inelastic, a price rise raises total revenue (1)
  • So PED tells the business whether a price change will raise or lower revenue, and guides decisions such as product differentiation to make demand less elastic (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P2f-01 · P2f.3 P2f.1 · 4 marks · level 2

Explain why the supply of new housing is price inelastic in the short run but more elastic in the long run.

  • In the short run, building takes time, land is limited and approvals are slow (1)
  • So a rise in house prices causes only a small rise in the quantity of new homes supplied: inelastic (1)
  • In the long run, builders can acquire land, gain approvals, hire workers and expand capacity (1)
  • So the quantity supplied responds more to the higher price: more elastic (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P2f-02 · P2f.1 P2f.2 · 2 marks · level 1

Outline the difference between perfectly elastic and perfectly inelastic supply.

  • Perfectly elastic supply (PES = ∞): firms will supply any quantity at one price; the supply curve is horizontal (1)
  • Perfectly inelastic supply (PES = 0): the quantity supplied is fixed whatever the price; the supply curve is vertical (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P2g-01 · P2g.2 · 4 marks · level 2

Compare perfect competition and monopoly in terms of the number of firms, the product, barriers to entry and pricing power.

  • Number of firms: very many in perfect competition; one in a monopoly (1)
  • Product: identical (homogeneous) in perfect competition; unique, with no close substitutes, in a monopoly (1)
  • Barriers to entry: none in perfect competition; very high in a monopoly (1)
  • Pricing power: firms are price takers in perfect competition; a monopoly is a price maker (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P2g-02 · P2g.4 P2g.3 · 4 marks · level 3

Explain how network effects and first-mover advantage can give digital platforms market power, and ONE effect of this on consumers.

  • Network effects: a platform becomes more valuable to each user as more people use it (1)
  • First-mover advantage: the first platform to attract users becomes the default, making it hard for rivals to win users (1)
  • Together they create high barriers to entry and allow a few platforms to dominate (1)
  • Effect on consumers, e.g. less choice, higher fees or more advertising, use of personal data; or benefits such as free services (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P2h-01 · P2h.3 P2h.4 · 4 marks · level 2

Explain why a negative externality in production leads to market failure. Refer to marginal private cost and marginal social cost.

  • Producers base supply on their marginal private costs (MPC) (1)
  • A negative externality, e.g. pollution, imposes extra costs on third parties, so marginal social cost (MSC) is greater than MPC (1)
  • The market therefore produces more than the socially efficient quantity, where MSC equals marginal social benefit (1)
  • Resources are misallocated: too much of the good is produced at too low a price, a market failure (deadweight loss) (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P2h-02 · P2h.5 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain why public goods are unlikely to be provided by private firms.

  • Public goods are non-excludable: people who do not pay cannot be stopped from using them (1)
  • So people can free ride, and few would pay a private firm for them (1)
  • Firms cannot make a profit, so the market under-provides (or does not provide) them; government provides them, e.g. street lighting, national defence (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P2h-03 · P2h.6 P2h.7 · 4 marks · level 2

Outline how asymmetric information and the abuse of market power can each cause market failure.

  • Asymmetric information: one party to a transaction knows more than the other (1)
  • E.g. a seller hides a product's defects or a buyer conceals risk from an insurer (adverse selection), so prices and quantities are not efficient (1)
  • Abuse of market power: firms with market power restrict competition, e.g. collusion or predatory pricing (1)
  • So prices are higher and output lower than in a competitive market (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P2h-04 · P2h.2 · 3 marks · level 2

Outline TWO challenges faced by Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander businesses in accessing capital and markets.

  • Access to capital, e.g. fewer assets lenders accept as security: land held communally or under native title generally cannot be mortgaged, and less inherited wealth (1)
  • Access to markets, e.g. remoteness raises transport costs and distance from customers; fewer established links into large firms' and government supply chains (1)
  • Outlines a response, e.g. Indigenous Business Australia loans or the Commonwealth's Indigenous Procurement Policy, which sets targets for buying from Indigenous businesses (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P2i-01 · P2i.2 P2h.3 · 4 marks · level 2

Explain how a tax on sugary drinks could correct the market failure caused by their overconsumption.

  • Sugary drinks are a demerit good: consumers ignore the external costs, such as public health costs, so they are over-consumed (1)
  • A per-unit tax raises producers' costs, so supply decreases and the price rises (1)
  • The quantity bought falls towards the socially efficient level (1)
  • The revenue could fund health programs; the effect depends on how price elastic demand is (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P2i-02 · P2i.3 · 4 marks · level 2

Explain the effects of a binding price ceiling on rents in a city.

  • A binding ceiling is set below the equilibrium rent (1)
  • At the lower rent, the quantity of housing demanded expands and the quantity supplied contracts (1)
  • A shortage results: some people cannot find a home to rent (1)
  • Other effects, e.g. landlords spend less on maintenance, rental homes are sold or withdrawn, waiting lists or black markets develop (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P2i-03 · P2i.4 P2i.5 · 4 marks · level 3

Explain TWO limitations of government intervention to correct market failure, using examples.

  • Identifies a first limitation, e.g. lack of information: the government may not know the size of an externality, so a tax is set too high or too low (1)
  • Explains it with an example (1)
  • Identifies a second limitation, e.g. unintended consequences, rent-seeking, administration costs, slow decisions (1)
  • Explains it with an example, e.g. a price ceiling on rents reduces the supply of rental housing (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P3a-01 · P3a.2 P3a.7 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain how marketing and behavioural sludges can challenge consumer sovereignty.

  • Consumer sovereignty means consumers' informed choices decide what is produced (1)
  • Marketing can shape wants and create demand that reflects firms' interests rather than consumers' own preferences (1)
  • Sludges add friction to choices that are good for consumers, e.g. hard-to-cancel subscriptions or complex fee structures, so consumers keep buying what they would otherwise stop buying (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P3a-02 · P3a.8 · 3 marks · level 2

Distinguish between normal, inferior and Veblen goods, using an example of each.

  • Normal good: demand rises as income rises, e.g. restaurant meals (1)
  • Inferior good: demand falls as income rises, as people switch to preferred alternatives, e.g. generic store-brand products (1)
  • Veblen good: demand rises as its price rises, because a high price signals status, e.g. luxury watches (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P3a-03 · P3a.6 · 4 marks · level 3

Explain the paradox of thrift.

  • Saving is deferred consumption; one household saving more can be sensible (1)
  • If all households save more at the same time, total consumption falls (1)
  • Firms sell less, cut production and jobs, so national income falls (1)
  • With lower incomes, total saving may not rise at all: what is wise for one household can be harmful for the economy as a whole (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P3a-04 · P3a.9 P3a.3 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain how consumption and income contribute to wellbeing, and why they are not the only influences on it.

  • Higher income lets households consume more goods and services, raising material living standards (1)
  • Consumption of essentials such as housing, food and health care supports wellbeing (1)
  • Wellbeing also depends on non-material factors such as health, leisure, relationships, safety and the environment, so more consumption does not always mean higher wellbeing (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P3b-01 · P3b.1 P3b.2 · 4 marks · level 2

Explain how TWO factors could increase the participation of parents in the workforce.

  • Identifies a first factor, e.g. more affordable and available childcare (1)
  • Explains it: lowers the cost of working, so more parents choose to work or work more hours (1)
  • Identifies a second factor, e.g. flexible working arrangements, longer paid parental leave, a workplace culture that supports carers (1)
  • Explains it (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P3c-01 · P3c.4 P3c.5 · 4 marks · level 2

Explain how capital deepening and worker skills raise productivity, and why higher productivity matters for living standards.

  • Capital deepening gives each worker more or better equipment and technology, so output per hour rises (1)
  • More skilled and better trained workers produce more and better output in the same time (1)
  • Higher productivity lowers unit costs and raises profitability (1)
  • It allows real wages and incomes to rise without higher inflation, raising living standards (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P3c-02 · P3c.6 P3c.5 · 3 marks · level 2

Explain why a firm's long-run average costs may rise if it grows very large.

  • Beyond the technical optimum a firm experiences diseconomies of scale (1)
  • Causes, e.g. communication and coordination problems, layers of management, slower decisions, lower worker motivation (1)
  • These raise the cost of each unit produced, so the long-run average cost curve slopes upwards (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P3d-01 · P3d.3 · 4 marks · level 2

Explain how artificial intelligence (AI) and automation may affect businesses' demand for labour.

  • AI and automation can do some tasks more cheaply than workers, so the relative cost of capital falls (1)
  • Firms may substitute capital for labour in routine tasks, reducing the demand for some workers, e.g. data entry, checkouts (1)
  • They can also raise productivity and output, increasing demand for workers who use or complement the technology (1)
  • The overall effect varies across occupations and skills; workers may need retraining (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P3d-02 · P3d.1 P3d.2 · 4 marks · level 3

Outline ONE argument for and ONE argument against a large rise in the minimum wage.

  • For: raises the incomes of low-paid workers and reduces in-work poverty (1)
  • Explains it, e.g. a fairer distribution of income from work; higher spending (1)
  • Against: raises labour costs for employers, who may hire fewer workers or cut hours, especially young and low-skilled workers (1)
  • Explains it, e.g. small businesses may raise prices or automate (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P4a-01 · P4a.1 P4a.2 · 4 marks · level 2

Explain how financial intermediaries channel savings into investment.

  • Financial intermediaries, such as banks, insurers and fund managers, stand between savers and borrowers (1)
  • They collect deposits, premiums and contributions from households and firms (1)
  • They lend or invest these funds, e.g. business loans, mortgages, shares and bonds (1)
  • They reduce costs and risks for savers by spreading investments and assessing borrowers, so more saving becomes productive investment (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P4a-02 · P4a.3 · 3 marks · level 2

Outline the role of superannuation as a source of savings and investment in Australia.

  • Employers must pay compulsory contributions into workers' super funds (12 per cent of ordinary earnings from 1 July 2025) (1)
  • This builds a large pool of long-term savings, among the largest in the world (1)
  • Super funds invest the savings in Australian and overseas shares, bonds, property and infrastructure, financing investment and providing retirement incomes (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P4b-01 · P4b.2 P4b.1 · 4 marks · level 2

Distinguish between the primary and secondary share markets, and explain how each helps the economy.

  • Primary market: companies issue new shares, e.g. in an initial public offering (1)
  • It raises funds for businesses to invest and expand (1)
  • Secondary market: existing shares are traded between investors, e.g. on the ASX (1)
  • It gives investors liquidity (they can sell easily), which makes them more willing to buy new shares (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P4c-01 · P4c.3 · 4 marks · level 2

Explain TWO roles of interest rates in the economy.

  • Identifies a first role, e.g. the cost of credit for borrowers (1)
  • Explains it, e.g. higher rates discourage borrowing for houses, cars and business investment (1)
  • Identifies a second role, e.g. the return on savings, the price of money or an indicator of risk (1)
  • Explains it, e.g. higher rates reward saving, so households may save more and spend less (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P4c-02 · P4c.4 P4c.6 · 4 marks · level 2

Using the loanable funds model, explain the effect of a fall in business confidence on interest rates.

  • Lower confidence means firms plan less investment (1)
  • So the demand for loanable funds decreases: the demand curve shifts left (1)
  • At the old interest rate there is a surplus of funds, so the interest rate falls (1)
  • The quantity of funds borrowed and lent falls (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P5a-01 · P5a.1 · 4 marks · level 2

Explain TWO roles of government in the economy, using an example of each.

  • Identifies a first role, e.g. redistribution of income (1)
  • Explains it with an example, e.g. progressive income tax and welfare payments narrow the gap between rich and poor (1)
  • Identifies a second role, e.g. reallocation, stabilisation or correcting market failure (1)
  • Explains it with an example, e.g. providing public goods such as roads; spending more in a recession (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P5a-02 · P5a.5 P5a.6 · 3 marks · level 2

Distinguish between regulation and a nudge as ways of changing behaviour, using an example of each.

  • Regulation is a rule backed by law that requires or forbids an action (1)
  • Example, e.g. compulsory seatbelts, food safety standards (1)
  • A nudge changes how a choice is presented to encourage a better decision without forcing it, e.g. automatic enrolment in a savings plan with the option to opt out (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P5a-03 · P5a.7 · 4 marks · level 3

Outline ONE argument for and ONE argument against the privatisation of a government business.

  • For: private owners face competition and the profit motive, which may improve efficiency and service (1)
  • Explains it, e.g. sale proceeds can reduce government debt or fund infrastructure (1)
  • Against: a private monopoly may raise prices or cut unprofitable services, such as in remote areas (1)
  • Explains it, e.g. the government loses future dividends and direct control of an essential service (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P5a-04 · P5a.2 P5a.3 · 3 marks · level 2

Outline the role of Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander advisory and partnership bodies in shaping government policy for economic outcomes.

  • They bring the knowledge and priorities of Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander communities into the design of policy (1)
  • Example, e.g. the Coalition of Peaks shares decision-making with governments under the National Agreement on Closing the Gap (2020), including targets for employment and education (1)
  • Policies designed with communities are more likely to fit local needs and improve outcomes and quality of life (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P5b-01 · P5b.5 · 4 marks · level 2

Distinguish between direct and indirect taxes, and between the legal and economic incidence of a tax.

  • Direct taxes are levied on income, profits or wealth and paid by those who earn or own them, e.g. income tax (1)
  • Indirect taxes are levied on spending and collected by businesses, e.g. the GST, excise (1)
  • Legal incidence: who must pay the tax to the government (1)
  • Economic incidence: who actually bears the cost, e.g. consumers paying higher prices; it depends on price elasticities (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P5b-02 · P5b.6 P5b.4 · 4 marks · level 3

Assess the GST against the features of a good tax.

  • Simplicity: one rate (10 per cent) collected by businesses, but exemptions (e.g. fresh food, health, education) add complexity (1)
  • Efficiency: a broad consumption tax distorts decisions less than high income taxes and is hard to avoid (1)
  • Fairness: it is regressive, because lower-income households spend a larger share of their income (1)
  • Judgement, e.g. an efficient revenue source, but it needs the transfer system to offset its unfairness (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P5b-03 · P5b.1 P5b.7 · 3 marks · level 2

Outline the purposes of taxation and ONE reason for reforming Australia's tax system.

  • Taxation raises revenue to fund government spending such as health, education and welfare (1)
  • It is used to redistribute income and to change behaviour, e.g. tobacco excise (1)
  • Reason for reform, e.g. reliance on income tax, bracket creep, a narrow GST base, an ageing population reducing the share of people paying income tax (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P5c-01 · P5c.1 P5c.2 · 3 marks · level 1

Distinguish between recurrent and non-recurrent government spending, giving an example of each.

  • Recurrent spending is ongoing, day-to-day spending, e.g. salaries, welfare payments such as the age pension (1)
  • Non-recurrent (capital) spending creates long-lasting assets, e.g. building roads, hospitals and schools (1)
  • Social security and welfare is the largest area of Australian Government spending, followed by health (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P5c-02 · P5c.3 · 4 marks · level 3

Explain why the Australian Government may need to reform its spending.

  • Spending on some programs is growing faster than revenue, e.g. the NDIS, aged care, health and interest on debt (1)
  • An ageing population increases these costs while reducing the share of people of working age (1)
  • Persistent deficits add to government debt and interest costs, leaving less for other priorities (1)
  • Reform aims to target spending better and improve its value for money, e.g. means testing, efficiency reviews (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P6a-01 · P6a.1 · 4 marks · level 2

Explain how international trade based on voluntary exchange benefits both countries involved.

  • Trade is voluntary, so countries trade only if both expect to gain (1)
  • Countries specialise in goods they produce at a lower opportunity cost (comparative advantage) (1)
  • Specialisation and trade allow more total output from the same resources: a more efficient allocation of resources (1)
  • Consumers gain more choice and lower prices; producers gain larger markets and economies of scale (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P6a-02 · P6a.3 · 3 marks · level 2

Describe the trade between Yolŋu people of Arnhem Land and fishermen from Makassar.

  • From at least the 1700s until 1907, fleets from Makassar (in present-day Indonesia) sailed to the Arnhem Land coast each year, arriving around December (1)
  • They caught, boiled and dried trepang (sea cucumber) and sold it to Chinese traders: an international trade route linking northern Australia, Sulawesi and China (1)
  • Yolŋu people traded and worked with them, receiving goods such as cloth, tobacco, knives and rice, with lasting cultural exchange (National Museum of Australia) (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P6a-03 · P6a.2 · 3 marks · level 2

Describe the composition and direction of Australia's goods exports.

  • Composition: dominated by mineral and energy resources such as iron ore, coal, natural gas and gold (1)
  • Rural exports such as beef, wheat and wool are also significant; manufactured exports are a small share (1)
  • Direction: mostly to Asia, with China the largest market and Japan, South Korea and India also major markets (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P6b-01 · P6b.1 P6b.2 · 4 marks · level 2

Distinguish between foreign debt and foreign equity, using an example of each.

  • Foreign debt: borrowing from overseas that must be repaid with interest (1)
  • Example, e.g. an Australian bank borrowing from US lenders (1)
  • Foreign equity: overseas ownership of Australian assets, such as shares, businesses or property, which earns dividends and profits (1)
  • Example, e.g. a Japanese company buying an Australian mine or a stake in an Australian company (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P6c-01 · P6c.1 · 3 marks · level 1

Outline the role of exchange rates in international trade and investment.

  • Countries use different currencies, so traders and investors must exchange one currency for another (1)
  • The exchange rate is the price of one currency in terms of another, e.g. A$1 = US$0.65 (1)
  • It converts the prices of exports, imports and overseas assets into each currency, which affects how much is traded and invested (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P6d-01 · P6d.1 · 3 marks · level 1

Outline the purpose of the balance of payments.

  • It records all economic transactions between Australian residents and the rest of the world over a period (1)
  • The current account records trade in goods and services and income flows; the capital and financial account records investment and borrowing (1)
  • It shows how Australia pays its way in the world and helps governments and businesses monitor Australia's international position (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P1a-06 · P1a.1 P1a.2 · 2 marks · level 1

Outline what an economy is and what economics studies.

  • An economy is the system through which a society's people, businesses and government produce, exchange and consume goods and services (1)
  • Economics studies how individuals, businesses and governments make choices about using scarce resources to satisfy their wants (1)

Short answer y11-sa-P3c-03 · P3c.1 P3c.2 · 3 marks · level 1

Outline THREE roles of businesses in the economy.

  • Produce the goods and services that households, governments and other businesses buy (1)
  • Employ labour and other factors of production, paying wages, rent and interest that become household incomes (1)
  • Invest and innovate, adding to the economy's productive capacity, and pay taxes that fund government spending (1)

True or false statements (227)

  • True: Globalisation is the increasing integration of the world's economies through trade, finance, investment, technology and the movement of people. 1A.1 1A.3 1A.4 1A.5 1A.6 1A.7
  • False: Financial flows are the smallest part of global economic integration. → Financial flows are the largest and fastest-growing part of global integration, far larger than trade flows. 1A.1 1A.3 1A.4 1A.5 1A.6 1A.7
  • True: Transnational corporations (TNCs) produce in more than one country and account for a large share of world trade. 1A.1 1A.3 1A.4 1A.5 1A.6 1A.7
  • False: Improvements in transport and communication have raised the cost of trading across borders. → Improvements in transport and communication have lowered the cost of trading across borders. 1A.1 1A.3 1A.4 1A.5 1A.6 1A.7
  • True: The international division of labour means countries specialise in different stages of production according to their comparative advantage. 1A.1 1A.3 1A.4 1A.5 1A.6 1A.7
  • False: Gross World Product is measured by adding together each country's exports. → Gross World Product is the total value of goods and services produced by all the world's economies (the sum of countries' GDP). 1A.1 1A.3 1A.4 1A.5 1A.6 1A.7
  • True: Comparative advantage means a country can produce a good at a lower opportunity cost than another country. 1B.1 1B.2 1B.3 1B.4
  • False: The IMF's main role is to settle trade disputes between countries. → The WTO settles trade disputes; the IMF promotes financial stability and lends to countries facing balance of payments problems. 1B.1 1B.2 1B.3 1B.4
  • True: The World Bank provides loans and grants for development projects in developing economies. 1B.1 1B.2 1B.3 1B.4
  • False: A monetary union is a group of countries that only remove tariffs on trade with each other. → A monetary union shares a single currency and one monetary policy, such as the euro area. 1B.1 1B.2 1B.3 1B.4
  • True: The G20 is a forum of major advanced and emerging economies, and Australia is a member. 1B.1 1B.2 1B.3 1B.4
  • False: Trade diversion means trade moves to the lowest-cost producer in the world. → Trade diversion means imports switch from a lower-cost non-member to a higher-cost member of a trading bloc. 1B.1 1B.2 1B.3 1B.4
  • True: A tariff raises the domestic price of an imported good and gives the government revenue. 1C.1 1C.2
  • False: An import quota gives the government revenue equal to the quota multiplied by the price rise. → With a quota, the importers keep the extra revenue from the higher price; the government receives no revenue. 1C.1 1C.2
  • True: Dumping is selling exports below the price charged in the home market or below the cost of production. 1C.1 1C.2
  • False: A production subsidy raises the price consumers pay for the good. → With a production subsidy, consumers still pay the world price; domestic production rises and imports fall. 1C.1 1C.2
  • True: The infant industry argument says a new industry may need temporary protection until it is large enough to compete. 1C.1 1C.2
  • False: Protection raises world efficiency because each country produces more of its own goods. → Protection lowers world efficiency because production moves away from the lowest-cost producers. 1C.1 1C.2
  • True: Economic development includes improvements in health, education and living standards, not just higher output. 1D.1 1D.2 1D.3 1D.4 1D.5 1D.8 1D.9
  • False: Emerging economies and developing economies are the same group of countries. → Emerging economies (such as China and India) are industrialising quickly; developing economies have lower incomes and are mostly agricultural. 1D.1 1D.2 1D.3 1D.4 1D.5 1D.8 1D.9
  • True: The Human Development Index combines life expectancy, education and income per person. 1D.1 1D.2 1D.3 1D.4 1D.5 1D.8 1D.9
  • False: Purchasing power parity compares incomes using market exchange rates. → Purchasing power parity adjusts incomes for differences in the cost of living between countries. 1D.1 1D.2 1D.3 1D.4 1D.5 1D.8 1D.9
  • True: Global income and wealth are distributed very unequally between countries. 1D.1 1D.2 1D.3 1D.4 1D.5 1D.8 1D.9
  • True: A downturn in the international business cycle can spread to Australia through lower demand for its exports. 1D.1 1D.2 1D.3 1D.4 1D.5 1D.8 1D.9
  • True: China is Australia's largest trading partner. 2A.1 2A.2
  • False: Most of Australia's goods exports are manufactured goods. → Most of Australia's goods exports are resources (minerals and energy) and rural goods. 2A.1 2A.2
  • True: Australia's foreign liabilities are larger than its foreign assets, so it has net foreign liabilities. 2A.1 2A.2
  • False: Net foreign debt is foreign ownership of Australian shares and businesses. → Net foreign equity is foreign ownership of shares and businesses; net foreign debt is borrowing that must be repaid with interest. 2A.1 2A.2
  • True: The direction of Australia's trade has shifted towards Asia over the past 50 years. 2A.1 2A.2
  • True: The current account records trade in goods and services, primary income and secondary income. 2B.1 2B.2 2B.3 2B.4
  • False: Australia usually records a surplus on net primary income. → Australia usually records a large deficit on net primary income, because it pays more interest and dividends to foreigners than it receives. 2B.1 2B.2 2B.3 2B.4
  • True: A current account deficit is financed by a surplus on the capital and financial account. 2B.1 2B.2 2B.3 2B.4
  • False: Foreign aid paid by Australia is recorded in the capital and financial account. → Foreign aid is recorded as secondary income, in the current account. 2B.1 2B.2 2B.3 2B.4
  • True: An improvement in the terms of trade means a given quantity of exports buys more imports. 2B.1 2B.2 2B.3 2B.4
  • False: The balance on goods and services is the current account balance. → The balance on goods and services leaves out primary and secondary income; the current account balance includes them. 2B.1 2B.2 2B.3 2B.4
  • True: The Trade Weighted Index measures the value of the Australian dollar against a basket of trading partners' currencies, weighted by trade. 2C.1 2C.2 2C.3 2C.4 2C.5 2C.6
  • False: A depreciation of the Australian dollar makes imports cheaper for Australians. → A depreciation makes imports dearer for Australians and exports cheaper for overseas buyers. 2C.1 2C.2 2C.3 2C.4 2C.5 2C.6
  • True: The Australian dollar has floated since December 1983. 2C.1 2C.2 2C.3 2C.4 2C.5 2C.6
  • False: In a floating exchange rate system, the central bank sets the exchange rate each day. → In a floating system the exchange rate is set by supply and demand; the central bank intervenes only rarely. 2C.1 2C.2 2C.3 2C.4 2C.5 2C.6
  • True: Higher Australian interest rates relative to overseas rates tend to increase demand for Australian dollars. 2C.1 2C.2 2C.3 2C.4 2C.5 2C.6
  • False: The Australian dollar can only rise against the US dollar if the TWI also rises. → The dollar can rise against the US dollar while the TWI falls, if it falls against other major currencies. 2C.1 2C.2 2C.3 2C.4 2C.5 2C.6
  • True: The RBA can buy Australian dollars with its foreign currency reserves to support the dollar when the market is disorderly. 2C.1 2C.2 2C.3 2C.4 2C.5 2C.6
  • True: Australia has cut its tariffs to very low levels, and most imports enter tariff-free. 2D.1 2D.2 2D.3 2D.4
  • False: RCEP is a bilateral free trade agreement between Australia and China. → RCEP is a regional agreement of 15 Asia-Pacific economies, including China, Japan, Korea and ASEAN. 2D.1 2D.2 2D.3 2D.4
  • True: Protectionist policies in other countries can reduce Australia's export income even when Australia is not targeted directly. 2D.1 2D.2 2D.3 2D.4

  • False: Savings, taxation and exports are the three withdrawals from the circular flow. → The withdrawals are savings, taxation and imports; exports are an injection. 3A.1 3A.2 3A.3 3A.4 3A.5 3A.6 3A.7
